"I don't want you to feel guilty about going."

"I don't."

"Good."

She looked at him. He had been patient with her, sometimes more than she deserved, but he had also disagreed with her when she was unfair. She could imagine missing the disagreements almost as much as the warmth of his hands. The thought made her laugh softly.

"What?" he asked.

"Nothing. You're going to be unbearable in your letters."

"I'll try to maintain the standard."

"Write when you can. Not because you're afraid I'll be angry."

"I will."

"And if you're busy, tell me."

"Same for you."

She nodded. Those were small, useful promises. She trusted them more than anything grand.

He kissed her, and she let the moment last. She could feel the cold against her cheeks and the warmth of him where they touched. There was no perfect way to say goodbye. She wanted to be near him, to keep the next few minutes from disappearing too quickly, and she knew he wanted the same thing.

At last she drew back.

"Go," she said.

"Not yet."

"We have a driver to terrorize."

He laughed, then took her hand and walked with her toward the service gate.

The van was waiting beside the road, dented and practical. Rhys loaded his bag and checked his ticket. Thalia stood a few paces away while he spoke to the driver. She had already said what mattered. She could let the public part be short.

Rhys turned and came back to her.

"One more?" he asked.

"You're getting greedy."

"Apparently."

She kissed him in the open, a simple farewell with no effort to conceal it. When he stepped away, she touched the pocket where he had kept the photograph from the orchard.

"Don't lose it," she said.

"I won't."

"And don't stare at it like an idiot."

"That I can't promise."

She smiled, and he climbed into the van. The driver started the engine. Rhys lifted a hand through the window, and she lifted hers in return.

She watched until the vehicle disappeared around the bend.

The path home looked exactly as it had an hour earlier. The cold remained, the lake lay quiet beyond the trees, and the pines moved in the wind. Yet the camp seemed to have acquired a space where Rhys had been. She knew it would become ordinary with time. That was part of what made it hurt.

She took the long route back, passing the place where they'd taken the photographs and the amphitheater where they'd danced. She didn't stop at either place. At Cabin One she found the book that held her picture and opened it.

There they were, captured in the middle of laughter. She looked at the blurred faces for a while, then closed the book.

She had not promised to wait perfectly. He had not promised to return unchanged. They had promised to write, to be honest, and to leave room for the rest of each other's lives.

It would have to be enough to begin with.

---

On Monday, Thalia discovered that missing someone was not an efficient activity.

She woke before four and lay awake until sunrise. The room was the same as it had been the week before: the familiar blanket, the books stacked near the table, the little draught that came through the window if the wind turned north. Annabeth was asleep in the other bunk. The camp outside was quiet.

Thalia knew what she was supposed to do with a morning. She could get dressed, inspect the winter targets, attend breakfast and make herself useful until the feeling passed. That had always been her method of handling an unwelcome thought. The problem was that missing Rhys did not behave like a problem that could be solved by carrying something heavy.

At breakfast she looked for him before remembering he wasn't there.

She was annoyed with herself for looking. Then she was annoyed that there was no one to see the look and laugh at her for it.

After the morning work she went to the north path, telling herself she wanted to return the striped socks. She had washed them, folded them neatly and put them in her pocket. There was no practical reason to keep them, except that she liked knowing they belonged to him. She had worn them almost every time she went to his cabin, and now that he was gone, the association made her feel foolish.

The door to the Apollo cabin was unlocked. One of the older campers had been assigned to check the stove while the building was mostly empty. Thalia spoke to him briefly, then went inside.

The room looked smaller without Rhys moving around it. The guitar was gone. The workbench had only two unfinished projects on it, a bow grip waiting to be sanded and a small leather case with blue thread still caught beneath the clasp. The slate by the door retained the laundry instructions and the joke she'd written after the Sunday visit.

She stood in front of it for a moment.

Someone had drawn a little arrow beneath her handwriting, pointing toward the words about terrible novices. The addition was not hers or Rhys's. It made her smile. The joke had escaped into camp life without revealing anything important about the private evening that produced it.

She put the socks on the clean shelf beside the workbench. Then, after a moment, she took them back.

She wasn't ready to return them. There was nothing shameful about that. He had told her to keep them for a while, and she had accepted. She could choose when the while ended.

She left the cabin and went down to the lake. The water was grey beneath a sky that threatened snow, and the path was empty enough that she could walk without stopping to greet anyone. She found a dry place on the low wall and sat for a while, watching the reeds move at the edge of the shore.

She remembered Rhys playing the first few notes of the tune he'd written for her. She remembered the way he'd watched her face in the orchard, waiting to see whether she'd allow the photograph. She remembered the smell of bread on Friday evening and his hand at her waist when they kissed goodbye.

These were ordinary memories. She was surprised by how much space they occupied.

At last she went back to Cabin One and took out a sheet of paper.

She had never been a good letter writer. She preferred speaking, arguing, making people look at her while she told them what she thought. A letter gave the other person too much time to interpret every word. She could imagine Rhys reading one of her sentences and smiling at the wrong part, or worse, deciding she had meant something gentle when she'd intended to be sharp.

She wrote the first line.

*Calder,*

Then stopped.

It was too formal. She crossed it out and wrote *Rhys* beneath it. That looked too intimate for the first line of a letter. She stared at the page for a long moment, then decided she was being ridiculous and continued.

*You left yesterday. The range is already worse. Somebody has moved the second target three inches to the left, and I assume you are responsible because you are not here to defend yourself.*

She smiled at the sentence. It sounded like her.

*I found your slate. Somebody added an arrow under the warning. I did not erase it. I am keeping the socks for now, because you said I could, and because there is no point in pretending I don't like them. They are still appalling.*

She stopped again. The next thing she wanted to say was more difficult.

*I miss you. I don't mean that I have nothing else to do. I have plenty to do. I just keep thinking I should tell you things and then remembering that I can't walk up the north path and find you.*

She read it back. The sentence was inelegant, but she couldn't improve it without turning it into something less true.

She continued.

*I liked Saturday morning. I liked not having to get up the minute I woke. I liked that you made breakfast and didn't ask me to fix anything. I also liked the part where you asked me to stay, because I am beginning to understand that it is possible to want somebody without making their time belong to you.*

She put down the pencil. It was the most sentimental thing she'd written in years. She considered tearing the page in half. Instead she folded it and put it in an envelope before she could change her mind.

Later she would add a postscript about his terrible cooking, so the letter would not leave him with the impression that she had become an entirely different person.

At supper she asked an adult Hermes courier when the next outgoing mail would leave camp. He told her the morning. She handed over the envelope, addressed in the careful handwriting she'd used when the words mattered.

That night she lay awake thinking about the letter traveling away from her. She could not retrieve it. She could not explain the tone or soften the sentence about missing him. Rhys would read the words as she'd written them, and she would have to trust him to know what she meant.

It was a small, frightening form of intimacy.

She found the photograph in her book, looked at it once, and put it back. The shared laughter caught in the image had not been made less real by distance. Neither had the desire that remained when she closed her eyes and remembered the feeling of his hands against hers.

Tomorrow she would wake, work, and do everything she usually did. She would also look forward to his answer. There was no reason those things could not coexist.

---

The first letter arrived on Friday.

Thalia found it tucked under the door of Cabin One, a narrow envelope with her name written in Rhys's slanted block capitals. Someone had added a little note in the corner to explain that the delivery had come with the morning camp mail. She picked it up and stood looking at it for an unreasonable length of time.

She had spent four days imagining what he would write. She had not expected the sight of the envelope to make her nervous.

She took it outside to the porch, sat on the top step, and opened it carefully.

*Thalia,*

*I arrived in one piece, although the train did its best to convince me otherwise. There was a delay at the second stop. A man tried to carry a chair through the narrowest doorway on the carriage and ended up trapping himself between two seats. I am telling you this because you would have had several opinions about his technique, all of them correct.*

Thalia smiled.

*My mother says hello. She has been behaving as if I am both a guest and a repairman. I have moved furniture, checked the back door, and discovered that the kitchen cupboard still refuses to close properly. She says I am not allowed to complain about any of it because I once broke the same cupboard when I was younger. I pointed out that the event was years ago. She said history is not a defense.*

*I showed her the photograph. She said you look like someone who knows exactly what she wants. I didn't argue, although I think she is going to be disappointed when she discovers how often you change your mind about whether you want tea.*

*I miss you.*

The words stood alone on the page. Thalia looked at them for a long moment. There was no joke attached, no elaborate explanation. He had written the truth and let it remain visible.

She continued.

*I miss the way you take over a room without noticing. I miss having somebody tell me when my bowstring is wrong and then accuse me of knowing it all along. I miss the way you kiss me when you're tired of arguing, although I would like to point out that sometimes I am enjoying the argument and you are interrupting excellent work.*

*I don't know how long I'll be here yet. There's more to do than my mother admitted in her letter, but less than I feared when I arrived. I can give you a better answer next week. I will not pretend it's a short visit just to make you feel better. I also don't want you thinking I've forgotten you because I'm fixing a door.*

*Write when you have something to tell me. Write when you're annoyed. Write when you've had a good day. I would rather hear the truth than get a perfectly cheerful letter that doesn't sound like you.*

*Rhys.*

At the bottom he'd drawn a small target and placed an arrow just outside the center.

*I did this on purpose. You can stop judging my technique.*

Thalia laughed aloud.

She read the letter again. The second time she slowed at the part about his mother, picturing him in a small kitchen arguing with a stubborn cupboard. She knew he was busy. She was glad. The letter made it easier to imagine his life continuing outside camp without treating that continuation as evidence that he had become less interested in her.

She folded the page and put it back in the envelope.

For a while she sat on the porch with the letter in her hands. She thought of writing back immediately, before breakfast, to prove that she'd been waiting. Then she realized he had specifically asked her to write when she had something to tell him, and she was entitled to wait until she knew what that thing was.

She took the envelope inside and placed it beside the photograph on her desk.

By afternoon she'd found several things to tell him. A target frame had been repaired badly enough that she'd had to insist it be done again. The weather had turned warmer for two hours and then colder, as if the sky had changed its mind. Someone had left a terrible new message on the Apollo slate. And she had caught herself humming his unfinished melody while carrying a bucket, which was a deeply embarrassing fact she intended to deny if challenged.

She wrote the reply after supper.

*Your mother sounds sensible. I approve of her position on the cupboard. I am glad you got there safely, although I would have paid money to watch you help the man with the chair.*

*The camp is still standing. I have not reorganized your cabin. The socks are still mine for now. The photograph is where I can find it, and I have looked at it exactly once today, which is a very dignified amount.*

She stopped, then added:

*I miss you too. I want to see you when you return, and I don't want you rushing home before you've done what you came to do. Both things are true. You can cope with the contradiction.*

She looked at the sentence. It sounded exactly like something she'd say aloud, and she was pleased with it.

Then she wrote a short final paragraph about the target he'd drawn, explaining in merciless detail why its arrow placement showed poor wrist alignment. She knew he'd laugh. She could picture his face when he reached that part of the letter, and the image pleased her more than she expected.

She sealed the envelope and put it with the outgoing mail.

That night she read his letter once more before turning down the lamp. The words hadn't answered every question about the future. They had done something more modest and more useful: they had made him present for a few minutes without requiring either of them to pretend distance didn't exist.

She could live with that.

---

Sunday, November twenty-sixth, was when Thalia learned how badly a person could want an ordinary conversation.

There had been no fresh letter in the morning delivery. There was no reason there should have been: the courier came through on an irregular schedule, and Rhys had already written that week. Thalia knew all of that. She checked anyway, twice, and found herself annoyed with the wooden pigeonholes for remaining empty.

By lunchtime the sky had begun to darken. The first flakes fell beyond the pavilion, small and uncertain, disappearing on the grass as soon as they landed. Thalia ate with the other instructors, carried her plate back and went uphill with no particular destination. She had a free afternoon. A month ago she would have treated that as a failure of planning. Now she wanted to enjoy it and found that her thoughts kept walking north without her.

At the fork she stopped beneath the empty lantern bracket. The trail to Rhys's cabin disappeared into the pines. She knew the stones that rocked under a boot, the section where the wind always struck from the lake, and the step that creaked beneath the porch. Knowing the path was not remotely the same thing as having somewhere to go at its end.

She turned toward the amphitheater instead.

The stage was empty, and a low line of snow had begun collecting along the back row. She sat where Rhys had once played his unfinished song and pictured him bending over the guitar, frowning because a note had arrived half a beat early. She remembered the way he'd looked up when she corrected him. They had argued about the count. Then she'd asked him to dance.

She was smiling when a voice said, "That good?"

Annabeth stood three rows above her with a rolled drawing tucked beneath one arm.

"It's cold," Thalia said.

"That wasn't an answer."

"It was an observation."

Annabeth came down the steps and sat, leaving enough room between them for Thalia to decide whether she wanted company. Thalia recognized the gesture and thought, with some irritation, that the entire camp seemed to be learning the same lesson.

"You're missing him," Annabeth said.

"Apparently everyone has decided I'm transparent."

"You've checked the mail three times."

"Twice."

"Three. You went back after lunch."

Thalia looked at her. Annabeth looked pleasantly unmoved.

"All right," Thalia said. "I miss him."

Her sister nodded as though the admission required neither congratulations nor a lecture.

"Is he all right?" she asked.

"He's at home. Helping his mother. Fighting with a cupboard that has apparently held a grudge for years."

"Sounds personal."

"It is. The cupboard has a strong case."

Annabeth smiled. The silence afterward was comfortable enough that Thalia didn't need to fill it. Snow gathered on the edges of the steps and vanished wherever a boot had left a warm mark in the wood.

"He's coming back?" Annabeth asked.

"He wants to. We don't have a date."

"That sounds difficult."

"It's not the end of the world."

"I didn't say it was."

Thalia glanced sideways. "You sound like him."

"Then he sounds sensible."

"Occasionally."

Her sister shifted the drawing into her lap. She had brought a new plan for the east wall, a design with carefully marked drainage channels and a small note that someone needed to check the soil before the first hard freeze. Thalia looked at it out of habit. Then she handed it back.

"You're allowed to go work on that," she said.

"I know."

"And I'm allowed to sit here and be unpleasant."

"I know that too."

Annabeth stood. Before she went, she reached down and squeezed Thalia's shoulder, a brief touch that reminded her of the little girl who used to fall asleep against her on cold nights.

"Write him another letter," Annabeth said.

"I already did."

"Then tell him something new."

Thalia watched her go. It was such ordinary advice that it was almost insulting. It was also right.

She returned to Cabin One and found the first letter from Rhys beneath her book. She reread the part about the man with the chair and laughed again, less because the story was funny than because she could hear how Rhys would have told it aloud, carefully setting up the ridiculous detail before leaving space for her to interrupt.

She took out a fresh sheet of paper.

*Rhys,*

*Snow began this afternoon. Not proper snow yet. The kind that thinks it can make an impression without committing to anything. I thought of you because the north path looks different when nobody's walking it beside me.*

She paused. Too solemn. She could hear herself trying to make the weather carry feelings it had not volunteered to carry. She almost crossed the sentence out. Then she left it alone. He had asked her to tell the truth, not to be clever every minute.

*I went to the amphitheater. Annabeth caught me sitting there and demanded an explanation. I told her I missed you. She took the information much better than I did.*

*Do you remember the first night you played that tune for me? I keep hearing the part you got wrong, which seems unjust because I know you have fixed it. I can hear the corrected part if I concentrate. I think I prefer it when you are there to make the mistake yourself.*

She stopped and tapped the pencil against the edge of the desk. There was something else she wanted to tell him. It was harder, though not because it was embarrassing. She had been thinking about how easily he had told her he wanted her there, how naturally his hand had found hers when they were walking, and how often she'd answered an affectionate remark with an insult because the insult gave her somewhere safe to stand.

She turned the page.

*I have been remembering Friday night. Not only because I miss kissing you, although I do, and you may feel extremely pleased with yourself about that for approximately five seconds. I keep remembering the part after supper when we stopped trying to make the evening special. I liked that you were happy for me to sit close without asking what was wrong. I liked being able to reach for you and finding you already turning toward me.*

*I want that again.*

There. No evasive joke at the end. She felt oddly exposed to a piece of paper. She could write something sharp now, make the line less earnest. Instead she allowed the sentence to stand alone.

Outside, the snowfall thickened. Someone called up the hill for the lamps to be lit early. The camp was continuing its winter work, oblivious to the fact that Thalia had written four words she might once have spent an entire week avoiding.

She finished with a note about the target frames and a warning that his mother was entitled to make him repair every cupboard in the house. Then she folded the letter and sealed it.

At the door, she hesitated. The outgoing-mail box stood down by the pavilion. If she posted the letter now, she couldn't take it back to improve the ending or disguise the part where she'd admitted what she missed. She put it inside her coat and started downhill.

She met the courier near the bend, a Hermes woman about her own age carrying a satchel and complaining cheerfully about the weather. Thalia handed over the envelope. The courier checked the address, slipped it inside and asked whether anything else needed sending.

"No," Thalia said.

The woman moved on.

Thalia stood in the path a moment, then turned toward Cabin One. It had taken a considerable amount of courage to say something unremarkable, and she was annoyed by that. She was also pleased. Rhys had written about wanting her. Now she had written about wanting him. Neither of them had been obliged to make the statement; that was what made it worth keeping.

When she reached the porch, the snow was finally beginning to settle. She paused and looked north, toward the path she knew so well.

He wasn't there. She still wanted him. Neither fact had to spoil the evening.

She went inside, shook the snow from her coat and began looking for the guitar she'd once borrowed from the music shed. She could remember enough of his melody to attempt the first phrase. The second would take some work. She suspected he'd have an objection ready when he heard what she'd done to it.

The thought made her laugh. She sat down with the instrument, set her fingers carefully on the strings and began.
---

## December 1: Things a Letter Cannot Do

Rhys's second letter arrived with the first serious snow.

The camp had spent the morning clearing the steps and sweeping the roofs of buildings too small to justify climbing onto. The snow packed into a dense, quiet layer beneath Thalia's boots, and the air had that peculiar stillness that made voices sound much nearer than they were. She'd spent two hours teaching a beginner's lesson indoors and was trying not to be irritated by the necessity. It was perfectly possible to teach archery without being outside. She disliked the compromise anyway.

The courier found her at the hall.

"Grace. Mail."

Thalia looked up from the bow she was checking. The courier was holding an envelope between two fingers, its corner damp from snow. She handed it over and continued toward the next building without lingering to see whether Thalia would open it.

Thalia turned the envelope over.

The handwriting was unmistakable. He had written her name a little too large for the available space, and the last letter nearly ran into the stamp. There was a faint smudge along one edge, probably made by the heel of his hand. She could remember that hand moving across a page in the cabin, pausing when he ran out of room and beginning another line beneath it.

She put the envelope inside her coat and finished the lesson.

It took an astonishing amount of discipline.

At supper she carried the letter to a quiet end of the pavilion, then decided she didn't want to read it in public. She took her food to Cabin One instead. Annabeth was sitting at the table, working through a set of measurements with a ruler and a stub of pencil.

"Mail?" she asked.

"Yes."

"From Rhys?"

"Your career in espionage is going very well."

Annabeth smiled and returned to the measurements.

Thalia sat on her bed and opened the letter.

It wasn't especially romantic at first. Rhys had spent the previous three days arguing with a plumber, had repaired a cupboard that had promptly developed a different problem, and had discovered that his mother possessed the same impossible standard for the arrangement of kitchen knives that Thalia possessed for target distances. He described an evening spent playing his unfinished song while his mother sat beside the stove and corrected his timing despite being unable to read a line of music.

Thalia laughed.

Then the letter changed.

*I keep finding things I would have told you if you were here. I could probably fill an entire notebook with them, but I suspect you would accuse me of wasting paper.*

*Yesterday I stood in the doorway with the last piece of the repair list in my hand and realized I'd finished the jobs I could do myself. The remaining appointments belong to other people. I wanted to tell you because you would have immediately ordered me to stop inventing new responsibilities.*

*You would have been right.*

She smiled despite herself.

*I think I can come back around the middle of the month. I haven't booked anything yet. My mother wants me to stay another week while she settles a few matters, and I want to be certain I can leave without making it harder for her. I'll tell you when I know.*

Thalia read those lines twice. She didn't mind that there was no exact date. Not in principle. She was less pleased by how quickly her imagination had begun counting days.

*I wish you were here tonight. Not because I've had a terrible day. It wasn't terrible. I'd just like to hear you complain about my guitar, eat something with you, and have you kiss me before we go to sleep. That last part is proving particularly difficult to replace with a letter.*

She read the sentence again.

It was direct, the way he could be when he chose to stop hiding behind a joke. There was an unexpected intimacy in seeing the desire written down, without his face or his voice there to soften it. She could not distract him by kissing him. She had to sit with the words.

She liked them.

At the bottom he had added one more line.

*Your turn. Tell me something you haven't told anyone else this week.*

Thalia set the letter down. She thought about writing immediately, but the question held her longer than she expected. There were things she had not told anyone: how often she'd looked up from supper because a familiar laugh sounded almost like his; how she'd held the photograph beneath the lamp and imagined his hands warm against her face; how the first morning without him had made her feel foolishly bereft even while the rest of her life continued exactly as it should.

She couldn't fit all of that into an ordinary account of her week. She didn't need to.

Annabeth stood from the table and stretched.

"Is he all right?"

"Yes."

"Good."

"He's dealing with repairs."

"That sounds like him."

Thalia looked up. "You sound fond of him."

"I like people who don't treat you as if you're difficult just because you disagree with them."

Thalia opened her mouth and closed it again.

"You're saying I disagree too much."

"I'm saying I know you."

Annabeth gathered her papers. At the door she paused.

"Tell him the truth," she said.

"You have no idea what I'm writing."

"No. But you looked like you were deciding whether to."

The door closed behind her. Thalia sat with the letter in her lap and considered the infuriating fact that her sister was often right.

She found a clean sheet.

*Rhys,*

*Your mother sounds as though she has the right idea about the kitchen knives. You should probably listen to her.*

*The snow has finally arrived. I am trying not to interfere with the people whose job it is to clear the paths. The word "trying" matters in that sentence.*

She paused, then continued.

*You asked me to tell you something nobody else knows this week.*

*I kept the shirt you lent me on the first Sunday. I know you know that, because it was in my bag when I left and you watched me take it. What you don't know is that sometimes I put it on when I read before bed. It smells less like your cabin now. I am annoyed by that. I don't especially want a new shirt. I want the old one to smell like you again.*

She stared at the words.

It was not an elegant confession. It was not even a flattering one. She'd never expected to become a person who admitted such things. But the thought had been hers for several days, and writing it down felt better than constructing a joke to hide it.

She wrote another line.

*I also miss kissing you. I won't pretend I don't. The exact things I miss are private, and I intend to remind you of them when you come back.*

She considered crossing that out. Instead she smiled, a little embarrassed, and left the sentence alone. He would know what she meant. More importantly, he would know how pleased she was to mean it.

Outside the cabin, the snow had stopped. Thalia could hear someone knocking it from a roof with the flat side of a shovel. She moved to the small window and looked at the path to the north. It was white now, interrupted only by the dark posts that carried the lamps. The cabin where she had spent so many evenings stood hidden beyond the trees.

She took the letter with her when she went to bed. It had been a long day. Tomorrow would bring another lesson, another stack of bows that needed attention and perhaps a new problem Annabeth would insist they solve before supper. She wanted those things to matter as they always had. She also wanted Rhys to come back, to open the north cabin door and look pleased to find her there.

She set the letter on the table beside her photograph. Then she took the borrowed shirt from beneath her pillow and pulled it over her head before turning out the lamp.

She was not trying to live in a memory. She was simply allowing herself to miss someone she intended to see again.

---

## December 8: A Song That Stayed Behind

There was a new score folded inside the envelope.

Thalia discovered it only after she'd already read Rhys's letter twice. The pages had stuck together at the edge, and she had mistaken the second sheet for a blank backing until she moved to the window and saw faint pencil lines showing through the paper.

The letter itself was brief. His mother had found a contractor who could handle the final repairs, and the question of his return had changed from whether he could leave to when the necessary work would be finished. He hoped to know by the next post. He had not promised a date.

*I wish I could give you one,* he'd written. *You'd probably look at the timetable and point out six better ways to arrange it.*

She would have. That wasn't the point.

At the bottom of the note he'd written, *There's something else in here. I think you'll recognize it.*

She unfolded the sheet. Five bars, then eight, then a full page of small, carefully spaced music. He had finally completed the tune they'd been arguing over. The part she remembered, the one he'd played badly in the amphitheater and again in the cabin, had become the opening of something longer. There were rests where she expected more notes. The troublesome transition from Friday had been rewritten, but not erased; she could see a version of her suggestion preserved in the lower line.

It annoyed her that he could make a piece of paper feel like his presence.

She brought the score to the hall after supper. The rooms were quieter now, and the instrument cupboard had been left unlocked for anyone practicing for the winter concert. She found an old piano against the wall and sat on the bench.

Thalia had learned a little music in the years before camp. She knew where the notes belonged, and she could find a melody if she didn't rush it. She wasn't particularly good at sight-reading, but she had the first phrase in her memory. She placed her fingers on the keys and began.

The instrument was badly tuned. One of the middle notes sounded flat enough to make her grimace. She stopped, made a note in the margin about the offending key and began again.

The tune rose.

She recognized the pause she'd insisted on. Without the second voice, the space felt larger, almost precarious, but the melody returned exactly where it should. She played the rest slowly, watching how the chords moved beneath the principal line. There were places where Rhys had written choices into the arrangement, small changes he could make depending on who performed it and what instruments were available.

She understood why he'd sent it.

He hadn't written a declaration about missing her. He'd sent her a thing he'd made, one they had discussed together, and invited her to hear it while he was gone.

She played it again.

A door opened behind her.

"That's new," said one of the older Apollo instructors.

Thalia turned. The woman stood near the instrument cupboard with a violin case in one hand.

"Yes."

"Is it yours?"

"No."

"Rhys?"

Thalia looked at her. She was tempted to ask how everybody seemed to know. Then she remembered that they'd told the woman's colleagues they were together at dinner and had not made any attempt to conceal the fact since.

"Yes," she said.

"Would you mind if I listened?"

"I'd rather you played the second line."

The woman smiled. She took out her violin, tuned it by ear and came over to examine the score. Thalia showed her where the harmony entered, then started from the beginning.

The first run was a disaster. The piano dragged, the violin rushed, and they both lost the beat at the place Rhys had written three notes against two. Thalia stopped, swore and started again.

"You're counting like a soldier," the woman said.

"That's how the measure is written."

"That's how you're interpreting it. Let the line breathe."

Thalia glared at the music. It was exactly the advice she had given Rhys weeks ago when he'd been trying to force the transition through without a pause. Now somebody was giving it back to her. She hated how often this seemed to happen lately.

She took a breath. They tried again.

This time the melody found its place. The violin entered softly, carrying the harmony beneath the piano, and when the pause came both players let it remain a pause. The notes afterward sounded brighter for the space they'd been given. Thalia reached the end and held the last chord until it faded.

For a moment neither spoke.

Then the woman lowered her violin.

"That's lovely."

Thalia looked at the music. She felt unexpectedly proud of him.

"He worked hard on it," she said.

"Clearly."

The woman asked whether they might perform it at the winter concert. Thalia nearly said yes. The music was good; there was no reason to keep it hidden. But she looked at the score again and realized that offering it to an audience was not her decision. Rhys had sent it to her, not to the camp.

"I'll ask him," she said.

"Of course."

Thalia folded the pages carefully and put them back in the envelope. She stayed long enough to help close the instrument cupboard, then carried the music home beneath her coat so the snow wouldn't damage it.

Annabeth was asleep by the time she reached Cabin One. Thalia turned the lamp low and sat at her desk, the score spread before her. She wanted to tell Rhys that it worked, that the violin had found the harmony and that she could finally hear the movement as he'd intended. She wanted to tease him about a chord that was unnecessarily difficult. She wanted to kiss him, too, and the thought arrived so naturally that she didn't try to pretend it was inconvenient.

She found a fresh page.

*Calder,*

*The song is good. I'm saying this only once. One of the piano keys here is out of tune, so any errors in my performance can be blamed on institutional negligence.*

*I played it with a violinist. It worked. The pause works too. You were right to keep it.*

She stopped. What she really wanted to tell him was that she had been happy while playing. It wasn't the feverish anticipation of finding him behind the door. It was a private happiness made from recognizing his choices in the music and imagining what he would say if he'd heard her stumble through the first attempt.

She added another line.

*I'm going to play it for you when you come back. You can criticize me afterward. I may even allow you to be correct about something.*

Then she took out the photograph and set it against the lamp. His laughing face looked back at her. The image had begun to crease at one corner from being moved so often, and she flattened it carefully with her thumb.

She wrote again.

*I am getting better at missing you without getting angry about it. I still don't like it. I doubt you expected me to.*

She put the letter aside. It wasn't finished. There was no need to send it before she had something else to say. He had told her to write when she wanted, not to perform affection on a schedule.

Outside, the first proper wind of the night struck the window. Thalia drew the curtain, put the photograph in its usual place and went to bed with the sound of his melody still in her head.

***

Two days later she returned to the hall before breakfast, when nobody would be there to hear her practice.

This time she didn't need the full score for the opening. Her fingers remembered the intervals. She could hold the pause without counting it as a failure, and the later phrases began to feel like part of the same piece rather than a set of problems she had to solve in order.

She missed him most sharply at the end.

There should have been a voice from the side of the room telling her that she'd hurried the last measure. There should have been an argument, a laugh and the sound of his boots crossing the floor. Instead the hall was silent.

She sat a moment with her fingers resting on the keys.

Then she played the last measure again, more slowly, and this time she got it right without anyone prompting her. She smiled, surprised by the feeling. Loving the person who had made the music had not prevented her from making something of it for herself.

She was pleased. She would tell him.

---

## December 12: Sunday, If the Trains Behave

The return date came on a postcard.

Thalia knew something had changed before she read it. Rhys had written across the back in broad, uneven lines, squeezing the last words along the bottom edge. He had apparently chosen the postcard because he couldn't find an envelope, or because he wanted the message to move faster, or simply because he knew she'd find the ridiculous picture on the front offensive. It showed a cheerful cartoon locomotive in a station full of flowers. A caption declared that travel was the best part of any holiday.

She stared at the caption with deep personal dislike.

On the back he had written:

*I have a ticket for Sunday, December 17. My mother has the repairs arranged, the paperwork is nearly finished, and she has informed me that I am becoming insufferable in her kitchen. I take this as an instruction to return to camp.*

*I should reach the road gate in the late afternoon. If the train is delayed, I will send word. I am looking forward to seeing you. I am also looking forward to discovering what you've done to the target distances in my absence.*

*Rhys.*

A small line at the bottom read:

*I still have the photograph.*

Thalia turned the postcard over and looked at the cartoon locomotive again.

"Idiot," she said, smiling.

She read it twice more, then carried it into Cabin One, where Annabeth was working on a model of the east wall. Her sister looked up as Thalia came in.

"Good news?"

"Yes."

"Rhys?"

"He's coming Sunday."

Annabeth's expression changed immediately. She looked delighted, and this time she didn't bother concealing it.

"Then you'll stop checking the mail every twenty minutes."

"I haven't been checking every twenty minutes."

"Twenty-five, perhaps."

Thalia picked up a pencil from the table and tucked it behind Annabeth's ear.

"You look like a building inspector."

"I am a building inspector."

"Very terrifying."

Annabeth laughed. The sound startled Thalia with how much she had missed it. She hadn't stopped spending time with her sister while Rhys was away, but there had been a part of her attention that kept wandering north, toward an empty cabin and a postponed future. Now the future had a date. She could stop treating every morning as a test of how well she endured waiting.

At supper she considered what to do for him.

She could clean his cabin. That would be useful and intrusive. He had left the room in order, and it was not hers to rearrange. She could prepare food, but he would probably arrive tired and hungry, and a meal was an entirely ordinary thing to offer. She could meet him at the gate. She would. The difficulty was that she wanted to do something more specific than provide a welcoming committee.

She thought about the music he'd sent.

The winter concert had been scheduled for the following week. She had not offered his composition to the organizers because she had not yet received an answer to her letter asking permission. She could play it privately for him, as she had promised. But if he was tired from traveling, the last thing he'd need was an immediate recital.

She could wait.

That was a new idea.

She went to the north cabin on Thursday to check that the stove had been cleaned. The other Apollo instructors had been keeping it ready between lessons, and the room smelled faintly of cold ashes and pine. Rhys's workbench was exactly as he'd left it. His remaining glove lay in the drawer beneath the small table. The photograph she'd allowed him to keep had gone with him.

Thalia stood by the cot and looked at the empty space beside it.

She remembered his hand at her waist and the way he'd drawn her closer when she arrived without warning. She remembered his quiet grin when he'd found her clean shirt in the drawer, the pleasure of waking with no immediate obligation to be elsewhere. She missed those things in a way letters couldn't entirely reach. There was nothing embarrassing about it. She wanted him. He was coming back. She could allow herself to anticipate the meeting without feeling that anticipation made her less capable of the rest of her life.

She placed one item on the table: a folded card with his name on it.

Inside she wrote, *Your range is still standing. Your guitar had better be in tune. I saved you a place beside the stove.*

She thought about drawing a heart. She did not.

Instead she added, *You may kiss me when you get here. I intend to make that difficult to resist.*

She grinned at the card, then put it beneath the lamp where he would find it later.

On Friday she washed the borrowed striped socks and folded them into her bag. She had decided to return them. Not because she no longer wanted them, but because she wanted to give them back herself and see his face when she made a joke about his terrible taste in clothing.

On Saturday she practiced the song once more. She still wasn't satisfied with the final chord, but she could play it without stopping. That was enough.

Sunday, she decided, could be a good day even if the train was late. She had spent the last several weeks discovering that a promise did not become weaker simply because it had to pass through ordinary delays. What mattered was that he had written her a date, and she trusted him to use it.

---

## Sunday, December 17: The Space Beside Her

The train was late.

It was not dramatically late. There had been no storm or broken bridge or army of monsters on the tracks. The driver of the camp van had simply sent word that the station was running behind schedule and that he would be at the road gate closer to six than four.

Thalia received the message at half past three.

She was standing in Cabin One, wearing the shirt she'd chosen after an unreasonable amount of consideration. It was the same dark one she'd worn to the range the week before. She had brushed her hair and then stopped because she could hear the voice of her younger self asking why anybody ought to spend time looking attractive for a man who had already seen her in three days' worth of exhausted camp clothes. The answer was that she wanted to. That ought to have settled it.

Now she had two extra hours.

Annabeth looked up from her book.

"Don't pace."

"I'm not pacing."

"You're about to."

"Your powers of prophecy are improving."

Thalia went to the door and looked outside. The snow had melted from the south-facing paths, leaving patches of wet gravel beneath the winter sky. A few campers were carrying firewood toward the pavilion. The tenth lantern hung dark on its hook, waiting for the evening light.

She had once stood beneath that lantern wondering whether to walk up to Rhys's cabin. Now she was waiting for him to come back down the same road. The symmetry annoyed her almost as much as the delay.

She occupied the next hour with something useful. She checked the range, made a list of equipment that would need repair after the freeze, and corrected a novice's grip without using the opportunity to lecture him about the value of patience. She returned to Cabin One at five, changed her wet socks and stood very still beside her bed until Annabeth looked at her again.

"Go to the gate," her sister said.

"It's early."

"Then you'll be early."

Thalia stared.

Annabeth turned the page.

"You're welcome."

***

The road gate was quiet when she arrived. The sky was darkening, and a narrow band of gold remained above the ridge. Two older campers stood near the supply shed counting crates. A wagon had been left beside the wall, its wheel propped on a stone. Nothing appeared prepared for an important reunion.

That was good.

Thalia didn't want an audience. She wanted to see him.

At half past six the van came around the bend.

She recognized the vehicle before she could see who was inside. It rattled over the uneven ground, slowed near the gate and came to a halt with a tired metallic shudder. The driver stepped out and went around to open the rear compartment.

Rhys emerged carrying his guitar case.

He saw her.

The change in his face was so immediate that she forgot the carefully chosen remark she'd been preparing. For several seconds they simply looked at each other across the space between the van and the gate.

Then he put the guitar down.

She met him halfway.

He caught her against him, and she kissed him with the unguarded pleasure of a person who had spent weeks imagining the moment and discovered that the reality did not need embellishment. His hands settled at her waist. She could feel the cold of his coat, the warmth beneath it, and the quick breath he drew when she caught his face between her hands.

It was not a polite greeting.

When they broke apart he was laughing softly.

"Hello, Grace."

"You're late."

"I know."

"Terrible first impression."

"Second, actually."

"Your first was worse."

He kissed her again before she could continue. She held him close, smiling against his mouth, and for a moment the weeks apart seemed to fall away. The smell of his coat was different, carrying traces of train carriage and winter road, but the way he held her was familiar. She knew his hands. She knew the expression he would make when he drew back. It was so good to find him again that she didn't pretend otherwise.

"I missed you," he said.

"I noticed."

"You wrote me a letter explaining it in considerable detail."

"I was being thorough."

He rested his forehead against hers, eyes closed for a moment. She could feel his smile return.

"Thalia."

"What?"

"I missed you too."

She kissed him once more, not because the answer required payment but because she wanted to. There was nothing shy about her eagerness now. She had missed his mouth and his laugh and the peculiar satisfaction of provoking him into saying something sharp. She had missed the quiet afterward, too, when neither had to defend the fact that they liked being close.

The driver cleared his throat at the van.

Rhys opened one eye. "I think he wants the road."

"Let him have it."

"He's got my bag."

"Then make yourself useful."

They collected the bag and the guitar. Thalia took the smaller bundle without discussion, and Rhys didn't insist on carrying everything to demonstrate his competence. They thanked the driver, then stood a moment watching the van pull away.

The cold was beginning to reach them through their coats.

"Food?" Rhys asked.

"At the cabin."

He looked at her. "You planned ahead?"

"I have occasional moments of brilliance."

"Only occasional?"

"Don't get demanding."

They started up the path.

At the fork he reached for her hand. She took it without looking around first. They passed the lower buildings, the range and the cedar brake. At the familiar turn he slowed.

"This is where you almost changed your mind," he said.

"Your memory is annoyingly good."

"I had a month to think about it."

She looked at him. "And?"

"I still like that you came."

She smiled. "Good."

There were a dozen things she wanted to ask him. Had his mother seemed relieved when the repairs were finished? Did the train feel longer on the way back? Had he played the song for her? Had he really looked at the photograph as often as he claimed? She didn't ask them on the path. They would have time for those answers later.

At the cabin door he found the card she'd left beneath the lamp.

He read it while she took off her coat.

"'You may kiss me when you get here,'" he read aloud.

Thalia looked over. "You did."

"'I intend to make that difficult to resist.'"

"I succeeded."

He laughed and put the card carefully into the drawer where he kept his letters. Then he came back toward her.

The room was cool but not cold. Someone had cleaned the stove and left a small supply of kindling nearby. Thalia had brought bread, cheese and a pot of soup that could be warmed quickly. She had also brought the striped socks, washed and folded, and put them on the table with considerable ceremony.

"Your property," she said.

He examined them. "I thought those were yours."

"They're hideous."

"You wore them for weeks."

"An act of charity."

He picked them up, then set them down and looked at her with the expression that had interrupted so many of her carefully prepared remarks.

"Come here."

She did.

This kiss was different from the one at the gate. There was no road, no driver, no cold wind requiring them to hurry. They had closed the door on the camp and were standing in a room that had held all their private conversations. The familiar workbench and the small lamp were where she'd left them; the stove was beginning to catch, and his travel bag still lay unopened near the wall. She could see the tiredness beneath his eyes and the way his face changed when she touched him.

Her fingers found the collar of his coat. She slipped it from his shoulders and let it fall onto the chair, then slid her hands up his shirt and drew him closer again. He was smiling when she kissed him, but the smile faded as the kiss lingered. He rested his hand against her cheek, and she followed it as though she couldn't quite believe the touch had returned.

She had imagined this moment in letters and in the half-light of Cabin One. The imagining hadn't prepared her for the immediate warmth of him, the familiar scrape of his cheek, the way he breathed her name when she refused to let the kiss end.

The stove clicked.

Neither moved.

"You should put your bag away," she murmured.

"I should."

"Your guitar's in the doorway."

"Terrible safety hazard."

"You've been away too long. The standards have collapsed."

He laughed into the next kiss. She felt the sound against her cheek and knew, with sudden certainty, that she'd missed this as much as anything. The laughter that came when a moment was too good to remain solemn.

She drew him toward the cot, careful of the travel bag, and sat. He followed, setting one hand on the blanket to steady himself. For a moment they looked at each other, both a little breathless and entirely pleased. No negotiation was necessary. Their eagerness had been plain since the gate.

"Your train was late," she said.

"A vicious betrayal."

"And I spent two hours pretending not to be annoyed."

"How did that go?"

"Poorly."

He smiled. "I can see that."

She caught the front of his shirt and pulled him closer.

"Then stop talking about trains."

He obliged.

The lamp remained on for a while. She liked seeing his face as they kissed, the way his eyes followed her when she moved, the particular look of recognition each time she caught him off guard. At some point he reached to turn the wick down, and she caught his hand and pressed it against her cheek instead.

"Not yet," she murmured.

He stayed where he was.

There was no hurry. She knew how tired he was from the trip. He knew she had been counting days. They had the evening to themselves, and she wanted to feel the pleasure of that without forcing it to become a performance. Their kisses slowed, then began again, and the warmth between them had the easy certainty of something returned to its rightful place.

At last she turned down the lamp herself.

The remainder of the night belonged to them.

***

Later, the soup had cooled in its pot, the travel bag was still unopened, and Rhys was attempting to explain why his mother had decided the entire kitchen needed new shelves when only one cupboard had been broken.

Thalia was lying beside him beneath the quilt, listening with one eye closed.

"You're telling me she replaced the cupboard."

"She replaced the cupboard, then decided the shelves didn't match."

"That's your family."

"Apparently."

"Terrible judgment."

"On which side?"

"All of you."

He laughed. She was aware that she was smiling too, though she didn't make an effort to hide it. There was something wonderfully ordinary about being here with him, the excitement of his return giving way to conversation about cupboards and meals and the things that had happened while they were apart.

She touched the small scar on his knuckle.

"Did you think about me?" she asked.

He turned his head. "Frequently."

"Not only when you were bored?"

"Especially when I was busy. I kept seeing something I wanted to tell you, then remembering I'd have to write it down."

She considered. "I liked your letters."

"I liked yours."

"I wrote something embarrassing."

"You wrote several things."

She gave him a warning look.

"Good things," he added.

"Better."

He moved closer and kissed her forehead, a small affectionate gesture that required nothing in return. She followed him anyway, turning until she could kiss him properly.

"You know what I thought would happen?" she said when they drew apart.

"Tell me."

"I thought you'd come back and we'd spend the first hour trying to be the people we remembered from the letters. I thought it would feel awkward."

"Did it?"

"No."

He considered. "I was nervous on the train."

"You?"

"Yes. I kept wondering whether the picture I'd carried home was going to match the person waiting here."

She looked at him. "And?"

"The picture was very good."

"That's not an answer."

He smiled. "You're better."

She made a face at the compliment, but he had already seen her smile. He didn't push. She was grateful, and a little annoyed to find herself grateful.

After a while he rose to warm the soup. She watched him move around the cabin, putting the pot on the stove and unpacking a small parcel from his bag.

"What's that?"

"Something my mother sent."

Thalia sat up. "For whom?"

"For you."

He held it out. Inside was a folded piece of cloth, a simple dark wool scarf with neatly finished edges. It wasn't elaborate or expensive. Someone had taken time choosing a color that would suit her and making certain the seam wouldn't scratch her neck.

"She made this?"

"Yes. She said the photograph made it clear you needed warmer clothes."

Thalia laughed. "That's because you took the picture when it was freezing."

"She also said I was a fool to let you steal my scarf without offering a proper replacement."

"Your mother has excellent judgment."

"I'll tell her you said so."

"Don't. She'll think I'm nice."

He came to sit beside her while she turned the scarf over in her hands. There was no invitation to explain what a gift meant. It had been given freely, by someone who knew her only through a few stories and a photograph. She felt the odd weight of being included in a family conversation without having been present to direct it.

She put the scarf around her neck and looked at him.

"Well?"

He smiled. "It suits you."

She leaned over and kissed him.

"Tell her thank you," she said.

"I will."

"And tell her the kitchen shelves were a terrible idea."

"I won't."

"Rhys."

"Absolutely not."

She laughed, pulled him close again and kissed him until the argument about shelves had lost all significance.

They ate the soup at midnight.

He was tired. She could see it by the way he kept pausing between words, the old travel exhaustion finally claiming him. She didn't want to turn his homecoming into another demand. When he finished eating, she put the bowls aside and drew the blanket back.

"Come to bed," she said.

He did, with a smile that held none of the earlier uncertainty.

She settled against him. Outside the shutter, the winter wind moved through the pines, familiar and persistent. The cabin was warm. His guitar case lay by the workbench, and the photograph had returned in the pocket of his coat. Those things would need to be unpacked in the morning. Tonight she was content to know he was here.

Just before she slept she felt his hand find hers beneath the quilt.

"Thalia?"

"Hm?"

"I'm glad I came back."

She turned toward him, touched his face and kissed him once more.

"So am I," she said.

The answer needed nothing else.
---

## Monday, December 18: The Second Morning

The first thing Rhys did wrong was move the targets.

Thalia found him at the range just after eight, adjusting the second rack with two other instructors. The grass was still rimed with frost. He had a pencil tucked behind his ear, a rolled schedule beneath one arm and the distracted expression of a man who believed a small improvement in equipment placement would go unnoticed by anyone who hadn't been there to supervise it.

She watched for a minute.

"You moved them."

Rhys looked up. "Good morning."

"The targets."

"Three feet closer. The ground's frozen at the far end."

"I measured that field yesterday."

"Then you measured it correctly. It wasn't frozen yesterday."

He was right. She disliked how pleased he looked about that.

She walked over to the line, checked the angle and the distance, then stood beside him while the others finished securing the posts.

"Fine."

"That's generous."

"Don't confuse being right once with a habit."

He leaned closer, lowering his voice so the two instructors wouldn't hear.

"Did you sleep?"

Thalia turned toward him. There was a particular kind of audacity in asking that question on the range after they'd spent the night together. The ordinary tone made it even worse. She wanted to catch his collar and kiss him until he forgot where the targets stood. Instead she made herself look at the equipment.

"Very well," she said.

"Good."

"Your bed remains an offense to civilization."

"I'll inform the manufacturer."

"The manufacturer ought to be ashamed."

He laughed.

One of the instructors called for him to check the final anchor. He went, leaving Thalia standing by the shooting line with the familiar and rather delightful feeling of having been interrupted in the middle of something she wanted to continue.

When he returned, she made no effort to conceal her smile.

"You're going to be distracting," she told him.

"At work?"

"Apparently."

He glanced toward the beginners arriving with their bows.

"Then we'd better do our jobs."

She considered that, then nodded. They walked to opposite ends of the line. It pleased her more than it should have that he had resisted the temptation to turn the lesson into an excuse to remain beside her. She hadn't fallen for someone who needed to be attached to her every minute. She had fallen for someone who could share an intimate night with her and still turn up the next morning prepared to teach children how to hold a bow.

That shouldn't have been remarkable. It was.

The lesson went well until the last round.

One of the older students had been trying to improve his accuracy by drawing too far, and the bowstring struck the inside of his forearm hard enough to make him drop his stance. Thalia was beside him before the pain had finished registering. She took the bow, checked that no one else was in the line of fire and asked the student to show her his grip without releasing an arrow.

Rhys came over from the targets.

"All right?"

"He's over-drawing," Thalia said.

The student looked embarrassed. "I wanted to hit the center."

"You'll hit nothing with a damaged arm."

"Thalia," Rhys said quietly.

She looked at him. He had seen the boy's expression.

She took a breath and handed the bow back, unstrung.

"You're trying to improve too many things at once," she told the student. "We can fix the draw first. The target can wait."

The boy nodded, relief visible in the set of his shoulders.

Rhys stayed near enough to help but did not take over. When the lesson ended he walked beside Thalia toward the equipment shed.

"You caught the mistake quickly," he said.

"I've made it myself."

"Then you know how frustrating it is."

"Yes."

He looked at her. "He didn't need the lecture."

She stopped beside the door.

"I know."

"You corrected yourself."

"Eventually."

He smiled. "That's something."

She wanted to be offended by the observation, but the truth was that she had learned to notice the difference between helping and humiliating more quickly since they'd begun working together. He was not always right, and she wasn't always wrong. Their judgments became better when they stopped treating every disagreement as a referendum on who was stronger.

She stepped closer, caught the edge of his coat and kissed him quickly before anyone could wander past. He looked genuinely surprised. It was worth the risk.

"That wasn't for the lecture," she said.

"What was it for?"

"Your terrible company."

"An excellent reason."

She left him laughing beside the shed.

***

After supper he came to Cabin One to collect her.

Annabeth was at the table with her drafting instruments. She looked up when he knocked, recognized the visitor and gave Thalia an expression of supreme amusement.

"He's here."

"I can see that."

"Try to come back before the next snowfall."

"Don't start."

Rhys stood politely on the porch, clearly aware that entering the room while the Grace sisters were having one of their private exchanges would be a mistake.

Thalia fetched her coat.

At the door Annabeth looked at him.

"Did you bring the guitar back?"

"Yes."

"Good. Thalia's been assaulting a piano for weeks."

Thalia turned. "Excuse me?"

"That's the technical description."

Rhys smiled, but did not laugh until they were out of earshot.

"She likes you," Thalia told him.

"I hope so."

"Don't encourage her."

"I've never met anyone less in need of encouragement."

That was fair. Thalia was still deciding how annoyed to be when he took her hand beneath the porch lantern. The gesture was simple, and she found herself looking at their joined fingers with a pleasure she didn't want to explain.

They walked north through the crisp air.

The cabin felt familiar the moment he opened the door. His guitar had been unpacked; the repaired glove lay beside a new spool of thread; her clean shirt remained folded in the drawer where she'd left it weeks before. She noticed that he had not moved it.

"Still here," he said, following her gaze.

"Clearly you didn't have the courage to return it."

"I was waiting for its owner."

She touched the drawer with two fingers. It was a small piece of herself he'd protected without making a ceremony of it. She was grateful, and not entirely comfortable with being grateful for something so ordinary.

He came closer. "What are you thinking?"

"That you've acquired an unreasonable number of my clothes."

"Your wardrobe is very demanding."

"You're welcome to complain."

"I wasn't going to."

She looked up at him. The expression on his face caught her completely off guard. It was the same look he'd given her at the gate, a mixture of pleasure and disbelief, as though some part of him still couldn't quite accept that she had come back with him.

She kissed him.

It was a quick decision and a slow kiss. He responded eagerly, his hand resting near her waist while she caught the collar of his shirt. They were alone and had the evening to themselves. The morning's work was finished. No one was waiting for a report or a favor. She could be here, wanting him, without making the desire into an argument.

When they drew apart, she stayed close.

"Do you know what I missed?" she asked.

"My cooking."

"Certainly not."

"My exceptional bedside manner."

"Debatable."

He smiled. She touched his face.

"I missed being able to do that."

He went still. Then he leaned into her hand, and something warm moved through her chest at the simple, unguarded response.

"I missed it too," he said.

She kissed him again, enjoying the way his hand settled at the back of her neck. He had been home for a day, and already the private part of their relationship was returning to its familiar rhythm. The anticipation of being apart had become the pleasure of being here again. She liked that the transition was not effortless, that they still had to adjust, but that neither had become a stranger.

The evening passed with the fire low and the winter outside. They ate a late supper, argued over whether his new scarf suited her better than the old one, and spent a long while curled close together on the cot, kissing until conversation became unnecessary. The lamp burned at the edge of the room and later went out.

***

In the morning she woke to find him already dressed, sitting on the bench with the guitar across his knees.

He was playing her melody.

Thalia remained under the quilt and listened. The tune had become smoother since he'd gone home. He had rewritten the last phrase, leaving a longer space before the final chord. It was still recognizable as the thing they'd worked on together, but it had acquired a confidence she'd never heard in it before.

He noticed her watching.

"Morning."

"You're early."

"Very inconsiderate."

She sat up, the blanket gathered around her shoulders. "Play it again."

He did.

This time she listened to the whole piece. When he finished, she looked at him for a moment.

"That last phrase is better."

He smiled. "Thank you."

"Don't expect another compliment before dinner."

"I've already had a very good morning."

She could have kissed him for that. Instead she made him play the last measure again, then took the guitar and tried the phrase herself. It was a little clumsy. He watched her carefully, but didn't correct her until she'd finished.

"You missed the transition."

"I know."

"You rushed."

"I know."

He grinned. "You sound familiar."

She gave the guitar back and pulled him toward her for a kiss, partly to silence him and partly because she liked that they could return to this ordinary argument after everything that had changed.

He laughed against her mouth.

The day began.
---

## December 21: His Song, Her Silence

The concert was meant to begin at seven. By half past six, the amphitheater was full of coats, damp boots and impatient people arguing about who had taken the last dry seat.

Thalia arrived with a folding stool under one arm and no intention of making herself useful. She had made that decision at breakfast. Rhys was playing, the Apollo instructors had arranged the music, and for once she was going to attend an event because she wanted to listen rather than because somebody needed an extra pair of hands.

She had been offered three tasks before she reached the door.

A child wanted help tying a scarf. She helped. A camper from Hermes's cabin asked her where to put the lantern boxes. She pointed. A young instructor asked whether she would inspect the stage platform before the performers arrived. Thalia stared at him until he remembered that this was not her responsibility.

By the time she sat down, she'd expended enough authority to command a small army.

The hall was warm. Curtains had been drawn over the glass, and rows of lamps cast a gold light onto the makeshift stage. Somebody had draped evergreen branches along the rail. The effect was almost tasteful, which meant Silena must have intervened.

Annabeth sat beside Thalia with a program in her hand.

"You're early," she said.

"I believe in preparation."

"You were pacing at home."

"I was walking."

"In a very small room."

Thalia took the program and examined the schedule. Several pieces were unfamiliar. Near the end was a short new composition credited to Rhys Calder.

She hadn't known he'd agreed to perform it.

Annabeth watched her face.

"You didn't know?"

"No."

"Are you angry?"

Thalia considered. It wasn't anger exactly. He had sent her the music privately, and she had asked whether he minded the winter musicians trying it. Now the piece was going to be played in front of the entire camp. That was allowed. It was his work. But she'd grown attached to the memory of hearing it for the first time in an empty amphitheater, with only the two of them there to argue over the troublesome phrase. She didn't know yet how she felt about sharing it.

She handed the program back.

"I'll tell you afterward."

Annabeth smiled and opened her notebook.

The concert began. A duet of flute and lyre went well until the lyre player broke a string, after which the audience waited with commendable patience while she found another instrument. A group of children sang an old winter song in three different keys. Thalia was prepared to criticize the arrangement when Percy, sitting two rows ahead, turned and gave her a warning look.

She folded her arms and kept her opinions to herself.

Rhys appeared near the end, carrying his guitar. He had changed into a dark shirt she recognized from his cabin, the one she'd helped repair on an earlier Sunday. He looked unexpectedly nervous. His shoulders were set too carefully, and he checked the instrument twice before sitting down. When he looked out at the audience, he found her immediately.

She raised one eyebrow.

His smile loosened.

He began to play.

The first notes were familiar enough that she felt a small jolt of recognition. She had spent weeks hearing the tune in pieces, then alone at the hall, then in the cabin when he returned. Now it was complete. A violin entered beneath the guitar, carrying the second line he'd written for the arrangement his mother wanted. The sound filled the room differently than it had in private, broader and more confident. The difficult transition came, and Rhys left the pause exactly where it belonged.

Thalia stopped thinking about whether she wanted to share the music.

It was good.

She listened with her hands folded in her lap, aware of Annabeth beside her and the audience all around, but not distracted by either. The melody carried something of the weeks when he had been away: absence held within a structure that did not collapse because one voice had temporarily fallen silent.

At the end, Rhys let the last chord fade before he lowered his hand. The audience applauded. A few people rose. Thalia stayed seated, smiling so broadly that Annabeth stared at her.

"Well?"

Thalia looked at the stage. Rhys was accepting a few congratulations from the other musicians, but his eyes had found hers again.

"He was good," she said.

"That's all?"

"Very good."

Annabeth grinned. "I know."

Thalia nudged her shoulder and went to find him.

***

Rhys was packing the guitar when she reached the side room.

"You're late," he said.

"I was considering whether to give you a review."

"Did I pass?"

"Marginally."

He smiled. She put her hands against his chest, taking advantage of the moment before any of the other musicians returned.

"You were excellent."

He stopped smiling. "Really?"

"Don't make me repeat it."

"I think I might."

She laughed and kissed him.

There was something satisfying about the kiss after hearing him play, not as a reward or a way to make his success hers, but because she'd seen him do something he loved and she wanted to tell him how much she admired it. He met her eagerly. For a moment the noise of the concert receded to a distant murmur behind the door.

When they parted he looked faintly dazed.

"I was nervous," he said.

"I saw."

"You did?"

"Of course."

"What did I do wrong?"

"Nothing. That's how I knew."

He laughed, and she kissed him again.

"How was your mother?" she asked when they had a moment to speak.

"Good. She wrote yesterday. The new shelves are holding."

"Then everything is wrong with the world."

"She liked the recording."

Thalia looked up. "You sent her the performance?"

"A rehearsal. I told you she wanted to use the arrangement."

"I know. I didn't realize she'd heard it already."

"She had opinions about the tempo."

"Naturally."

He smiled. "She also asked whether you helped."

Thalia considered. "What did you tell her?"

"That you argued with me until I stopped ruining the pause."

"Accurate."

"And that the piece wouldn't sound the same without you."

That one took her by surprise.

She looked at him. There was no trace of an attempt to charm her. He was simply telling her the answer he'd given, as if it were obvious. She felt a small, unfamiliar heat move into her face.

"That was excessive," she said.

"Probably."

He brushed a loose strand of hair away from her eyes.

"Do you mind?"

She thought about the question. She had not written the whole piece. She had offered a few suggestions, argued about a difficult phrase and played it badly several times before learning to hear what he meant. But she had been there while he worked, and the melody carried a trace of their conversations.

"No," she said. "I don't."

He smiled and pulled her close enough to kiss her again.

The door opened.

A violinist came in carrying a case and stopped with a look of mild surprise. Thalia stepped back at once, more from being startled than from shame. The woman smiled, said she needed the spare strings from the shelf and reached past them without commentary.

Thalia felt her face burn.

Rhys waited until the woman left.

"We could have locked the door," he said.

"We could have behaved."

"Do you regret it?"

Thalia considered. "No."

"Then I don't either."

She straightened his collar and glanced toward the hall.

"Everyone's waiting."

"Are they?"

"Probably not. But if we stay here any longer, Percy will start asking questions."

"Terrible prospect."

She laughed, then followed him back to the audience.

***

They left together after the last lantern had been extinguished.

The night was clear, with hard stars above the ridge and frost beginning to whiten the grass. Rhys carried the guitar in one hand and took Thalia's hand in the other. She had been looking forward to the walk all evening. It was the first time they'd attended an event together in a way everyone could see, and the public knowledge of their relationship had not diminished the private pleasure of being beside him.

At the fork he stopped.

"Come home with me?"

She looked at him. She knew what she wanted. There was no point pretending she needed to consider it.

"Yes."

He smiled, and they walked north.

Inside the cabin she put her coat on the hook while he set down the instrument. The room had become familiar enough that she didn't need to look for the spare blanket or the cups, but tonight she was interested in neither.

He turned toward her.

"You're staring."

"I've been watching you all evening."

"I noticed."

"Did you enjoy it?"

He smiled. "More than I expected."

She crossed the room, rested both hands at his waist and kissed him. There was no hurry in it at first. She wanted to enjoy the anticipation they'd carried home, the warmth of him after the cold walk, the way his hand moved naturally to her back as she leaned closer.

He met her enthusiasm without hesitation. When she drew away, he followed her just far enough to make her laugh.

"Impatient," she said.

"Very."

She kissed him again.

The concert had made her desire feel different. She had watched him from a distance, listened to the music he had made and spent the evening wanting to reach for him. Now she could. The pleasure of it was almost enough to make her forget the instrument case by the door, the winter beyond the shutters and the work awaiting both of them in the morning.

She didn't forget entirely. She liked knowing that the world would be there when the night was over. She was simply glad it could wait.

The room grew warm. He moved to turn down the lamp, and she caught his hand and drew him back toward her. He smiled. They were both laughing when the light finally went low.

The rest of the evening remained theirs.

***

Afterward she found the concert program folded on the table, where she'd put it beside the bread.

Rhys was making tea. She stood with the program in her hands, reading the line beneath his name. It contained no explanation of how the song had been written, no record of the arguments or the letters or the private hours in which they had learned to hear one another more clearly. It was only a title and a composer.

She thought that was enough.

"You're staring at the program," he said.

"I'm evaluating the typography."

"Verdict?"

"Offensive."

He laughed. She set it down and came to sit beside him.

"Can I tell you something?" she asked.

"Yes."

"I liked being in the audience."

He looked at her. "You did?"

"Don't sound surprised."

"I thought you'd insist on coming backstage to correct something."

"I considered it."

"Naturally."

She rested her hand against his. "But I liked watching you do something that wasn't about me."

He went quiet for a moment. Then he squeezed her fingers.

"I liked knowing you were there."

She looked up at him. That was all either of them needed to say. He had made something for the world and also for himself. She had heard it from the audience, then come home with him, and none of those facts threatened the others.

She leaned closer and kissed him.

He kissed her back, warm and unhurried, and when they drew apart she was smiling. The room smelled of tea, wood smoke and the faint sharpness of winter air caught in their coats.

"Your tempo was slightly fast," she said.

He groaned.

"Only in the middle phrase."

"Thalia."

"I thought you deserved an honest review."

He took the program from her hand and set it safely out of reach.

"Come here," he said.

She laughed and went willingly.

---

## December 24: The Door That Stayed Closed
