Thalia had never been particularly interested in gifts.

People got anxious about them. They bought the wrong thing, tried to guess what someone wanted and then spent the rest of the evening wondering whether the recipient had been polite enough. She preferred things that could be used, repaired or argued about.

Rhys had apparently ignored that preference.

He opened the cabin door wearing a look of such deliberate innocence that she knew immediately something was wrong.

"What did you do?"

"Good evening to you too."

"That's not an answer."

He stepped aside. The room was lit by a string of little lanterns hanging along the upper shelf, their light warmer than the usual single lamp. The stove was going, and on the table lay a parcel wrapped in plain paper. Beside it stood a small plate of bread, cheese and apples.

Thalia stopped.

"You planned something."

"A small thing."

"I told you not to."

"You told me not to make every evening into an event. This one felt like it might deserve a little effort."

She looked at him. The string of lanterns had probably taken him an hour to hang, given the awkward position of the hooks. The table had been cleared. The cot had a new blanket folded at the foot, dark grey with a simple woven pattern. It was a modest transformation, but it had required thought.

"You're difficult," she said.

He smiled. "Learned from the best."

She put her coat on the hook, then crossed the room and kissed him. She had spent the day wanting to see him, and the moment she was close she decided that complaining about the lanterns could wait. His hands came naturally to her waist, and he met her eagerness without the cautious hesitation of those first weeks. They knew one another better now. She liked that he could read the invitation in the way she caught his collar and that she could hear his pleasure in the small laugh he gave when she refused to let the kiss end.

He drew back just enough to look at her.

"You're early."

"I wanted to come."

"That explains it."

"And I'm still not promising to be pleasant."

"I hadn't expected a miracle."

She smiled and kissed him again.

The parcel remained unopened for another ten minutes.

Eventually he steered her toward the table, which she considered a poor choice but accepted when he reminded her that the bread was warm.

"Open it," he said.

She untied the string. Inside was a small leather case, lined with dark cloth, with enough space to hold two photographs and a folded letter. The stitching was neat and strong. She recognized his hand in it, the same careful workmanship he used on bow cases and worn gloves.

"You made this."

"I did."

She ran a thumb along the seam. There were two small pockets inside, each lined to keep a photograph from rubbing against the other. It was a practical object, beautiful without announcing itself as such. She liked that he had remembered the photographs, the way she carried hers inside a book until the corners began to bend.

"That's good work," she said.

He smiled. "I was hoping you'd think so."

She closed the case carefully.

"And now you're expecting me to make a speech."

"Not in the slightest."

She caught the front of his shirt, drew him closer and kissed him instead. It was a much better form of thanks. He responded with immediate pleasure, one hand settling at the back of her neck. She let the kiss linger until they were both smiling.

"What about your gift?" she asked.

"You brought yourself."

"That's an appalling answer."

"I rather like it."

She shook her head and took a folded piece of paper from her coat. It was the copy of his music she'd annotated for the concert, with her corrections and complaints written in the margins. She had repaired the torn edge, folded it neatly and put it inside a cover she'd made from dark blue paper.

He looked at it, surprised.

"You kept this?"

"Somebody had to preserve the evidence of my superior musical judgment."

He opened the pages. She had written small notes beside the phrases he'd changed, including a line under the difficult transition: *You finally listened.* Beneath it, in smaller letters, she'd added: *I'm glad I did too.*

Rhys read the line and stopped.

"Thalia."

"Don't make a fuss."

He looked up at her. "This is lovely."

She felt her face heat and was annoyed by it.

"It's an annotated score."

"I know."

"Very practical."

"Of course."

He set it beside the photograph case, came around the table and kissed her with such open affection that she forgot how embarrassed she had been. She moved toward him at once, catching his shoulders and laughing when the edge of the table pressed against her hip.

"Your furniture is still dangerous."

"An ongoing problem."

"You're supposed to have fixed it."

"I've been distracted."

"By what?"

He gave her a look.

She smiled and kissed him again.

***

Later, the lanterns were still burning and the little room had begun to feel entirely separate from the rest of camp.

Thalia was sitting against the pillows with one leg folded beneath the blanket. Rhys had left his shoes by the door and was trying to find the second one, which had apparently made its way beneath the table. She watched him search with great satisfaction.

"Have you considered that it escaped?"

"With your help, perhaps."

"Accusations without evidence."

"I know you."

"Apparently not well enough to keep track of footwear."

He found the shoe behind the stove and looked at her with theatrical suspicion. She smiled too innocently to be believed.

He came back to sit beside her.

The warmth between them had changed over the evening, from the initial rush of seeing one another to the quieter affection that came after they had taken the time to eat, talk and make each other laugh. She found she wanted to be close again, not from anxiety that the time might disappear, but because it was pleasant and she could ask for it without turning the request into a challenge.

She touched his jaw. He leaned toward her.

Their kiss began softly. She drew him nearer by the collar, and he answered with a familiar eagerness, smiling against her mouth when she caught the edge of his shirt between her fingers. The lanterns cast small pools of light across the blanket. Outside, the winter wind pressed against the shutters. Inside, the room was warm enough that she stopped thinking about the weather.

He kissed her cheek, then lingered near her neck. She closed her eyes for a moment, letting herself enjoy the closeness and the private rhythm they had found together. There was nothing theatrical in it, no pressure to turn the evening into a grand milestone. They liked one another. They wanted one another. The pleasure of those facts had become less surprising and more certain.

When she drew back, she was smiling.

"Shut the door properly," she said.

"It's shut."

"The latch."

He glanced toward it. She had noticed earlier that the upper catch wasn't set, a small habit of his when he expected someone might still come by to collect equipment. Tonight she wanted the room to remain theirs.

He stood, fastened the latch and returned.

"Better?"

She caught his hand and drew him beside her.

"Much."

The remaining lanterns burned low while they stayed close together. At some point the conversation ended, not because there was nothing more to say but because neither wanted to interrupt the quiet. The rest of the night belonged to them.

***

In the morning she woke to the smell of bread warming on the stove.

The photograph case lay on the table beside her annotated score. Rhys had put the two gifts next to one another without arranging them for display. She liked the sight: two objects made with care, neither expensive nor designed to impress strangers.

He came over with a plate.

"Breakfast."

"You're turning this into a habit."

"A terrible one."

She took the plate and watched him sit beside her. He looked tired in the pleasant way she suspected she did too. His hair was disheveled. There was a faint mark on his wrist from a cord he'd been repairing the day before. She noticed it and chose not to comment.

"Do you think we'll keep doing this?" she asked.

"Eating breakfast?"

"Making things for each other. Spending time like this."

He considered. "I hope so."

She looked at him. He wasn't offering a promise of perfect evenings or a future nobody could guarantee. He was saying he hoped they would keep choosing one another, with all the ordinary disagreements and practical complications that would come along.

"Good," she said.

He smiled. She leaned over and kissed him, a brief, warm kiss that ended with both of them laughing because the plate nearly slipped from her knee.

"Careful," he said.

"Your plate is defective."

"Of course."

She took another bite of bread, then reached for his hand beneath the blanket.

The door remained closed until they were ready to go outside.
---

## December 29: An Afternoon in the Village

The village was eight miles from camp, which was close enough to visit and far enough that Thalia had never bothered doing it without a reason.

Rhys wanted new guitar strings. The nearest place that sold them was a small music shop beside the bakery, and he had asked whether she wanted to come. It was the least romantic invitation she could imagine, which was precisely why she accepted.

They left after breakfast, riding in the supply van with two instructors going into town for winter provisions. Thalia sat by the window and watched the last of the camp buildings disappear behind the trees. The road curved along the edge of the lake before turning toward a low cluster of houses and shops. She had made the trip before, usually in a hurry, with a list of things someone needed and a deadline to get back.

Today there was no deadline beyond the van's return at five.

Rhys sat beside her with his guitar case across his knees. He was talking about a string he'd been trying to replace for weeks, its unusual gauge and the way he kept breaking the cheaper substitutes. Thalia listened with half her attention while the other half considered how strange it felt to be going somewhere with him that belonged to neither camp nor the privacy of his cabin.

At the edge of the village he caught her hand.

She turned toward him.

"What?"

"Nothing."

"Then why are you looking at me?"

"Because you look as if you're preparing for battle."

"I'm looking at a road."

"Very aggressive road."

She smiled and squeezed his fingers.

The music shop was narrow and warm, crowded with instruments, old posters and a collection of small wooden stools that looked as though they had been chosen specifically to trip customers. A bell rang when they entered. Behind the counter sat a woman who appeared to know Rhys already.

"Back again?" she said.

"Unfortunately."

"Still breaking strings?"

"Only the important ones."

Thalia looked at him. "You have a reputation."

"A terrible one."

The woman brought out a drawer of spare strings and began helping him choose a replacement. Thalia wandered toward the back of the shop, where a small upright piano stood beside a shelf of secondhand music. Its keys were yellowed, and the lid had been scratched by generations of careless players.

She sat down and tried the opening of Rhys's tune.

The piano was better tuned than the instrument in the hall. The first phrase sounded warmer, and the pause fell into its place without effort. She played the second line, then stumbled over a chord she'd been struggling with since December.

"Wrong finger," Rhys said behind her.

She turned. He stood in the aisle holding a packet of strings, looking unbearably pleased with himself.

"Your music is defective."

"Try the left hand first."

"I know."

"Then show me."

She played the passage again. He watched her hands, then reached around to point out the note she was missing. His sleeve brushed her shoulder. The small touch distracted her more than an entire lecture could have done.

"You're doing that on purpose," she said.

"Correcting your fingering?"

"Distracting me."

He grinned. "Maybe."

She caught his hand and kissed his knuckles, a brief gesture that startled him into silence. The shopkeeper looked up from the counter, smiled to herself and returned to her work.

Thalia pretended not to notice.

They bought the strings and left.

Outside, the air smelled of bread and cold stone. The main street was busy with people finishing errands before the new year. Nobody looked at them for longer than a moment. A man carried a box of vegetables into a restaurant. Two children ran past arguing about a toy they'd both wanted. A woman stood in front of a shop window considering a coat she apparently had no intention of buying.

Thalia realized she was watching the ordinary life of the village with an odd curiosity. These people had no reason to know who she was. They would not look at her and think of Zeus, or the archery range, or the responsibilities she'd assumed at camp. To them she was simply a young woman walking beside a man who held her hand.

The anonymity was pleasant.

Rhys led her into the bakery. They bought hot rolls and cups of tea, then sat at a small table near the window. The chairs were comfortable, the heater worked and nobody asked them to fix anything.

"An extraordinary place," Thalia said.

Rhys looked up. "The bakery?"

"They've invented a room where people are allowed to sit without being useful."

He laughed.

"You're enjoying yourself."

"Possibly."

"Then we should come back."

She looked at him. The invitation was simple, and she was surprised by how much she wanted to accept.

"We could," she said.

"Next month?"

"Maybe."

He raised an eyebrow.

"You're going to make that word your entire personality."

"Too late."

She laughed and broke her roll in half.

They spent the next hour talking about nothing especially important. Rhys told her about the first time he'd visited the shop, when he was thirteen and had saved for months to buy a secondhand instrument. Thalia told him about a winter when Annabeth had insisted on making every meal herself until the kitchen ran out of clean pans. They argued about which of them had been worse at thirteen. Neither was willing to concede.

At one point he reached across the table and took her hand.

"You're quiet," he said.

She looked out the window. "I'm thinking."

"About what?"

"That I like being here."

He smiled. "Good."

"Not only with you."

"Also good."

She looked back at him. "I like that nobody knows us. I like that I can be sitting here and not have to be the daughter of anybody important."

He considered that.

"You're still you," he said.

"Yes. But nobody has decided what that means before they meet me."

He nodded. He knew something about that himself, though he didn't make a speech about it. Being an Apollo child had brought expectations about music and healing and archery long before he'd been old enough to decide which of those things he wanted to be good at. Thalia could see that he understood.

She squeezed his hand.

"Thank you for bringing me."

"You're welcome."

"Don't get sentimental."

"I'm doing my best."

They stayed until the cups were empty and the afternoon light had begun to fade. Then they walked back toward the van. At the corner near the bakery, Rhys stopped beneath a small shop awning and drew her closer.

She met the kiss gladly. It was brief, warm and entirely unremarkable to the people passing on the street. A woman walked by carrying flowers; a boy hurried past with a bicycle. Nobody cared. That ordinary indifference pleased Thalia more than she'd expected.

When they separated, Rhys smiled at her.

"What?"

"Nothing."

"You're lying."

"Maybe."

She kissed him once more, simply because she could.

On the ride back to camp she leaned against his shoulder and watched the village disappear behind the trees. The guitar strings were safe in the bag between them. The day had contained no dramatic event, no danger and no test of their relationship. She had enjoyed it enormously.

Later, when he asked whether she wanted to come to his cabin, she said yes without turning it into a performance. They spent the evening close together, talking about the village and kissing whenever the conversation gave them a reason to stop. She liked that the private warmth of the cabin followed so naturally from the freedom of the afternoon outside it.

The world beyond camp was still full of difficulties. For one day, they had visited a piece of it together and found they were capable of simply being happy there.

---

## January 7: The Work That Doesn't Belong to Them

Rhys's new arrangement was going badly.

Thalia knew because he'd stopped making jokes about it.

He had spent three evenings at the workbench with his guitar, writing and erasing the same section of music. On the fourth evening she arrived to find him staring at a blank page as if it had personally insulted his entire family. The stove was cold. He had forgotten the cup of tea beside his elbow, and the window had fogged at the edges.

"You're going to freeze," she said.

He looked up. "I hadn't noticed."

"That's the problem."

He set the pencil down. She could see how tired he was. Normally he would have laughed, or challenged her to fix the arrangement herself. Tonight he simply looked at the page again.

"Bad day?" she asked.

"Bad week."

She took off her coat, then stopped. She had come expecting an evening with him. The thought had carried her through a long day of training, a disagreement with one of the senior instructors and a headache that had begun behind her eyes around noon. She wanted to sit beside him, eat something, kiss him and stop thinking about everyone else's expectations.

He looked as if he wanted the opposite: silence, an empty room and enough time to finish what he'd started.

She could pretend not to notice. She could sit beside him anyway and hope that her presence would improve his mood. She had done that before, and sometimes it worked. Tonight she suspected it wouldn't.

"What's the problem?" she asked.

He rubbed a hand over his face.

"The opening is wrong. Everything after it sounds as though it belongs in a different piece."

She looked at the sheet. The score was longer than anything he'd shown her before, a complex arrangement with several lines crossing beneath the melody. He'd written the opening in three different versions, each one crossed out more heavily than the last.

"Which part do you want to keep?"

"None of it."

"Then start again."

"I have."

"Then do it another way."

He looked at her sharply. "I'm trying."

The words were not loud. They didn't need to be. Thalia felt herself stiffen, offended by a tone she had used on other people too many times to count.

"Fine."

Rhys closed his eyes for a moment.

"Thalia."

"What?"

"I didn't mean it like that."

"You sounded as if you did."

He set the pencil down carefully. She could see that he was trying not to turn a frustrating evening into an argument with her. She was trying to do the same. Neither was particularly good at it when tired.

"I wanted to see you," she said.

"I know."

"And you're acting as if I'm in the way."

He looked at the music. "A little. Not because you're doing anything wrong. Because I need to work this out and I'm having trouble thinking."

The answer was honest. It hurt anyway.

She stood beside the table for several seconds, then picked up her coat.

"Then I'll go."

"You don't have to leave."

"I know. But I don't want to sit here making you feel guilty for needing time."

He turned toward her. "I don't want you thinking I've stopped wanting to see you."

"I don't."

She wasn't entirely sure she believed herself. That was the uncomfortable part. They had been together often since his return, and she had begun to treat the ease of their evenings as something she could count on. When it faltered, an old part of her interpreted the change as danger.

Rhys stood. He came close enough to touch her but didn't try to pull her into a reconciliation she hadn't chosen.

"I'm sorry I was sharp," he said.

She looked at him. "I'm sorry I tried to make your problem mine."

He nodded.

She could have kissed him then. She wanted to. But she knew the kiss might become an excuse to stay, and she didn't think staying was the right choice for either of them tonight.

"Finish the piece," she said.

"I will."

"And eat something."

"Yes, captain."

She smiled faintly. "Don't call me that."

"Then stop issuing orders."

He looked tired, but some of the strain had gone out of his face. She leaned up and kissed his cheek, a small farewell that neither of them tried to turn into something more.

Then she went home.

***

The next day they met at the range as usual.

Rhys was teaching the older group, and Thalia had the beginners. They worked at opposite ends of the field without interrupting one another. When the students left for lunch, he came over carrying two cups of tea.

"Peace offering," he said.

She took one. "Depends how much honey's in it."

"None."

"Then it's a very poor offering."

He smiled. "I deserve that."

She looked at him properly. The tiredness was still there, but it had softened. He was carrying a folded sheet of music beneath one arm.

"Did you finish?"

"No."

"Ah."

"I figured out why the opening wasn't working."

"That's progress."

He nodded. "It was the wrong instrument carrying the melody. I kept trying to make the guitar do something it couldn't."

She looked at him. "You needed to give the first line to the violin."

"Exactly."

She sipped the tea. The field was quiet now, the targets covered and the wind low. It was an ordinary winter afternoon, but she felt a relief she hadn't expected. He had used the evening alone for what he needed. She had spent it reading, talking with Annabeth and going to bed early. Neither had been diminished by the separation.

"I missed you," he said.

She looked at him. "You saw me yesterday."

"Not like that."

She smiled. "I missed you too."

He reached for her hand. She took it.

"I was going to ask if you'd come by tonight," he said.

She considered. "Are you finished being unbearable?"

"Not entirely."

"Then I might need another day."

He laughed. "Fair."

She drew him a little closer and kissed him, letting the kiss linger because she wanted it. He responded with the eagerness she remembered, but neither used the moment to pretend the previous evening had been easy.

When they separated, Thalia looked at the music under his arm.

"Show me the new opening sometime."

"I will."

"Not tonight."

He smiled. "Not tonight."

That evening she stayed in Cabin One. She worked on the east-wall plans with Annabeth, read three chapters of a book she'd been neglecting and went to sleep without looking toward the north path.

The next night she went to Rhys's cabin.

He met her at the door, put his guitar aside and kissed her with a smile that made her forget the careful speech she'd prepared about respecting each other's time. She kissed him back, catching the front of his shirt and stepping inside.

They spent the evening together, neither apologizing for the nights they had spent apart. The music remained unfinished on the workbench. It wasn't a threat. It was one more part of his life, and she liked knowing he had something he wanted to make even when she wasn't there to watch.

At the end of the night, she found a clean page on the table.

The new opening was there, written without erasures.

She traced the first few notes with a finger.

"Better," she said.

Rhys looked over her shoulder. "You haven't heard it."

"I can tell."

He laughed. She turned toward him and kissed him, pleased by the fact that this time they were close because both had finished what they needed to do.

---

## January 14: What People Thought They Knew

Thalia heard the rumor while carrying a crate of arrows from the storage shed.

She had not been eavesdropping. The two instructors were standing beside the fence, talking in voices loud enough to make discretion irrelevant. One said Rhys would be leaving camp after the winter training program. The other said he'd accepted a place with a music group outside the valley. Neither sounded especially certain, but both spoke as though the information had been settled.

Thalia stopped with the crate in her arms.

"Where did you hear that?"

The instructors turned.

"Rhys was talking about opportunities after winter," one said.

"He said he might travel," the other added.

"Did he say he was leaving?"

A pause.

"Not exactly."

"Did he say he'd joined a group?"

Another pause.

"Someone mentioned it."

Thalia set the crate down with more care than she felt.

"Then perhaps you should find out before telling people it's decided."

The two instructors looked embarrassed. One apologized, and the other began explaining that the conversation had been misunderstood. Thalia didn't stay to hear it. She carried the arrows to their rack, checked the list and finished the task she'd been given. By the time she was done, her irritation had settled into something colder and less pleasant.

Rhys had told her about his music. He'd talked about possible work after the winter, places he might visit, friends who had suggested performances. He had not told her he was leaving. That could mean the rumor was nonsense. It could also mean he hadn't yet decided how to tell her.

She disliked both possibilities.

She went to the north cabin after supper. Rhys was at the workbench, testing a new string on his guitar. He looked up when she entered, smiled and set the instrument aside.

"You're here."

"Are you leaving?"

He blinked.

"Am I what?"

"Leaving camp after winter."

The smile faded. He took a moment before answering.

"I've been considering some work outside the valley," he said. "I've told you that."

"You haven't told me you've accepted anything."

"I haven't."

"People seem to think you have."

He leaned back against the bench. "Who?"

"Two instructors at the range. They were talking as if it were settled."

Rhys closed his eyes for a moment.

"That's my fault," he said.

Thalia folded her arms.

"How?"

"I told a friend I'd been invited to audition for a group that travels in spring. I said I was considering it. Apparently the story improved while it was being repeated."

"You could have told me."

"I meant to. I hadn't decided whether the offer was serious enough to bother you with."

The words stung.

"Bother me with?"

He looked at her. "That's not what I meant."

"It sounds like you decided I couldn't handle knowing you had an opportunity."

"I was trying not to worry you about something that might not happen."

"You don't get to make that choice for me."

He was silent. Thalia could see him recognize the truth of it, and she was almost disappointed. She had come prepared for an argument. The facts were simpler and more difficult: he had meant to spare her uncertainty and had turned that uncertainty into a secret.

"You're right," he said.

She shook her head. "Don't agree just to end this."

"I'm not."

He set the guitar down completely and came to stand in front of her, leaving enough distance that she didn't feel compelled to turn the argument into a kiss.

"I should have told you I was thinking about it," he said. "Even if I didn't know whether I wanted to do it."

She looked at him.

"Do you?"

"I don't know."

"Why not?"

"Because I like it here. I like teaching. I like the work. And I don't want to spend the whole spring traveling just because somebody says there's an opportunity."

Thalia considered. It was a reasonable answer. It was also exactly the answer he might have given her a week earlier if she had asked.

"And you don't want to leave me," she said, more quietly.

He met her eyes.

"No," he said. "I don't."

She felt the sentence settle. He wasn't promising never to go. He was telling her that leaving would matter, that the relationship had become part of the calculation without becoming the only thing in it.

"I don't want you turning down work because you think I'll be angry," she said.

"I know."

"But I want you to tell me about it."

"I will."

She looked away. The anger hadn't vanished, but she had enough of the facts now to know what remained. She wanted to be trusted with uncertainty. So did he. They would probably be bad at it occasionally.

Rhys moved toward the table, picked up a sheet of paper and handed it to her. It was a letter from the music group, outlining a short tour and a possible audition. The terms were vague. There was no contract, no place reserved, only an invitation to try.

She read it.

"You haven't accepted."

"No."

"You haven't even chosen a date."

"No."

She handed it back.

"Then why did the rumor turn into a farewell party?"

"People are remarkably efficient when they're wrong."

She laughed despite herself.

He smiled, relieved.

"Are we all right?" he asked.

"We're annoyed," she said. "That's different."

"Fair."

She moved closer and rested her hands against the front of his shirt. The anger was still there, but it no longer had the sharp edge of being excluded. She wanted him, and she didn't see why wanting him had to wait until they had resolved every question about the future.

She kissed him. He responded with a warmth that made her realize how worried he had been about what she'd think. The kiss wasn't a resolution to the decision about spring. It was a reminder that they could still reach for each other while the decision remained open.

When they drew apart, Thalia touched his cheek.

"Show me the audition piece."

He blinked. "Now?"

"Yes."

He glanced toward the guitar.

"You haven't decided if I should do it."

"That's your decision."

"Then why do you want to hear it?"

She looked at him. "Because you care about it."

He smiled, slowly and without any attempt to hide it.

He picked up the instrument and played. The piece was unfamiliar to her, quicker than most of his compositions, full of sharp turns and pauses that demanded confidence. She listened without trying to make an immediate judgment. When he finished, she asked him to play the opening again.

They spent the next hour talking about music.

Later, when the lamp was low and they'd moved from the workbench to the cot, Thalia lay beside him thinking about how differently the evening had gone from what she'd imagined. She had arrived expecting to defend herself against abandonment. Instead they had talked about a possible opportunity and found a way to take it seriously without making it a threat.

She rested her head against his shoulder.

"If you audition," she said, "I want to hear how it goes."

He touched her hand. "You will."

"And if you get the place, don't pretend it's an easy decision."

"I won't."

She turned toward him and kissed him slowly, letting the warmth of the moment remain what it was. There would be work to do tomorrow, letters to answer and perhaps a difficult choice before spring. Tonight they were here, together, and neither was trying to make the other's world smaller.

---

## January 21: Snowed In

The snow arrived while they were arguing about supper.

Rhys wanted to cook. Thalia wanted to eat something that didn't require forty minutes of preparation and three rounds of complaints about the stove. They had reached an impasse over whether the last of the potatoes deserved to be made into an elaborate dish when the first flakes struck the window.

Thalia turned.

"That wasn't in the forecast."

Rhys looked over her shoulder. "It is now."

By the time the potatoes were on the table, the path outside had disappeared beneath a low white layer. The wind had shifted, sending snow sideways through the pines. The lantern at the bottom of the rise was barely visible. They could still have walked back to Cabin One, but it would have been uncomfortable and unnecessary.

Thalia looked at Rhys.

"Well."

"Well," he said.

She was already smiling. He looked at the path, then at the spare blanket folded at the foot of the cot.

"You weren't planning to go home tonight anyway," he said.

"I was keeping my options open."

"A very important distinction."

She took the plate he offered and sat near the stove. The potatoes were good. She told him they were merely acceptable, which he correctly interpreted as a compliment of unusual generosity.

Outside the snow thickened. Inside the cabin the room grew warm, its smaller dimensions making the weather feel distant rather than threatening. Rhys had prepared for winter properly: wood stacked near the stove, the window seals checked, enough food for several days if the paths became impassable. Thalia had contributed to some of those preparations herself, although she had not expected to appreciate the work quite this personally.

They ate slowly, listening to the wind.

At one point Rhys looked toward the door.

"What?" she asked.

"I was thinking about whether the younger campers will have trouble getting to breakfast."

"Clarisse will have them moving in groups. Percy will complain about the cold and help anyway. Annabeth will already have measured the safest path."

He looked at her. "You sound certain."

"I know them."

She was surprised by the ease of the answer. A month ago she would have been halfway down the hill with a shovel before anyone could ask for help. Now she understood that there were people at camp who could manage a snowfall without her, and that trusting them was part of respecting their competence.

Rhys reached across the table and took her hand.

"Then we're staying here."

"Looks like it."

She squeezed his fingers. The thought pleased her more than she wanted to admit. An unplanned night had arrived, one neither had engineered as a test of affection. They were simply together, and the weather had made leaving a ridiculous proposition.

After supper she helped him stack the dishes. The cabin was small enough that every movement brought them near each other. She caught the edge of his shirt as he passed, pulled him back and kissed him. He responded with immediate warmth, one hand settling at her waist while the other found the side of her face.

"You were doing something," he murmured.

"Washing a bowl."

"Very important work."

"Somebody has to finish it."

"It can wait."

She smiled. "At last, a useful opinion."

The kiss began again. She enjoyed the way he leaned into her, his familiar eagerness still capable of making her forget whatever she'd intended to say. The fire had warmed the room enough that she no longer noticed the cold in her hands. Outside, snow was building against the shutter. Inside, the ordinary closeness between them took on a quieter intensity, sharpened by the pleasure of knowing they had nowhere to hurry.

She drew back and caught his collar.

"You've got flour on you."

"From supper."

"You were making potatoes."

"There's bread in the oven."

She stared at him. "You made bread too?"

"I had time."

"An alarming development."

He laughed and kissed her again. She liked the sound against her cheek, the moment when his composure gave way to amusement and then to something more intent as she moved closer. They had spent weeks learning how to be affectionate without making every movement a statement about their future. Tonight they didn't need to discuss the future at all.

They finished the dishes eventually, though it took longer than the work deserved. By then the snow was thick enough that the lanterns below the ridge had vanished entirely.

Rhys closed the shutter and checked the stove. Thalia folded the blankets, then turned and found him looking at her.

"What?"

"Nothing."

"You're doing it again."

"What?"

"Looking as if you're about to say something sentimental."

He smiled. "I like being snowed in with you."

She pretended to consider.

"The feeling is tolerable."

"High praise."

She crossed the room and kissed him, the kiss turning into another before she was ready to let him go. His hand found the back of her neck. She smiled, then drew him nearer.

They had spent so much of the winter looking for moments when they could be alone. Tonight there was no need to search. The room belonged to them for as long as the snow held the path, and they let the evening unfold without watching the clock.

Later the lamp went low. They stayed close beneath the blankets, their conversation fading into the comfortable silence of two people who had stopped feeling the need to fill every minute. The night remained private.

***

In the morning, the cabin was half buried.

Thalia discovered this when she opened the door and found a shallow wall of snow against the threshold. She stared at it, then looked over her shoulder at Rhys.

"Your cabin is defective."

"Naturally."

"The snow is inside."

"Only because you opened the door."

"Very poor design."

He came over with a broom and began pushing the snow back outside. Thalia helped, laughing when the first sweep sent a flurry of white across his boots. The work was cold and unpleasant, but they made quick progress. By the time the path was clear enough to walk, both were out of breath and grinning.

"Breakfast?" he asked.

"After we check the lower cabins."

He nodded. Neither had forgotten the people who might need help. They had simply allowed themselves a night when those responsibilities belonged to someone else.

They walked down the hill together, boots crunching through fresh snow. At the cedar brake the path opened onto a broad field of white, crossed by the first tracks of campers heading toward the pavilion. Thalia could see the lanterns again, their glass dark beneath the morning light.

Rhys took her hand. She squeezed it, then let go when they reached the busy path.

"See you after breakfast?" he asked.

"I'll be there."

She kissed him quickly, in full view of anyone who happened to look up. Then she turned toward Cabin One, the cold sharp against her cheeks and the warmth of his company still with her.

The night had been theirs. The morning belonged to everyone. She liked being able to move between the two without feeling she had abandoned either.

---

## January 26: Something That Was Hers

Chiron offered Thalia the advanced archery program on a Thursday.

He did it quietly, at the end of a meeting about winter instruction, with no announcement to the other instructors and no suggestion that the offer was a reward for being unusually difficult to manage. He had been considering changes for spring, he said, and wanted someone to redesign the lessons for campers who had outgrown the beginner groups but were not ready to teach.

Thalia looked at the proposal.

"How much freedom would I have?"

"Enough to make mistakes."

"That's a terrible answer."

"I thought you might appreciate it."

She read the outline twice. There were hours to schedule, equipment to request and a small budget that would require more ingenuity than she liked admitting. The program would be hers to build, though other instructors would still have a say in how it operated. It was exactly the kind of work she enjoyed when nobody was looking over her shoulder.

"I'll do it," she said.

Chiron nodded as if he had expected nothing else.

"Then I'd like an outline before the end of February."

She took the papers, folded them beneath her arm and left the office with a sense of purpose that had nothing to do with Rhys Calder.

She enjoyed that feeling.

At the range she spent two hours inspecting the equipment and thinking about the program. She made lists of skills the students would need, considered how to divide them into groups and found herself arguing aloud with an imaginary instructor about the value of teaching judgment instead of merely rewarding accuracy.

By the time she reached supper, she had three pages of notes.

Rhys found her at the end of the pavilion bench.

"You're working."

"Obviously."

"On what?"

She handed him the outline.

He read it carefully, turning the pages one at a time. She watched his expression, already preparing to defend the parts she knew were unconventional. He stopped at the section on independent evaluation.

"You're giving them a chance to assess each other?"

"Yes."

"That's going to be chaotic."

"At first."

He considered. "Then they'll learn how difficult it is to make a fair judgment."

"Exactly."

He looked up, smiling. "This is good."

She felt an immediate rush of pleasure. It was not because she needed him to approve of the work. She didn't. It was because he had understood the point without requiring an explanation and had offered a real observation instead of praise for its own sake.

"Chiron asked me to build it," she said.

"Of course he did."

She gave him a warning look.

"What?"

"You sound smug."

"I'm pleased for you."

She looked down at the pages. The answer made her smile.

He leaned closer.

"Come celebrate?"

"I have work."

"Tomorrow?"

"More work."

"Then when you're ready."

She looked at him. He meant it. He wasn't going to pull her away from the program because he wanted another evening with her. He would still be there when she finished, and he expected her to have a life of her own beyond his cabin.

She appreciated that more than she wanted to admit.

"Later tonight," she said. "After I finish this section."

He smiled. "I'll be there."

***

She arrived shortly after nine, carrying the outline and a piece of bread she'd forgotten to eat at supper. Rhys opened the door with his sleeves rolled, a guitar string caught around one finger and a mildly accusing expression.

"You're late."

"I told you I had work."

"I know."

"Then don't complain."

"I was practicing being difficult."

"Poorly."

She stepped inside. The cabin was warm, and he had left the table mostly clear except for two cups and a small notebook. She set her papers down and looked at him.

"I finished the section."

"Good."

"And I want a break."

He smiled. "I was hoping."

She kissed him.

There was nothing hesitant about it. They had been together long enough that she knew how much she liked the way he drew her close, the quick change in his breathing when she caught his face between her hands, the smile he tried to hide when she broke off long enough to make a joke and then kissed him again. She felt the familiar pleasure of being wanted and the newer satisfaction of knowing she had come here after finishing work that belonged entirely to her.

He drew back, looking pleased.

"Good day?"

"Very."

"Then I'm glad you came."

She touched his collar, smoothing a fold in the fabric.

"Don't make me talk about the outline for another hour."

"I wouldn't dare."

"Good."

She kissed him again, slower this time, enjoying the contrast between the day's brisk competence and the quiet warmth of being near him. The room had become a place where she could leave her armor by the door without becoming less herself. She liked that. She liked the private intimacy of a man who could argue with her over targets in the morning and be delighted to see her at night.

Later they sat beneath the blanket while she told him about the program. She hadn't intended to. The ideas were still moving too quickly in her head, and he listened with the amused patience of someone who had been asked not to discuss a subject and then found himself trapped inside a detailed explanation of it.

"You're doing it again," he said.

"What?"

"Explaining the outline."

She looked at the pages on the table.

"So I am."

He smiled. "I'm enjoying it."

"You're easily amused."

"Possibly."

She leaned against him, content enough to stop talking. For a while the room was quiet except for the stove. Then she looked up.

"Would you want to help?"

He considered. "With the program?"

"Yes."

"I could. But it sounds like you've already got a plan."

"I do."

"Then maybe you should build the first draft yourself."

She stared at him. He was right, and she hated how quickly he had seen what she was trying to do. She wanted him involved because she wanted to share something important. She also wanted to make sure his place in her life was protected by a shared task. Those were not quite the same thing.

"You're refusing an opportunity to be bossed around," she said.

"A rare act of self-preservation."

She laughed and kissed him.

"All right. I'll do the first draft."

"And I'll read it when you want me to."

She nodded. The arrangement was better than she'd expected. It meant the project belonged to her, and his interest in it remained freely given rather than assigned.

She spent the night in his cabin. The choice had begun to feel ordinary, but never dull. She could leave her notes on the table, sit close to him with no urgent purpose and allow the rest of the evening to belong to them. There was desire in the closeness and affection in the small things that followed, the blanket drawn up when the room cooled, a mug placed where she could reach it, the way he smiled when she woke before the alarm.

In the morning she took the outline and went back to work.

She felt more like herself than she had in weeks.

---

## January 30: A Line Drawn in Chalk

The argument began in front of six instructors and ended with Thalia leaving the range before she said something she couldn't take back.

They had been reviewing her proposed advanced program. She wanted students to take responsibility for building a practice course, judging the safety of their own arrangements and explaining their decisions afterward. The instructors liked the idea until Rhys asked how she intended to prevent a poorly placed target from exposing another shooting lane.

She had an answer. It wasn't a good enough answer.

"The lanes will be separated," she said.

"By what?"

"Temporary screens. Chalk lines. Supervision."

"Chalk won't stop an arrow."

"I know what chalk does."

He looked at the plan. "Then the students need a fixed barrier before you can let them place anything."

Thalia felt the familiar heat of irritation. They had discussed a version of the plan the night before, sitting together on his cot. She had expected questions. She had not expected him to challenge a part she thought they'd already agreed on while five other people listened.

"That's not the exercise," she said.

"Then it shouldn't be an exercise until it can be done safely."

The room went quiet.

Chiron asked for both positions to be written down. Thalia complied. She explained the educational value of allowing experienced students to evaluate risk, and Rhys explained why certain risks ought to be removed by the instructors before any evaluation began. Both arguments were sound. They were also incompatible in the version of the plan Thalia had brought to the meeting.

When it ended, Chiron said they would return to the question the next day.

Thalia left without waiting for Rhys.

He found her later in the equipment shed.

"You walked out," he said.

"The meeting was over."

"You didn't look at me."

"I was occupied."

He leaned against the doorway. "You're angry."

"Very."

"Because I disagreed with you?"

"Because you waited until there was an audience to tell me."

He looked at her. "I didn't know we were agreed."

"We discussed it last night."

"We discussed the aim of the exercise. You didn't show me the exact lane layout until this morning."

She remembered the conversation. He was right about the drawing, which only made her more irritated.

"You could have spoken to me afterward."

"I could have. But I thought the barrier question needed answering before the group approved anything."

She looked down at the plan. It was covered in notes, some from the instructors and some in her own hurried pencil. She felt exposed in a way she hated. The project mattered to her. She wanted it to be good, and she had begun to rely on Rhys understanding her intentions even when he thought the details were wrong.

"Do you think I don't care about safety?" she asked.

He shook his head at once. "No."

"Then why did you make it sound as if I hadn't thought about the risks?"

"Because I didn't explain my objection well enough. I should have been clearer."

She looked up. That was a useful admission, but it didn't solve the disagreement. He still thought her design could put people in danger, and she still thought his alternative would remove the very problem the students were supposed to learn to identify.

They stood in silence.

At last Thalia picked up the plan.

"We can test both versions tomorrow," she said.

"With no arrows."

"Obviously."

"Use weighted bags for the trajectory."

"Yes."

He nodded. "All right."

She was still angry. He could tell, and for once he didn't try to coax a smile out of her. He left her with the plan and returned to his own work.

***

The next morning they built the course together.

They used rope to mark two shooting lanes, hung temporary screens and placed bright cloth on the ground where an arrow might travel if a beginner misjudged the angle. They then tested the arrangement with weighted bags, measuring where a careless release or a startled movement could send an object beyond the intended area.

Rhys's objection proved valid. One of the proposed positions allowed a bag to cross into the second lane when thrown from the wrong point. Thalia watched it happen and felt a sharp disappointment that was not entirely about being wrong.

She also discovered that the barrier he wanted could be moved far enough back to preserve most of the students' decision-making space.

They marked the revised design in chalk and tested it again. This time the bags remained inside the protected zone. The students would still have a problem to solve, but the most serious danger would already have been removed.

"That's better," Rhys said.

Thalia looked at the course. "Yes."

He waited.

She turned toward him. "You were right about the barrier."

He smiled faintly. "And you were right about keeping the exercise open enough to require judgment."

"Don't make a ceremony of our mutual competence."

"I wouldn't dare."

They walked back to the shed together. The tension had eased, but the hurt from the public disagreement lingered. Thalia could feel it whenever she remembered the other instructors watching her defend a plan with a weakness she should have caught herself.

At the door she stopped.

"I don't want you agreeing with me because we're together," she said.

"I know."

"But I want you to understand that I care about this."

"I do."

"And next time, if you think I'm missing something important, tell me sooner."

He nodded. "I can do that."

She looked at him. "I won't always be pleasant when you do."

"I've noticed."

She laughed in spite of herself.

He came closer, but waited. She put her hands on his shoulders and kissed him, the familiar warmth of the gesture returning after a day of keeping their distance. It felt good, not because the kiss erased the disagreement but because they had done the work that made affection possible without resentment.

When they separated he was smiling.

"Come by tonight?" he asked.

She considered. "Yes."

"Good."

"After I finish the revised plan."

He grinned. "I wouldn't expect anything less."

That evening she arrived with the new drawing rolled beneath her arm. He took it from her, set it on the table and pulled her close before she could begin another explanation. She kissed him gladly, laughing when the edge of the paper bumped against his shoulder.

For a while the work could wait.

Later they reviewed the drawing together, taking turns with the pencil and arguing over a line that was still two inches too close to the target. The conversation was much more pleasant with the room warm and no one else watching. It was also more accurate, which pleased Thalia nearly as much as the kissing had.

She stayed the night. In the morning she took the revised plan to Chiron, who approved a small pilot session and told her to report the results before expanding it.

It was not a victory over Rhys. She found she liked that better.

---

## February 4: The Audition

Rhys left for the audition on a Sunday morning with his guitar case, a clean shirt and a small notebook Thalia had given him because his previous one was full.

She went to the road gate with him. This time she did not spend the morning pretending she wasn't worried. He wasn't leaving to repair a family home or settle an emergency. He was going because he wanted to see whether his music could carry him somewhere new. It was his choice, and she liked him for making it, even when she disliked the part that took him away from her for a few days.

"You're going to be late," she said as he checked the guitar case.

"I have twenty minutes."

"Which you'll use explaining to me why you're not late."

He closed the latches.

"You brought the score?"

"Yes."

"The corrected version?"

"Yes."

"Not the one where the second line disappears?"

"That one is safely at home."

She nodded, satisfied.

Rhys looked at her, amused. "Anything else?"

"Don't let anybody rush you through the opening. And don't start too fast just because you're nervous."

"Thalia."

"What?"

"You're doing it again."

She looked at him. She had spent the last ten minutes making lists of instructions because she didn't want to say what mattered more: that she hoped they would hear the same talent she heard in him, and that she feared the answer would take him farther away.

She took a breath.

"I want you to do well," she said.

"I know."

"And if they offer you something, I want you to tell me before you decide."

He nodded. "I will."

She stepped close, caught his coat between her hands and kissed him. He met her eagerly, the familiar warmth of his response making the cold road feel distant. For a moment the guitar and the audition and the journey ahead of him fell away. She could be happy for him and want him to stay. Both feelings were permitted.

When she drew back, she smiled.

"Go impress somebody."

"I'll try."

"Don't try. Do it."

He laughed, kissed her once more and went toward the waiting van.

***

The next two days passed more quickly than Thalia expected.

She had her own program to prepare, a pilot session to supervise and a stack of equipment requests to review with Chiron. The work was engaging enough that she sometimes forgot to look toward the north path until supper. Then she would remember Rhys was away and feel a small ache, no longer sharp with panic but persistent.

On Monday she ran the first advanced lesson.

The students arrived expecting a difficult shooting course. Instead she gave them a map of the range, a bundle of rope and a list of safety constraints. They had forty minutes to design a usable exercise without placing anyone in the path of a stray arrow. No shooting would take place until their plan had been tested.

The group argued immediately.

Thalia watched them from the equipment bench. She had anticipated some of the objections and deliberately left the others for the students to discover. One boy wanted the targets farther away than the available space allowed. A girl pointed out that the sight lines would be poor. Another student tried to solve the problem by simply closing the second lane. The debate was loud and inelegant and exactly the kind of thinking Thalia had hoped to produce.

When the forty minutes ended, the students had three competing proposals. None was perfect. Each had something worth discussing.

Thalia was delighted.

She wrote up the results after supper, noting which students had caught the risks and which had needed prompting. Then she found herself wanting to tell Rhys about the lesson, not because he had helped design it but because he would understand why she was pleased. She wrote him a short note and left it with the outgoing mail, knowing it probably wouldn't reach him before he returned.

*The students argued for forty minutes and nobody was injured. I call that progress. One of them tried to solve a lane-spacing problem by removing the lane. You would have enjoyed the performance.*

*I hope the audition goes well. If it doesn't, you're still required to come back and criticize my target placement.*

She closed the letter with a small sketch of an arrow striking just off center.

***

Rhys returned Tuesday evening.

Thalia saw him at the edge of the pavilion before he saw her. He looked tired, carrying the guitar case in one hand and a small paper bag in the other. He had traveled all day. There was dust on his coat and a thin crease across his forehead that told her he'd been thinking too hard.

She went to him.

He saw her, stopped and smiled.

"Well?" she asked.

"Hello to you too."

She kissed him. It was meant to be a quick greeting and became something warmer when he caught her waist and drew her close. She had missed the simple pleasure of being able to reach for him, and she didn't bother hiding it.

When they separated, she looked at him properly.

"How was it?"

He took a moment before answering.

"They liked the piece."

"Of course they did."

"They want me to send a longer arrangement and come back for a second session in a few weeks."

She felt the pleasure first, quick and genuine. Then the uncertainty followed.

"That's good," she said.

"It is."

"And you haven't decided what you'll do."

"No."

He looked at her, waiting.

Thalia thought about the program she'd built, the students she'd supervised and the sense of accomplishment that had filled her day. She knew what it felt like to be taken seriously for work she'd done herself. She wanted that for him.

"Then you're going to write the arrangement," she said.

"Am I?"

She raised an eyebrow. "You're going to decide whether you want it enough to do the work. I'm not making the choice for you."

He smiled. "That's better."

She caught his hand.

"Come home with me," she said, using the phrase before she had time to wonder whether it was too much.

He looked surprised, then pleased.

"To my cabin?"

"Unless you've acquired a second home without telling me."

He laughed and followed her north.

The room was cold when they arrived, so they lit the stove and left the travel bag by the door. Thalia helped him put away the guitar, then stopped as he reached for the stack of music he'd brought back from the audition.

"Leave it."

"I was going to sort it."

"Tomorrow."

He considered. Then he set the papers down.

She stepped closer and kissed him. There was no drama in it, no need to celebrate the audition as a victory before he knew the result. She wanted him near because she had missed him, and the warmth of his answering kiss told her he felt the same.

He touched her cheek.

"You had a good lesson," he said.

She blinked. "How do you know?"

"Chiron told me before I came up."

"That man is a gossip."

Rhys laughed. "He sounded impressed."

"He should be."

"I'm impressed too."

She felt a quiet pleasure in the fact that they had returned with separate stories to tell. They could share the stories now, and they would remain their own.

Later, after they had eaten and settled beneath the blanket, he asked her to describe the students' plans. She did so with more detail than either of them had intended. When she finished, he described the audition room, the musician who had asked him to change the tempo and the moment he realized he'd been playing too quickly because he was nervous.

They laughed at the parts that had gone wrong.

Then they stopped talking.

Thalia leaned toward him, and the intimacy of being together again felt natural rather than ceremonial. She kissed him slowly, content to let the evening become private after a day spent in the company of other people's expectations. He drew her close. For a while neither had anything more important to say.

Their work was unfinished. They were pleased with it anyway.

---

## February 10: An Invitation Without a Script

The invitation came in Rhys's mother's handwriting.

It was a short note, enclosed in his weekly letter, with no formal greeting and only three sentences.

*If you're ever in our part of the country, I'd like to meet you. Rhys tells me you think his cooking is terrible. I'd enjoy having an independent opinion.*

*There will be tea. I make no promises about the chairs.*

Thalia read it twice, then looked at him.

"Your mother thinks I'm coming to inspect furniture."

"She knows you."

"She knows a few things you've told her."

"That might be enough."

She studied the note. The handwriting was square and slightly impatient, and there was a small ink smudge beneath the signature. She recognized something of Rhys in the economy of it. His mother was not offering a grand welcome. She was suggesting tea and a conversation, the sort of ordinary meeting that ought to be easy for two adults.

For Thalia it wasn't easy.

She had spent years being described as Beryl Grace's daughter and Zeus's daughter, sometimes as though those labels explained everything anyone needed to know about her. She disliked meeting people who had decided in advance what she would be. Rhys's mother knew about her from letters and photographs, from the parts of her life he had chosen to describe. Thalia trusted him. She wasn't certain she trusted the version of herself someone might imagine from a few stories.

"Does she expect me to come soon?" she asked.

Rhys shook his head. "No. She knows we're busy."

"Then why send it?"

"Because she wants to meet you."

Thalia looked up. The plain answer made her pause.

He came closer and sat beside her.

"You're worried," he said.

"I'm considering the risks."

"Of tea?"

"Of being interrogated by a woman who raised you."

He laughed. "A serious danger."

She looked at him. "You make it sound ridiculous."

"It's not ridiculous. She can be difficult."

"Like you."

"Worse, sometimes."

"An alarming prospect."

He smiled, then became serious.

"She isn't expecting a version of you who never argues. She likes people who have opinions. She just wants to know the person I keep writing about."

Thalia looked at the note again. She could see no demand in it. No timetable, no instruction to impress anyone, no suggestion that the meeting would determine the future of the relationship. She had created most of the pressure herself.

"I'd like to meet her," she said.

Rhys looked pleased.

"But not before the next training review. I have too much to finish."

"She'll understand."

"And we go for one afternoon. Not an entire week."

He laughed. "I'll tell her."

"Don't make a formal announcement."

"I'll put it in a letter."

"An appalling compromise."

He took her hand.

"Thalia," he said, "you don't have to do this for me."

She studied him. He meant it. She could refuse the invitation and he would still want to see her. But she wasn't going because she needed his approval or his mother's. She was curious about the woman who had taught him how to sew badly and cook well, who had sent her a scarf after seeing a photograph and who apparently considered kitchen furniture a suitable subject for an introduction.

"I want to," she said.

His smile returned.

"Then we'll find a day."

She leaned toward him and kissed him, letting the small satisfaction of the decision settle between them. The invitation was no longer a threat. It was a possibility they could choose.

***

That evening Thalia went back to Cabin One and found Annabeth sitting at the table with a pile of measurements.

"I'm going to meet Rhys's mother," she said.

Annabeth looked up.

"When?"

"After the training review."

"Good."

"That's all?"

"What do you want me to say?"

Thalia considered. She had expected teasing, or at least a pointed question about whether this meant the relationship was becoming serious. Her sister was too perceptive to need the question answered.

"Nothing," Thalia said.

Annabeth returned to her work. Then she looked up again.

"Are you nervous?"

"A little."

"That's unusual."

"I've been told."

Annabeth smiled. "You don't have to be impressive all the time."

Thalia stared at her. The sentence was so close to something Rhys might have said that she felt briefly surrounded by people who were becoming too good at knowing her.

"Have you been conspiring?" she asked.

"Constantly."

"Terrible."

Annabeth laughed. Thalia leaned over the table to look at the latest drawing, and the conversation moved on.

Later, when she returned to Rhys's cabin, she found him reading her notes for the advanced program. He had marked two sections with questions and left the rest alone. She liked that. He had taken the work seriously without treating it as his own.

She sat beside him.

"Your mother," she said.

He looked up. "Yes?"

"What would she think if I arrived and said your kitchen shelves are badly designed?"

He smiled. "She'd probably agree and then ask you to fix them."

"That sounds dangerous."

"Very."

She kissed him, smiling. The kiss lasted longer than she'd planned, and when she drew back he was watching her with a warmth that made the room feel smaller.

"I am glad you want to meet her," he said.

Thalia looked at him. "Don't make it more important than it is."

"I won't."

She caught his collar and kissed him again. They had their own private evening ahead, and the possibility of meeting his mother didn't need to occupy all of it.

When the lamp went low, she was glad to find that wanting him remained as immediate as ever, unchanged by the fact that she was beginning to know the people and places outside the cabin that had made him who he was.

She stayed the night, and the invitation remained folded inside the book on her table, waiting for a day they would choose together.

---

## February 10: An Answer He Hadn't Asked Her to Give

The letter arrived on a Saturday, folded beneath the edge of a practice schedule and addressed to Rhys in an unfamiliar hand.

Thalia was at the range when the courier delivered it. Rhys had been checking a target frame for damage, his coat thrown over one shoulder because the afternoon was warmer than anyone had expected. The courier called his name, handed over the envelope and went on toward the infirmary without pausing for conversation.

He looked at the writing and went very still.

Thalia knew immediately what it was.

"Open it," she said.

He glanced at her. "You could let me be nervous for a minute."

"You've been nervous for six days."

"Then I'm getting good at it."

She put down the measuring pole. There was no audience beyond two instructors near the far targets, and neither was paying attention. Rhys turned the envelope over twice, as if a more favorable answer might be visible on the back.

"Do you want me to go?" she asked.

"No."

"Then open it."

He laughed once, a short sound that had more tension than amusement in it, and broke the seal.

Thalia watched his face instead of the paper. His eyes moved across the first paragraph, paused, and went back to the beginning. He read the second paragraph more slowly. By the time he reached the end, his expression had changed into something she could not immediately name.

"Well?"

"They want me."

She caught her breath. He looked at the page again, as if he needed to confirm that the sentence hadn't altered.

"They want me," he repeated. "Not permanently. There’s a spring series, six weeks of rehearsals and performances. They want me to come for the full run."

Thalia stared at him.

For weeks they'd discussed the possibility of an audition, the fact that he ought to try, the fact that liking somebody at camp wasn't a good reason to refuse a career opportunity before it existed. Now the opportunity did exist, inconveniently folded into a piece of paper.

"That's good," she said.

He looked up. "Is it?"

"Of course it is."

"Thalia."

"You went because you wanted a chance to play with them. They gave you the chance."

"Yes."

"Then it's good."

He studied her. She could see what he was searching for: the hesitation she hadn't managed to conceal, the old instinct to turn a pleasant thing into a calculation about who would leave whom and what that meant. She was tempted to tell him not to examine her. She was also tempted to make a joke that would let both of them avoid the subject entirely.

Instead she stepped forward and threw her arms around him.

He nearly dropped the letter.

"Gods, Grace."

"Congratulations," she said into his shoulder.

His laugh came more easily this time. He held her close, the envelope creased between two fingers. For a while she let herself feel exactly what she felt: pride, happiness, an immense affection for the man she'd watched struggle with an unfinished tune, and a hard little knot of selfish disappointment.

When she pulled back, he was smiling.

"You're pleased," he said.

"Don't sound astonished."

"I'm not."

"You're looking astonished."

"I'm looking at you."

She kissed him. It was more than a congratulatory peck, long enough that he forgot the letter in his hand until the corner brushed her cheek. She laughed, caught the envelope before he could crush it and pressed it against his chest.

"Keep that. It appears important."

He grinned and folded it carefully into his pocket.

"Let's go somewhere," she said.

"Where?"

"Anywhere without a target in sight."

They took the flat path toward the lake. Thalia led at first, walking quickly because she wanted to move, then slowing when she realized she was leaving him several paces behind. He caught up without commenting.

The afternoon had a weak winter brightness. Wind moved over the water, breaking it into long grey bands. Thalia stopped beside the low wall where they'd sat on an earlier Sunday and watched him take the letter out again.

"Read me the details," she said.

He did. The series would begin after a short preparation period. There were rehearsals four afternoons each week, evening performances and travel between several nearby towns. The pay was modest but respectable. Accommodation was arranged; musicians were expected to bring their own instruments and be available for the entire six weeks. There was no language about continuing afterward.

"That's work," Thalia said when he finished.

"Yes."

"You like work."

"Some kinds."

"You like this kind."

He looked at her. "Yes."

She nodded. That was the difficult part of loving someone competent. Their abilities opened doors that didn't necessarily lead toward her.

She had abilities of her own. Chiron's advanced archery program was already moving beyond the pilot stage, and she was preparing to take on a larger group in spring. If anyone had suggested she abandon the work because Rhys wanted more evenings together, she would have told them precisely what she thought of the proposal.

She intended to offer him the same freedom.

But an intention wasn't a feeling, and she wasn't going to pretend otherwise.

"I hate that you're going," she said.

"I haven't said yes yet."

"You want to."

"I think I do."

"Then I hate that you'll be gone."

He stood beside her, watching the water.

"I don't want to leave either."

"That's not true."

"It's not all true. Part of me wants to go. Part of me wants to stay."

She looked at him. "You don't have to choose which feeling is respectable."

He smiled faintly. "You've been practicing that advice."

"On a very difficult student."

He reached for her hand. She took it, then pulled him closer until they were standing face to face.

"Do you think this changes anything between us?" he asked.

She thought about the letter, the six weeks, the possibility of him returning after rehearsals and finding a different routine at camp. She thought about the months they'd spent learning how to be together without turning affection into ownership. She couldn't promise that nothing would change. That would be a lie.

"I think you'll be away," she said. "And I'll be here. We already know how to do that badly and improve."

"Comforting."

"I try."

He smiled. She rested a hand against his cheek and felt the cold air around his face.

"Go," she said. "Play. Come back when you've finished, and tell me about it."

He kissed her with a sudden, unmistakable eagerness that made her laugh against his mouth. She hadn't been expecting gratitude. She had been expecting an argument about whether she'd meant it.

When he drew back, she caught his coat and kept him near.

"I didn't say it wouldn't be difficult."

"I know."

"And I'm allowed to complain."

"I'm counting on it."

"And you are not allowed to turn your six weeks away into some noble sacrifice you're making for me."

He laughed softly. "Noted."

She kissed him again. The wind was cold. Her hands were cold. The warmth of him was immediate and familiar, and she was almost painfully conscious of how much she'd missed it the last time he went away. That didn't make her want to hold him back.

At the fork they turned toward his cabin rather than the pavilion. They were both smiling a little too much to pretend the afternoon was ordinary now.

Inside, he set the letter on the workbench, weighted beneath the small leather case. Thalia took off her coat. He came over to help, and she turned before he could finish hanging it, caught his shirtfront in one hand and pulled him close.

"You're going to be insufferable tonight," she said.

"I've been told."

"You'll keep saying that they chose you."

"They did."

"And I'll keep having to congratulate you."

"A terrible burden."

She laughed, kissed him and felt the excitement of the news settle into something distinctly physical. He was looking at her in the way he did when the rest of the room stopped mattering. She knew that look by now and had never grown tired of it.

She drew him nearer. He met her immediately. Their smiles disappeared into another kiss, and she felt him lean toward her as if the same thought had reached them both at once: this was a good day, and they wanted to spend part of it alone.

The letter remained on the workbench. The stove was lit. The door was closed against the wind.

They let the evening become private.

***

Later, Rhys was trying to explain the travel schedule for a second time.

Thalia lay beside him beneath the quilt, listening with one eye closed and a hand resting against his arm. She understood the schedule. She had understood it at the lake. She just liked the sound of his voice when he was excited and forgot he was repeating himself.

"You're not listening," he said.

"I am."

"What did I say?"

"That the second town has a rehearsal hall with terrible acoustics, the third has a piano you're apparently afraid to touch, and the opening performance is on a Friday."

He considered. "You were listening."

"I'm occasionally capable of it."

He laughed and kissed her forehead.

She opened her eyes.

"Rhys?"

"Hm?"

"Did you think I would tell you not to go?"

He went still. "A little."

"That's insulting."

"I know."

She thought about protesting further. Then she remembered her own panic when she heard the rumor in January and the way she'd arrived at his cabin ready to take offense before she'd asked what was true.

"Maybe not entirely insulting," she admitted.

He turned toward her. "I didn't want you to feel I'd already made the decision without you."

"You can make decisions about your career without asking my permission."

"I know."

"But I'd rather hear about them from you than from the range."

He nodded. "Agreed."

She rested her head on his shoulder. The conversation was less thrilling than kissing him had been. It mattered just as much. They were learning to share news without treating it as a test of loyalty, to let a future exist beyond their cabin without making the cabin meaningless.

At last she raised her head and kissed him once more.

"Congratulations," she murmured.

He smiled against her mouth. "Thank you."

This time she let the words be enough.

---

## February 14: The Unromantic Gift

Thalia was halfway through an argument about arrow retrieval when somebody handed her a flower.

She looked at it. A single white blossom, still attached to an unruly length of stem, had been tied with dark thread to a scrap of paper. It looked less like a romantic offering than a botanical specimen rescued from a particularly careless laboratory.

The courier was a daughter of Hermes with a grin sharp enough to constitute a warning.

"From the north cabin."

"Who sent it?"

"There's a note."

"That wasn't my question."

"Then ask him."

The girl departed before Thalia could demand a better answer.

Thalia untied the paper. Rhys had written only one sentence.

*This is not an elaborate romantic gesture. I have witnesses who can confirm it took less than forty seconds.*

She stared at the flower, then laughed.

The fourteenth of February had no particular hold on her imagination. She knew the customs people attached to it. She had observed enough couples at school and camp to know that on certain days they seemed determined to make affection into a public competition over gifts. She had no interest in being presented with proof that a man could spend money or commission a speech.

But she liked a white flower tied badly to a joke.

That irritated her.

She carried the flower back to Cabin One after the lesson and put it in a cup of water. Annabeth examined it over the edge of her notebook.

"Rhys?"

"Apparently."

"That's sweet."

"It's poorly tied."

"You kept it."

"Botanical curiosity."

Annabeth looked at the cup, then at Thalia. She wisely returned to her work.

At four Thalia went to the north cabin carrying the only gift she'd been able to think of: an old wooden case she'd repaired herself. The hinges had been loose when she found it in the storage cupboard; she'd sanded the edges, replaced the screws and lined the interior with a piece of dark cloth. It was large enough to hold his pencils, strings and the folded sheets of music that always ended up scattered across the workbench.

She'd spent three evenings making it and told herself the work was an exercise in carpentry.

Rhys opened the door before she could knock twice.

"You're early."

"I have business."

"Terrifying."

She pushed the case into his hands.

He looked at it, then at her.

"What is this?"

"A receptacle. You may have encountered the concept."

He opened it. His expression changed.

"You made this."

"No. It fell from heaven and asked to be delivered."

"Thalia."

She shrugged, suddenly uncomfortable. It would have been easier to give him a sharpened knife or a repaired bow, an object so practical that gratitude could be dismissed as an irrelevant formality. This was practical too, but she'd chosen the cloth because it was the same deep blue as the scarf he'd lent her. She'd checked the inside dimensions against the papers on his workbench. There was no way to pretend the care had been accidental.

He ran his thumb along the edge of the lining.

"It's beautiful," he said.

"Don't exaggerate."

"I'm not."

He looked up. She was prepared for a joke. He set the box carefully on the table and crossed the room toward her.

"Come here."

She went, already smiling.

He kissed her with enough immediate pleasure that she laughed softly against his mouth. His hands settled at her waist. She drew him closer, and the kiss became less of a thank-you than a happy recognition: they'd both been thinking about each other in private, and now they were in the same room.

"You're pleased," she murmured.

"Very."

"Then stop talking."

He kissed her again.

The workbench pressed against Thalia's back. She caught Rhys's collar, felt the warmth of him beneath the fabric, and smiled when his composure faltered. There was a kind of delight in knowing how to unsettle him. Months earlier she would have called it victory. Now she knew it was something more intimate than that.

His fingers found the loose end of her scarf.

"You're wearing the one my mother made."

"It's warmer."

"Mine wasn't warm enough?"

"Yours was appalling."

"You wore it all winter."

"An act of endurance."
