He untangled the scarf gently and laid it on the chair. She didn't try to stop him. She liked how carefully he handled things that mattered to her, even when she pretended they didn't.

He turned back toward her. She caught his hand, drew him close and kissed him until the room seemed to narrow around them. He was smiling when she paused, but she could feel his pulse quick beneath her fingers.

Something changed when he shifted his weight.

He made a small sound and pulled one hand away.

Thalia looked down. There was a dark bandage around the base of his thumb.

"What happened?"

"Nothing dramatic. My hand cramped yesterday while I was practicing. I should have stopped."

She took his hand and examined the wrapping. It was neatly done, which meant somebody other than Rhys had probably insisted on it.

"You're injured."

"Minorly."

"That isn't a word."

"It is today."

She could have scolded him. She could have announced that he was impossible and demanded he report to the infirmary before touching his guitar again. Instead she looked at his face and saw a quick flicker of embarrassment. He wanted to be here with her, and he disliked having his own body interrupt the moment.

She knew something about that.

"Sit," she said.

He raised an eyebrow. "I thought we were in the middle of something."

"We were. Now sit."

He went to the cot, grinning, and held up the bandaged hand.

"Should I expect a lecture?"

"Later. A long one."

"Looking forward to it."

She sat beside him and reached for his uninjured hand. She had learned the strange pleasure of being able to change an evening without treating the change as failure. He'd done the same for her bad heel when it hurt. Now she could do it for him without becoming his nurse or his judge.

She leaned in and kissed him.

He responded eagerly, relieved enough that she felt the difference. This time she took her time. She could move closer, settle comfortably beside him and let the warmth of his company replace the urgency of the first few minutes. When he kissed her cheek, she turned and caught his mouth again.

The hand with the bandage remained safely between them. They found other ways to be affectionate. Thalia liked that neither had to perform perfect confidence to enjoy an evening.

After a while he drew back, smiling.

"You're very persuasive."

"I'm not making an argument."

"That's new."

She kissed him again before he could elaborate.

***

They ate supper at the small table, the new wooden box beside the lamp.

Rhys opened it twice while they were eating.

"You're going to wear the hinges out," Thalia said.

"I want to look."

"You've seen it."

"I like looking."

She tried not to smile. There was something charming about his complete lack of embarrassment at appreciating a gift she'd made. He turned it around, examined the join where she'd repaired the corner, and asked what she'd used to sand the edges.

"Fine paper," she said. "Then oil."

"It smells good."

"That's because I cleaned it before giving it to you."

"A radical concept."

He laughed. She picked up his bandaged hand and laid it gently on the table between them.

"How much practice?"

"Too much."

"How much is too much?"

"Four hours. Then a little more."

"You're nineteen. Learn to count."

"I was distracted."

"By what?"

"Trying to play something difficult without admitting it was difficult."

She considered. "That's idiotic."

"I know."

"And familiar."

He looked at her. "You?"

"I have been accused of similar behavior."

"By whom?"

"Some unreasonable physician with a horse's body."

Rhys laughed, and she felt the last of her annoyance dissolve. The problem was real but small. He would rest his hand. She would stop treating him like a student who required correction. Neither had to turn it into a test of whether the other cared.

"What did you think of the flower?" he asked.

"Poor construction."

"The flower?"

"The knot."

"I tied it quickly."

"Your note said so."

"I was hoping the note would be persuasive."

"It was."

He looked at her. "Really?"

She took a moment to answer. She could have made another joke. Instead she gave him the thing she'd been thinking about since morning.

"I liked that you wanted to make me laugh," she said.

His expression changed.

"Then it worked."

"Against my better judgment."

He reached across the table with his good hand. She took it.

The rest of the evening was ordinary in the nicest possible sense. They played a few cards, argued about the rules, and put his new box beneath the lamp where it could hold the scores. At some point Thalia moved to the cot and he joined her. She rested her head against his shoulder while he told her about the music group's proposed route and the first rehearsal schedule. She listened, asked questions and then kissed him when she was tired of thinking about all the places he would be without her.

The kiss was warm and unhurried. He drew her near, and they stayed that way while the stove burned lower. She could want him and miss him before he'd even left. She could also enjoy the fact that he was here tonight.

Later she told him, "Your flower's in water."

He smiled. "You kept it."

"For scientific purposes."

"Of course."

She kissed his cheek.

"You can send another," she added.

He laughed. "Even if the knot's terrible?"

"Especially if the knot's terrible."

Outside, the snow began falling again. The new box sat on the workbench with his pencils arranged inside it. Thalia looked at it once before they turned down the lamp, pleased by the thought that something she'd made would travel with him when he went.

---

## February 16: The Offer Had Terms

Rhys came back from the second audition carrying a contract.

He hadn't signed it. That was the first thing he told Thalia when she met him near the road gate, and the speed of the announcement made her laugh despite the nerves she'd been carrying all afternoon.

"You look guilty," she said.

"I thought I should begin with the most important fact."

"Have you robbed a bank?"

"No."

"Then I'm unlikely to be furious."

He handed her the folded papers. They stood beneath the gate lantern while she read them, the afternoon wind catching the loose edge and trying to turn the pages before she was ready.

The ensemble wanted him for six weeks beginning in April. The work involved rehearsals, performances and travel between several towns. The payment was modest but real, enough to cover expenses and leave something over. He would not be able to return to camp between performances except perhaps for a brief visit. They wanted an answer within ten days.

Thalia reached the end and looked up.

"They want you."

"Yes."

"That's good."

"It is."

He watched her. She could see the worry in his face, the way he had chosen to bring the offer to her before allowing himself to enjoy it.

She handed the papers back.

"What do you want?"

He took a breath.

"I want to do it."

She nodded. The answer hurt, but only because she wanted him near. It was also the answer she had hoped he would give. He had worked hard for the invitation, and she had heard the music that brought him there.

"Then you should," she said.

He looked relieved for half a second, then uncertain again.

"And us?"

Thalia looked toward the road. The van had already gone, leaving them alone in the cold. She considered the question carefully.

"We've been apart before."

"Not for six weeks."

"No."

"And you'll have the advanced program."

"Yes."

He folded the contract and put it inside his coat.

"I don't want you pretending this is easy," he said.

"I'm not."

"I also don't want to come back and find you've spent six weeks being angry because I went."

Thalia looked at him sharply.

"Then don't make me responsible for being happy every day you're gone."

He considered, then nodded. "Fair."

She moved closer. She had been thinking about him all day, imagining him in the audition room with his guitar and the difficult opening he'd practiced until his fingers hurt. She wanted him to know she was proud of him without having to make the feeling into a speech.

She took his hand.

"I'm pleased for you," she said.

He looked at her. "Really?"

"Yes. I'm also going to miss you. Those are both true."

His smile was immediate and unmistakable.

She kissed him.

The kiss was not a solution to the logistics ahead. It was simply the pleasure of finding each other again after a day that had mattered to him. He held her close, and she felt the warmth of him beneath the cold coat. When they drew apart, he was still smiling.

"Come home?" he asked.

She nodded.

***

At the cabin they spread the contract across the table.

Thalia wanted to know where the rehearsals would take place, how much time he would actually spend traveling and whether the payment covered the costs the group expected him to incur. She read every line, making notes in the margin where something was unclear. Rhys watched with growing amusement.

"I thought you said it was my decision."

"It is. I want you to make it with good information."

"An important distinction."

"Don't get clever."

He leaned back and let her read.

The terms were reasonably fair. The group would cover lodging and most travel, and the payment was enough to make the project worthwhile. There were still questions about the exact schedule and one clause that left too much discretion with the organizers. She marked it, then handed him the paper.

"Ask them to clarify that."

"I was going to."

"Good."

He looked at her. "You're enjoying this."

"I like contracts that don't try to trick people."

"And you like telling me what to do."

"Obviously."

He laughed, then folded the papers away.

They ate supper slowly. Thalia told him about the advanced program's first successful week, the students who had surprised her and the mistakes she still needed to correct. He listened with real attention, asking questions that showed he understood the work without treating it as a lesser version of his own opportunity.

At one point she put down her fork.

"What if I wanted to visit you?"

He looked up.

"On tour?"

"Yes."

"I'd like that."

"I'm not saying I will. The program may not allow it."

"I know."

"But if there were a weekend when you were close enough, we could arrange something."

He nodded. "We could."

She considered. That was all they could decide now. She had a program to run, he had a tour to accept, and neither knew exactly how the schedules would fit together. It was a problem with actual constraints, not a test of whether their affection was sufficiently grand.

She found that oddly comforting.

After dinner she put the dishes aside and came to sit beside him. He had been quiet for several minutes, looking down at the contract as though it might change if he stared long enough.

"Rhys."

He looked up.

"You should be happy."

"I am."

"Then stop acting as if you've done something wrong."

He smiled faintly. "I don't want to hurt you."

"You'll hurt me if you lie. You won't hurt me by wanting to do work you're good at."

He looked at her for a long moment.

"All right," he said.

She touched his face and kissed him. This time she let the kiss become the answer to the immediate question: did they still want each other, even with distance ahead? His hand moved to her waist, and the eagerness of his response made her smile. They had months of shared history now, nights spent together, letters written in absence, disagreements they had survived. She trusted the wanting, not because it guaranteed the future but because it remained freely chosen.

The lamp burned lower. The contract stayed folded on the table while they spent the evening close together, talking when they wanted to and allowing silence when they didn't. They had ten days to answer the ensemble. Tonight they didn't need to decide anything more.

Before she slept, Thalia looked toward the papers one last time.

Six weeks was a long time. She would miss him. She was also pleased that a person she loved had been offered something he had earned. She had never expected the two feelings to fit so comfortably beside each other.

---

## February 18: The Sound of Not Playing

By Sunday, Rhys had made the sore hand everybody else's problem.

He had been told to rest it for three days. Two had passed. He had obeyed the instruction for perhaps four hours, then begun moving pencils around the workbench and pretending that working on arrangements did not involve the same joints he was supposed to leave alone.

Thalia found him sitting beside the window with a book open on his lap. The guitar was in its case. That ought to have been reassuring. Instead the book was upside down.

She stood in the doorway long enough to decide he had noticed her.

"Your education is coming along badly," she said.

Rhys looked down. "It was right side up earlier."

"That's what worries me."

He shut the book and set it on the chair. His injured thumb was wrapped in a clean strip of cloth. He kept the hand resting against his leg, which told her that it hurt more than he had admitted when she left on Friday.

"How's the hand?"

"Annoying."

"Excellent. It's developed your personality."

"Someone had to."

She hung up her coat and sat beside him. She had come expecting to find him practicing a piece or getting ready for supper. Instead he looked frustrated enough to throw the book through the window, and his inability to do anything about the frustration made him look worse.

"What's wrong?" she asked.

"I have six weeks of music ahead of me. I'm supposed to be getting ready. I can't even turn a tuning peg without my thumb reminding me that I was an idiot."

"You're healing."

"Slowly."

"Two days."

He looked at her. "You have no idea how long two days are when you can't do the thing you're supposed to be good at."

Thalia opened her mouth, ready to describe her own weeks with the bad heel. Then she stopped. He knew she'd been injured. The fact that she'd endured something difficult didn't make his frustration unreasonable.

"Fine," she said. "It feels longer."

"Yes."

"And you're angry."

"Very."

"Then be angry."

He laughed once, without amusement. "What does that accomplish?"

"Nothing. Not every feeling has to accomplish something."

He stared at her. She had used the tone she normally reserved for declaring that a target had been measured incorrectly. The irony was not lost on either of them.

"You've been taking notes," he said.

"From the worst teacher I've ever met."

He smiled, but his attention wandered toward the guitar case again.

Thalia followed his gaze. "Do you think they'll change their minds?"

His head snapped back toward her. The question was too close, and they both knew it.

"I didn't say that."

"You didn't have to."

He was silent. She waited, though waiting did not come naturally. At last he leaned back against the wall.

"They heard me play once," he said. "One audition. They decided I was worth inviting. What if I show up and they realize they were wrong?"

Thalia could have answered with a list of reasons he was talented. She knew several. She'd watched him perform in front of the camp, heard the arrangements he'd worked on until midnight, seen other musicians take his ideas seriously. None of that would settle a fear he had already learned to argue against.

"Then you'll learn something," she said.

He looked at her.

"And you'll still be the man who can play the tune you spent months writing," she continued. "It won't vanish because one group of musicians dislikes your tempo."

"Very comforting."

"I'm not trying to comfort you."

"That much is clear."

"I'm trying to tell you that you have work worth doing even if somebody doesn't approve it."

He turned that over in silence.

"I don't like thinking I might fail," he said.

"Nobody does."

"You seem perfectly happy to."

"Then you haven't watched me lose at anything."

He laughed, properly this time. She was relieved, though she made no effort to show it.

They sat for a moment without speaking. Then Rhys reached for the book again, looked at it, and put it down.

"I don't know what to do with myself."

"Come out."

"Where?"

"Anywhere. You can walk without using your hand."

"You've been consulting Chiron."

"Chiron would make it sound less fun."

He put on his coat. She took the scarf his mother had made her and wrapped it around her neck. They went down the lower path toward the shore, where winter had thinned the crowds and the cold kept everyone moving.

The air smelled of water and damp earth. A few patches of snow survived under the trees, grey at the edges. The lake was restless, dark ripples running toward the near bank.

Thalia stopped beside the old wall.

"Tell me something else," she said.

"About my hand?"

"About the music. Something you haven't told me."

He thought for a moment. Then he said, "I don't actually like performing as much as writing."

She looked at him. "You were the one who went to the audition."

"I like playing. I like being good at it. But the part I think about when I'm trying to sleep isn't people applauding. It's the moment a piece finally starts making sense."

She considered. She remembered the night she had found him with the guitar, missing the same transition over and over. She had liked watching him work long before he had finished the song.

"Then why audition?"

"Because if I want to keep writing, I need to understand what other musicians can do with what I make. And because I don't want to spend the rest of my life being afraid to find out."

Thalia turned toward him. "That's a better reason than applause."

He smiled faintly. "I thought you'd say so."

They walked back slowly. The conversation moved toward other things: whether he would have time to visit his mother between performances, what she planned for the next advanced lesson, whether the stubborn piano in the hall was worth repairing or better left as a cautionary tale. Rhys seemed less inclined to stare at his injured hand when he was occupied arguing about the piano.

By the time they reached the cabin, the sun had gone down and the cold had moved into the air.

He opened the door and let her enter first.

"Better?" she asked.

"Somewhat."

"Then I've performed a public service."

"Should I write you a commendation?"

"Please don't. Annabeth would frame it."

He laughed. She turned toward him and reached for his coat. She eased it from his shoulders, careful of the wrapped hand, and let it fall over the chair. The slight physical closeness was enough to change the air between them. She had been thinking about touching him for much of the walk, and now the warmth of the room made it possible to stop pretending she wasn't.

She moved closer. He watched her with an expression that had become familiar: eagerness held in check only by the knowledge that she liked choosing when to cross the last inch herself.

She kissed him.

He answered immediately, his uninjured hand settling at her waist. She caught the back of his collar and drew him nearer, enjoying the warmth of his response and the way he smiled briefly against her mouth. The sore hand remained close to his body. She didn't ask him to perform competence through pain. They had enough other ways to be close.

She drew back and looked at him.

"What?" he asked.

"You're not thinking about the audition."

"Not right this second."

"Good."

He kissed her again, more eager now. She laughed softly and leaned into him until she felt the edge of the table behind her. He stopped before either of them knocked something over, but she was still smiling at the narrow escape.

"Your room is an obstacle course."

"You keep arriving determined to prove it."

She caught his shirt and led him away from the table.

The lamplight caught the side of his face. She wanted to see him like this, warmed by the walk and the kiss, no longer measuring himself against people he'd met for twenty minutes during an audition. She drew him down beside her on the cot. Their knees touched. He pressed a kiss to her cheek, then her neck, and she closed her eyes, content to let the evening slow into something that belonged entirely to them.

After a while she reached to turn down the lamp. He caught her hand with his uninjured one and kissed her knuckles.

"Thank you for getting me outside," he murmured.

"You were becoming unbearable."

"True."

She kissed him again.

The rest of the night passed without guitar music.

***

In the morning Rhys woke to find a note on his workbench.

*Stop practicing with the hand until it heals. The world will survive three days without your genius. I probably won't survive three days of you complaining.*

Below it, in smaller writing, Thalia had added:

*I had a good night. Don't make that the subject of a song.*

Rhys read it twice and laughed aloud.

Thalia was already at the range, setting up her lesson. When he arrived she looked up and saw the expression on his face.

"Don't."

"I haven't said anything."

"You've got the face."

"The note was very kind."

"It was an order."

"Of course."

He moved close enough to lower his voice.

"I had a good night too."

She tried to look unimpressed. The effort failed.

Then she pointed toward the bench where the wrapped hand ought to be resting.

"Sit."

He did, still smiling.

---

## February 20: The Woman Who Fixed the Shelves

Rhys's mother opened the door before they knocked.

She was smaller than Thalia had imagined, with dark hair pulled back from her face and the same patient expression Rhys wore when somebody was explaining a mistake he had already noticed. She looked from her son to Thalia, then to the small bag in Rhys's hand.

"You brought bread."

"You asked."

"I asked whether you were eating properly."

"That's a different question."

His mother ignored him and turned to Thalia.

"You must be Thalia."

"Yes, ma'am."

The word escaped before Thalia could stop it. She had not called anyone ma'am in years without sarcasm.

The woman smiled. "That's more formal than necessary. Come in."

The house was warm and full of small signs of work done carefully. A table stood near the front window; the kitchen shelves had indeed been replaced, and the new ones looked perfectly sensible. There were books piled beneath a chair and a repaired lamp with a cord that had been wound neatly along its base. None of the furniture appeared particularly dangerous.

Thalia was disappointed.

Rhys's mother caught her examining the shelves.

"He told you about those."

"He told me you made him replace all of them."

"Only the ones that were falling down."

"He made it sound like a personal campaign."

"He likes being dramatic."

Thalia smiled before she could help herself.

They sat at the kitchen table. Rhys put the bread down and offered to make tea. His mother waved him away and filled the kettle herself. Thalia watched the familiar argument unfold with amusement. He wanted to be useful. She wanted to demonstrate that he could visit without treating the kitchen as a construction site. It was a disagreement Thalia recognized in several forms from her own life.

While Rhys fetched cups, his mother looked at her.

"How's the heel?"

Thalia blinked.

"He told you about that too."

"He said you were trying to do too much before it was healed."

"An exaggerated account."

"Is it?"

Thalia considered. "Not entirely."

The woman smiled. She didn't ask more. She simply poured the tea and passed it across the table.

They talked about camp for a while. Rhys's mother wanted to know how the winter program worked, whether the cabins had enough heat and what kind of lessons Thalia taught. The questions were specific, not intrusive. She seemed genuinely interested in how the place functioned rather than in any mythical reputation attached to Thalia's name.

That made it easier.

Thalia described her advanced program, explaining the balance between teaching technique and forcing students to make decisions of their own. His mother listened carefully, asked how they prevented dangerous mistakes and smiled when Thalia explained the test with weighted bags instead of arrows.

"That's clever," she said.

"Rhys was responsible for the barrier idea," Thalia admitted.

His mother looked at him. "You had a good idea?"

"Occasionally."

"A rare event."

Thalia laughed.

Rhys looked betrayed. "You're both enjoying this."

"Very much," his mother said.

They ate bread and soup together. Rhys told a story about the audition and the offer that had followed. His mother listened without interrupting, then asked what he planned to do.

"I think I'm going to accept," he said.

Thalia looked at him. He hadn't said it quite so plainly before. They had talked about the terms, the schedule and the practical problems. Now he was saying the decision itself.

His mother nodded.

"Then you should."

"You aren't worried about me traveling?"

"I'll always worry about you traveling. That doesn't mean you shouldn't go."

Rhys glanced at Thalia. She recognized the look: a mixture of surprise and relief, as though he'd been expecting the same argument in two different rooms and had instead received the same sensible answer.

After the meal his mother took Thalia into the kitchen to show her the repaired shelves. Rhys remained at the table gathering the cups.

The woman leaned against the counter.

"He's happier when he talks about you," she said.

Thalia went still.

"I don't mean you have to do anything about that," his mother added. "I just thought you might like to know."

Thalia looked down at the new shelves. The workmanship was sound, the edges carefully sanded, the brackets firmly fixed.

"I like him," she said.

"Good."

"And I don't always know what I'm doing."

His mother laughed softly. "Neither does he."

Thalia looked up. The woman's smile was kind, but not indulgent. She had raised a son who could take care of other people and still had to learn how to let them take care of themselves. Thalia recognized that lesson more intimately than she wanted to admit.

"You don't have to explain yourself to me," the woman said. "He's nineteen. You're eighteen. You can make your own choices."

Thalia nodded, grateful for the lack of interrogation.

They returned to the table. Rhys had finished cleaning the dishes and was attempting to fix a loose hinge on the cupboard door. His mother stood in the doorway and folded her arms.

"Leave that alone."

"It's loose."

"It has been loose for years."

"That's not a defense."

Thalia caught his expression, the same concentration she'd seen at the range and in his cabin. She moved beside him and looked at the hinge.

"Your screw's too short," she said.

"Thank you."

"You're welcome."

His mother groaned. "Now there are two of you."

They laughed and left the hinge alone.

***

On the ride back to camp, Thalia leaned against Rhys's shoulder and watched the last of the town lights fall behind them.

"She likes you," he said.

"I know."

"You're very confident."

"She insulted your cooking. That establishes common ground."

He laughed.

Thalia considered. She had expected to feel examined, perhaps judged against an image he had created through his letters. Instead she had met a woman who was practical, affectionate and perfectly willing to disagree with her son. Thalia had been allowed to arrive as herself, without having to perform some more suitable version.

"She's good," she said.

Rhys looked over. "She is."

"Don't tell her I said so."

"I'll write it in my next letter."

"Traitor."

He took her hand. She let their fingers settle together and smiled into the dark.

At the north cabin they put away their coats and the remaining bread. Rhys turned toward her, looking pleased in that private way she had come to know.

"Thank you for coming," he said.

"I wanted to."

"I know."

She touched his face and kissed him. The kiss was warm and lingering, carrying the relief of a day that had gone well and the familiar desire to be close when nobody else needed them. He drew her nearer, and she leaned into him without hesitation.

Later, sitting together beneath the blanket, Thalia told him which questions she'd expected his mother to ask and which ones she was glad she'd been spared. He laughed at some of her imagined interrogations and took others seriously. Neither tried to turn the visit into a promise of anything grand.

It had been tea, bread, a few honest conversations and a cupboard with a loose hinge. She liked that.

When she finally slept, the house they'd visited seemed less like a separate world Rhys disappeared into and more like a place she might return to, on a day when both of them wanted to go.

---

## February 22: When the Ice Broke

The thaw began beneath the footbridge.

Thalia heard the crack from halfway down the path, a sudden, sharp sound followed by the rush of water finding a channel through the ice. She stopped beside the railing and looked down. A dark strip had opened across the stream, widening as the current worked beneath the frozen edges.

Spring was still several weeks away. She didn't trust the weather to remember that.

Rhys came up behind her carrying a basket of folded cloths from the hall.

"What are you watching?"

"The ice."

"Compelling."

"More than your laundry."

He set the basket beside the railing and leaned over to look. A broken piece of ice turned slowly in the current, hit a stone and vanished beneath the bridge.

"Looks as if it's giving up," he said.

"It'll freeze again tonight."

"Probably."

She glanced at him. His injured hand was unwrapped now. He had spent the last few days complaining less and working more, which was an improvement, though she had caught him reaching for the guitar twice when he was supposed to be resting.

"How's the hand?"

"Better."

"That wasn't an invitation to practice for six hours."

"I hadn't planned to."

"You were thinking about it."

He smiled. "I was thinking about you."

She turned toward him. "That line might work on somebody else."

"It's the truth."

She could feel herself smiling. It annoyed her how efficiently he'd learned to do that. She moved close enough to adjust his collar, which was standing up on one side.

"Better," she said.

"Thank you."

"Don't make a fuss."

He caught her wrist gently before she lowered her hand and kissed the inside of it. The touch was small, almost casual, and entirely effective. She stared at him for a moment.

"You're very pleased with yourself," she said.

"Only because I saw your face."

"Keep walking."

He picked up the basket, and they continued toward the north cabin. The clouds had begun gathering over the ridge, and the wind carried damp air instead of the dry cold of the last few weeks. Every path smelled of thawing earth. Thalia found herself looking forward to the warmer weather, though it would mean more work outdoors and the return of campers who preferred training beneath open sky.

At the cabin Rhys set the basket down and went to light the stove. She took off her coat. There was a folded letter on the table beside a new score, and the old wooden box she'd made him sat open with his pencils arranged in an orderly row. She noticed that he'd begun using it, and felt a quiet satisfaction he had no chance of seeing.

He returned with two cups of hot water and a small tin of tea.

"Not cider?" she asked.

"I thought you'd eventually tire of it."

"An unreasonable assumption."

"I'll make a note."

She sat on the cot, waiting until he'd poured the water before speaking again.

"Are you nervous?"

"About?"

"The six weeks."

He looked at her over the rim of his cup. "A little."

"Still?"

"It's getting closer."

She nodded. She had the same awareness of approaching time, a private count she hadn't admitted to anyone. But she didn't want to spend tonight rehearsing his absence. She wanted a room with him in it, a warm drink and the pleasure of being alone together after a morning spent answering questions from everybody else.

She took the cup from his hand and set it beside hers.

"What are you doing?" he asked.

"Saving you from terrible tea."

"I hadn't even tried it."

"Then I've intervened in time."

She stood and drew him close.

He kissed her immediately, smiling into it, and she felt a familiar rush of happiness at how readily he met her. The kiss wasn't hurried. She had been thinking about him all afternoon, about the way he'd leaned over the footbridge and how his injured hand had rested against the railing. Now she could touch him without pretending there was a practical reason.

He slipped his arm around her waist and kissed her again. She caught his collar and pulled him nearer, enjoying the immediate warmth of him and the small sound of amusement he made when they nearly collided with the table.

"You're in the way," she murmured.

"It's my cabin."

"That isn't a defense."

He laughed, then kissed her cheek and lingered there. She closed her eyes for a moment, letting the anticipation settle instead of forcing it toward an outcome. Their relationship had acquired so many ordinary routines that she sometimes forgot how quickly a familiar touch could make the rest of the world seem unimportant.

She liked that she could be both surprised and entirely certain.

At last he moved away to lower the stove's draft. She followed him with her eyes, aware that she could reach for him again and that he would probably come. The fact that she could choose was more satisfying than demanding it.

He turned back toward her.

"What's that look?"

"Nothing."

"That's a remarkably cheerful nothing."

She smiled. "Come here."

He crossed the room at once.

She caught his face between her hands and kissed him until he was smiling too. The fading light made the cabin warmer in appearance than it really was; the lamp was still unlit, and a pale strip of afternoon lay across the floor. She liked seeing him in that light, without the lamplight that had made their first few nights seem almost theatrical.

He moved with her toward the cot. She sat, drew him down beside her and kept one hand against his chest. He was laughing softly when he settled, because she'd managed to pull the blanket from beneath them in the process and half the contents of his pocket had fallen onto the floor.

"You've destroyed my organization," he said.

"You had organization?"

"Briefly."

She leaned over to retrieve a pencil, then stopped when he caught her hand and drew her gently back toward him. Her smile returned before the kiss began.

For a while they stayed close, their warmth and laughter gradually becoming quieter. She was aware of the soft fabric of his shirt beneath her fingers, his cheek against hers, the way his breathing changed when she kissed him and then waited for the next movement. There was no need to turn each touch into a discussion. They knew one another well enough to recognize eagerness, hesitation and amusement without translating every feeling into words.

The room grew darker.

At last she reached behind him and turned the lamp down before he could even think of lighting it. He laughed and drew her close again. The remaining daylight faded slowly along the window.

The afternoon became theirs.

***

By evening the thaw had turned to rain.

Thalia lay beneath the quilt listening to it strike the window while Rhys searched for the pencil she'd knocked off the cot. He had found three others, an old quiver strap and a button that belonged to nobody he could identify. The missing pencil had apparently vanished beneath the floorboards.

"Your cabin is eating stationery," she said.

"It's very hungry."

"Feed it the terrible music you wrote last week."

"Unkind."

"Accurate."

He emerged from beneath the cot with dust in his hair and no pencil. She laughed. He looked at her, then at the dust on his sleeve.

"You're enjoying this."

"Enormously."

He sat beside her. She reached to brush the dust from his hair, and he caught her hand long enough to kiss her fingers. There was something intimate in the ordinary gesture that she had not expected from a man she had already spent an entire afternoon kissing.

"Do you have to go?" he asked.

"Eventually."

"I was hoping you'd stay."

She considered. The weather had worsened, and the path would be slippery. She could return to Cabin One safely if she wanted. She also had no particular reason to leave before morning. Annabeth knew where she was. The next day's lesson wasn't early.

"I'll stay," she said.

His smile was immediate, almost boyish. She liked seeing it, and she liked that he'd asked without making her decision for her.

They ate bread and cheese at the table. Rhys told her about a musician he'd met at the audition, someone who had spent ten years moving between towns and still talked about every concert as if it were the first. Thalia asked what that life looked like outside the performances. Rhys admitted he wasn't sure. There were travel days, rehearsals, unfamiliar rooms, meals eaten at odd hours and the complicated business of being away from the people one wanted to see.

"Does it appeal to you?" she asked.

"Some parts."

"Which?"

"The work. The chance to hear new people. The idea of finding out what I can do."

"And which parts don't?"

He looked at her. "Leaving here."

She nodded. There was no point pretending she wasn't part of the answer.

She reached across the table and took his hand. He held on, and for a few moments they sat without discussing how to solve the problem.

Outside, the rain slowed. The ice in the stream would keep breaking, making the paths muddy and uncertain. Spring was approaching whether the camp was ready or not.

Thalia looked at him. He was warm from the stove, with a little dust still caught at the edge of his hair, and she wanted to be close again.

"Come to bed," she said.

He smiled, rose and followed her.

The room remained untidy. The pencil was still missing. Neither of them cared.

---

## February 23: The Conversation They Didn't Have

Thalia knew she wanted him the moment she saw him standing in the doorway.

It was not a complicated realization. She had spent the afternoon teaching, had missed supper because a student wanted to review the new course, and had arrived at Rhys's cabin hungry, cold and impatient with the world. He opened the door wearing an old shirt with the sleeves pushed to his elbows. There was a smudge of ink on his thumb and an unfinished score on the table behind him.

"You're late," he said.

"I was busy."

"An excellent excuse."

She looked at him for a moment, then stepped close and kissed him.

He responded with such easy pleasure that she felt the exhaustion of the day loosen almost at once. His hands settled at her waist, warm through the fabric of her coat. She caught his face between her fingers and kissed him again before he could say anything else.

He laughed softly.

"Hello to you too."

"Hello."

"Bad day?"

"Not particularly."

"Good day?"

"Long day."

He brushed a strand of hair from her cheek. She liked the small familiarity of the gesture, the way he'd learned that she would lean into it even while pretending to object to his fussing. She stayed close enough that he didn't mistake her answer for a request to be left alone.

"Do you want food?" he asked.

"Eventually."

He smiled. "I know that answer."

She kissed him again.

The room seemed warmer than it had when she arrived. The stove was lit, the shutters closed and the workbench cleared enough that nobody would have to move a pile of bowstrings before sitting down. She knew the location of every object. More importantly, she knew the person standing in front of her, the way his expression changed when he wanted to tease her, the sudden quiet when he wanted to be serious and the familiar eagerness that met her now.

She took off her coat and hung it beside his.

He waited, watching her.

"Stop looking so pleased with yourself," she said.

"I'm pleased with you."

"That's worse."

"Is it?"

She smiled and reached for him.

There were days when she liked the work of making their relationship clear: the conversations about leaving, the disagreements over privacy, the practical compromises that allowed them both to remain themselves. Tonight she wanted the other part. She wanted the room, the quiet and his company without a speech explaining why. He seemed to understand. When she kissed him, he answered with the same warmth, letting their shared familiarity do what words would only slow down.

They moved toward the cot, laughing when her boot caught the corner of a folded blanket. She kicked the boot free and caught his sleeve, pulling him closer. The small accident didn't interrupt anything. They had stopped expecting intimacy to look elegant.

The lamp cast a narrow strip of gold across the wall. Thalia liked being able to see his face while they were close. She reached for the wick but changed her mind and left the light where it was. He noticed, smiled and came nearer.

There was no question waiting to be answered. Their desire was plain in the way they sought each other, in the easy movement of his hand against her back and the way she held him close when the kiss might otherwise have ended. She could change her mind about any moment. So could he. Tonight neither wanted to.

The night grew quiet around them.

***

Later, the room had cooled and the food was still untouched.

Thalia lay against his shoulder beneath the blanket, watching the lamp burn lower. He had one hand resting over hers, and she was tracing the faded line of an old scar on his finger with the absent concentration of somebody who enjoyed having him close.

"You're staring at my hand," he said.

"I'm inspecting your workmanship."

"On my own hand?"

"Poorly maintained."

He laughed. The sound was low and tired, and she liked how close it was.

"Do you know what I like?" she asked.

"I'm afraid to guess."

"That we didn't have to talk about anything tonight."

He looked at her. "We talked."

"We argued about whether I was late."

"A serious subject."

"And whether your hand required repair."

"An ongoing concern."

She smiled. "You know what I mean."

He did. She could see it.

They had been together long enough to know where the important boundaries lay. They didn't need to construct a formal agreement every time she arrived wanting him. They could read the excitement in each other's faces and the willingness in the way they moved close. They could also stop, laugh, go to sleep or change course without making the evening a referendum on the entire relationship.

Rhys touched her cheek.

"I liked tonight too," he said.

She leaned toward him and kissed him gently, a quiet answer that required no further explanation.

Then she remembered the food.

"I'm starving."

He groaned. "You could have mentioned that earlier."

"I had other priorities."

"Apparently."

She climbed out of bed, found the loaf he'd left on the table and returned with a piece for each of them. They ate sitting beneath the blanket, dropping crumbs and accusing each other of taking the larger portion. The argument was ridiculous and familiar. She liked that part almost as much as the earlier silence.

Outside the wind moved through the pines. The cabin held its warmth. She had a program to run in the morning; Rhys had music to finish. They would wake, dress and go about their separate work. Tonight they had spent a few hours alone together because they wanted to, and neither had tried to make that wanting into something burdensome.

Thalia rested her head against his shoulder.

"Don't get used to me being this pleasant," she said.

"I wouldn't dream of it."

She smiled into the blanket and closed her eyes.

---

## February 24: The Letter He Signed

The ten days were almost up.

Rhys had written three versions of his answer to the ensemble and left all three on the table. Each was competent. Each contained a slightly different excuse for why he hadn't already written yes.

Thalia found him looking at the pages with an expression she'd learned to recognize.

"You're avoiding it."

"I'm deciding."

"You've decided."

He looked up, irritated. "You don't know that."

She stopped in the doorway.

He was right. She had spent the last week talking about what she thought he ought to do, reading the terms, identifying the clauses that needed clarification and considering how the tour might fit around their lives. But she could not make the choice for him, and she had no right to treat a decision he had to live with as a formality.

"You're right," she said.

He looked startled.

"Please put that in writing."

"Don't get greedy."

She took off her coat and sat on the edge of the cot. He returned to the papers, but after a moment he pushed them aside.

"I'm afraid of accepting," he said.

"I know."

"I want the work. I want to see if I can do it. But I don't want to come home and find I've made a mistake about everything else."

Thalia looked at him. The word *home* caught her attention, though she decided not to make it into a subject yet.

"You'll make mistakes," she said.

"Thank you."

"I will too. The students will. Chiron will. Your mother will rearrange another kitchen and probably blame you."

He laughed reluctantly.

"The question isn't whether you'll be wrong about something," she continued. "It's whether the work is worth trying."

He sat still.

"Is it?" she asked.

He looked at the three letters. Then he folded the first two, put them aside and reached for the third.

"Yes," he said.

Thalia watched him sign it.

There was no great ceremony. His hand moved across the paper in the same slanted handwriting she'd come to recognize from the letters he sent her in November. He wrote his name, checked the address and folded the page into an envelope.

When he finished, he looked at her.

"That's it."

She smiled. "You did it."

He looked pleased and a little frightened.

She stood, crossed the small space between them and kissed him.

It was not a kiss meant to convince him he'd chosen correctly. She didn't know that. Neither did he. It was a kiss because he'd made a difficult decision honestly, and because she was proud of him for taking responsibility for what he wanted.

He caught her close, his hands warm against her back, and she felt his answering enthusiasm with such certainty that she laughed against his mouth.

"What?" he asked.

"Nothing."

"That's a dangerous nothing."

"I'm pleased with you."

He looked at her for a moment.

"That may be the nicest thing you've ever said."

"Don't make me repeat it."

He kissed her again.

***

They mailed the letter before supper.

Thalia insisted on accompanying him because she wanted to witness the final act, and he insisted she was behaving like a supervisor auditing a delivery. Both statements were true.

At the outgoing-mail box he held the envelope for one last second, then slipped it inside.

"There," he said.

"Now you can't take it back."

"Not without committing a fairly serious offense against the mail service."

"Don't be dramatic."

They walked back toward the pavilion hand in hand.

The camp was settling into its evening routine. Smoke rose from the chimneys, and the west path lanterns had begun to light. A few younger campers passed them carrying wood, arguing about whose turn it was to stack the last pieces. Thalia recognized the conversation. She had spent a good part of her childhood having versions of it with Annabeth, learning how to make a household work when nobody wanted to do the dull jobs.

Rhys squeezed her hand.

"What are you thinking?"

"That we should eat."

"An unusually practical answer."

"I've had a long day."

He looked toward the dining pavilion.

"Come by after?"

She considered. "I have the final advanced-session report to finish."

"Then finish it."

"I could come late."

"I'd like that."

She looked at him. The invitation was simple. They had made enough decisions for one afternoon; there was no need to turn tonight into an attempt to solve the entire spring.

"I'll come," she said.

His smile warmed.

***

She arrived after nine, carrying her completed report in a canvas case.

Rhys opened the door, looked at the case and groaned.

"You brought the report."

"It's finished."

"Then why are you carrying it?"

"Because I wanted to show you the final numbers."

He leaned against the doorframe.

"You're unbelievable."

"Frequently."

She held out the report. He took it, set it on the table without opening it and stepped toward her.

"Tomorrow," he said.

She smiled. "Tomorrow."

He kissed her, and she moved close at once. The day had been full of choices that required careful thought; this one required none. She had wanted him since she watched him sign the letter, and he was meeting her with the same unmistakable desire. His hand settled at the back of her neck; hers found the familiar line of his shoulder. She liked how naturally they moved together now, without either having to turn each touch into a question.

She took off her coat. He hung it on the hook. When he came back, she was waiting with a smile he recognized well enough to answer before she said anything.

"Very patient," he murmured.

"Not especially."

She kissed him again.

The room was warm, the stove low and the workbench clear. They had been planning an evening for themselves all week without admitting it, and now that they were here, the rest of the world could wait a little.

He drew her toward the cot. She followed, laughing when he stepped on the edge of the blanket and nearly pulled it onto the floor. He caught it, and she caught him, and for a moment neither could kiss the other because they were laughing too hard.

Then the laughter settled.

Thalia touched his cheek and looked at him. There was no performance in his face, only pleasure and the familiar trust they'd learned through months of imperfect evenings together. She wanted him. He wanted her. They stayed close as the lamp went low.

The rest of the night was private.

***

In the morning, Thalia woke to find her report open on the table.

Rhys was sitting beside it with a pencil in his hand. He had already made three notes and was studying the section on student evaluation with the kind of attention that made her like him in the first place.

She lay still for a moment, watching him.

He looked up.

"Morning."

"You're reading my report."

"You brought it."

"I told you to read it tomorrow."

"It is tomorrow."

She groaned. He smiled, pleased with himself.

"Any errors?" she asked.

"One."

"Where?"

He pointed at the page.

She sat up, pulled the blanket around her shoulders and came over to look. The mistake was small but real: a percentage had been calculated using the wrong group of students. She stared at it, then at him.

"You're enjoying this."

"Immensely."

She took the pencil, corrected the number and put it down.

"Better?"

"Much."

He leaned closer and kissed her cheek.

"Your program is good," he said.

She looked at the report. The students had learned something. The design was working. She had made changes when evidence showed she needed to, and she had protected the parts that mattered when other instructors had wanted to simplify everything into a competition.

"So is your music," she said.

He smiled.

They had both chosen work they cared about. They would both be busy. They would both leave sometimes. That was not a defect in the relationship.

Thalia rested her hand against his and leaned in to kiss him once more before the day began.

---

## February 26: A Spring That Belonged to Both of Them

Chiron asked to see them together on Monday.

Thalia disliked the phrasing of the invitation. It sounded as though they had broken a rule or left something important unfinished. She arrived at the office with her advanced-program notes under one arm and Rhys beside her, looking annoyingly calm.

Chiron gestured toward two chairs.

"Sit."

"We haven't broken anything," Thalia said.

"I hadn't accused you of doing so."

"Good."

Rhys coughed into his hand. She gave him a look.

Chiron unfolded a schedule. He wanted to discuss the spring instructor rotation. Thalia's advanced program had been approved for a longer run, with more students and an assistant instructor assigned to each group. Rhys's winter teaching duties could be transferred before he left on tour, but the handoff would need careful planning.

Thalia listened as Chiron laid out the dates. Her program would grow at the same moment Rhys began his rehearsals. They would both be occupied. Neither would have much time for anything beyond work in the first week of April.

"That's manageable," she said.

"It is," Chiron replied. "If you make your decisions separately and coordinate only the parts that overlap."

Rhys glanced at her.

She looked at him. "Don't."

"I haven't said anything."

"You have the expression."

Chiron continued as though neither had spoken.

The meeting lasted an hour. By the end, Thalia had an assistant to train, Rhys had a list of duties to transfer, and both had a clear view of what would happen before April. It was not romantic. It was useful.

Outside the office, Rhys stopped beneath the covered walkway.

"That went well."

"Chiron is very good at making the future look like a table."

"Sometimes a table helps."

"Sometimes a table becomes an excuse to make decisions for people."

He studied her. "You're still annoyed."

"Not at him."

"At me?"

She considered. "A little."

"For taking the tour?"

"No."

"For leaving?"

"That's part of it."

He waited. She knew he could endure an uncomfortable silence better than most people, and she wasn't going to let him win an argument simply by standing still.

"I'm angry because I have a good thing happening," she said. "My program is growing. You're doing something you've wanted for years. And instead of being pleased all the time, I keep thinking about how little of spring we'll spend together."

He nodded.

"That's not unreasonable."

"I know."

"You don't have to be pleased all the time."

She looked up. There it was again, the permission she didn't need but liked hearing anyway.

"I hate missing you," she said.

"I know."

"And I want you to go."

"I know that too."

She folded her notes under her arm. "Then we're both going to have a difficult spring."

"Probably."

He smiled.

She wanted to kiss him. The impulse was immediate and familiar, and she was tired of pretending it required explanation. She caught his collar and kissed him beneath the walkway, not caring whether anybody passed at that moment. He met her eagerly, drawing her closer for a second kiss when she smiled against his mouth.

"Better?" he murmured.

"Considerably."

They walked down to the range together.

***

That afternoon Thalia began training her assistant.

The younger instructor was capable but nervous, eager to prove she deserved the position. Thalia recognized the feeling and, for once, resisted the impulse to correct every small mistake immediately. She let the woman run the first demonstration, watched the students respond and waited until the end to offer feedback.

The session went well.

At supper she found Rhys at the far end of the pavilion, surrounded by music sheets and a stack of schedules. He looked as occupied as she had been earlier. She could have gone over and demanded his attention. Instead she took her food to the next table, ate, and left him to finish.

Half an hour later he came to find her.

"You're avoiding me."

"I'm letting you work."

"A shocking change."

"I've had a long day."

He sat beside her. "How was the assistant?"

"Good. Nervous. Better than she thinks."

He smiled. "Sounds familiar."

She looked at him. "Don't start."

"I'm not."

They talked about the day's work for a while, comparing the difficulties of teaching students to make decisions and teaching adults to take over responsibilities without feeling that the previous person had abandoned them. It was a useful conversation. It was also one neither could have had with quite the same understanding several months earlier.

By the time supper ended, Thalia felt less angry about spring.

They weren't losing the weeks before it. They were preparing to do work they cared about. The relationship would have to fit around those responsibilities, not replace them.

At the north path Rhys took her hand.

"Come home?"

She smiled. "Yes."

They walked together as the lanterns came on one by one.

Inside the cabin he closed the door, set down his papers and turned toward her. She was already moving closer. Their kiss was eager and familiar, a private pleasure after a day spent negotiating practical things in public. She caught his shoulders; he drew her against him, and for a while they let the planning stay on the table where they'd left it.

Later the room grew quiet. She lay beside him beneath the blanket, listening to the stove, and realized she felt something she hadn't expected: pride. They were both growing into different responsibilities, and neither had asked the other to remain smaller in order to make the relationship easier.

She touched his hand.

"April is going to be terrible," she said.

He laughed softly. "Parts of it."

"Write that down."

"Very formal."

"I'm making a record."

He kissed her cheek.

"We'll manage what we can," he said.

She nodded. It wasn't a guarantee. It was enough.

---

## March 1: The Room with the Closed Curtains

Thalia had spent all day wanting the evening.

It wasn't a response to disaster. The advanced lesson had gone well. Chiron had watched the students assess their practice lanes and had asked only two questions, both useful. Annabeth had finally stopped criticizing the new safety screens. The weather was dry, if not warm. Everything had moved more or less as she'd intended.

She wanted Rhys anyway.

At six she found him in the hall returning a stack of instruments to the cupboard. He had a guitar case in one hand and a roll of paper under the other arm. Two musicians were talking to him about rehearsal changes. He listened, made a note, and nodded as one left.

He looked up and saw her.

His expression changed.

She liked how little he tried to conceal it now.

"Grace."

"Calder."

He put down the case. "Good day?"

"Good enough."

"That's unusually generous."

She glanced toward the departing musicians. "Are you finished?"

"With the hall? Almost."

"With the arguing?"

"Never."

"Good."

She crossed to him and took the roll of paper from his arm. It was a rough score, covered in penciled amendments. She had no intention of helping him with it tonight.

"Put this away," she said.

He smiled slowly. "Is that an order?"

"A suggestion from a concerned citizen."

"And then?"

She stepped closer. Her fingers caught the open edge of his collar.

"Then you come with me."

He laughed softly, but there was nothing amused about how quickly he closed the cupboard and picked up his coat. She watched his hands, the injured one moving freely now, and felt a flash of satisfaction at the change. He had listened. He had rested. Now he was here, entirely himself, following her out of the hall because he wanted to see where she intended to lead him.

They walked by the lower path. The sky was violet at the edges, and lamps had begun turning the gravel gold. Neither felt inclined to discuss the next training session or the exact order of the coming performances. There would be time for practical questions in the morning.

At the north cabin he opened the door.

Thalia stepped inside and turned immediately to face him.

"You're staring," he said.

"I've been considering a problem."

"Should I be frightened?"

"Quite possibly."

He closed the door. She took his coat from his shoulders and hung it beside hers, then moved close again. He was smiling now, anticipating her. She found the anticipation almost as satisfying as the kiss itself.

"You've had that look since the hall," he murmured.

"What look?"

"The one that makes me forget what I was saying."

She caught his shirtfront and kissed him.

He met her with unmistakable eagerness, one hand at her waist and the other slipping into her hair. She had missed this, despite seeing him yesterday and the day before: the sudden privacy of being able to reach for him without the camp surrounding them, the way his voice fell quiet when she kissed him, the pleasure of feeling him turn completely toward her.

He drew back only far enough to smile.

"You planned this."

"No."

"Thalia."

She considered pretending otherwise, then surrendered the point.

"All afternoon."

He laughed, delighted. She kissed him again to stop the commentary.

The room was warm from a fire someone had banked earlier. The curtains were still open; beyond them she could see the darkening path and the first lanterns beginning to glow. She moved toward the window and pulled the curtains shut while Rhys watched her from beside the cot.

When she turned, he was smiling in a way that made her breath catch.

"Very subtle," he said.

"I have no interest in subtlety tonight."

His smile changed.

She came back to him, rested her palms against his chest and kissed him slowly. His hands moved around her waist, drawing her nearer until she could feel the warmth of him through their clothes. There was nothing uncertain about the answer in his movements. He was as pleased to be here as she was, and neither had to explain why.

Thalia remembered the first Monday when she had stood outside the cabin, embarrassed by the depth of her wanting. That girl had been certain affection must be negotiated like a contested border. Tonight she simply wanted him, and she could say so with the way she reached for him.

He brushed his lips against her cheek and lingered near her neck. She tipped her head toward him, eyes closed for a moment, enjoying the warmth of his breath and the light touch that followed. When he drew back she caught him again and kissed him harder, laughing when he stumbled against the cot.

"You're losing your balance."

"You're making that difficult."

"An unfortunate weakness."

He sat, pulling her toward him. She settled beside him, comfortably careful of the angle of her leg, and kissed him again. His hand rested against her back. The lamp had not yet been lit, and the closed curtains turned the room into a softer version of itself, without the bright edges of daylight.

For a while the only sounds were the ticking stove and their quiet laughter.

When she reached to loosen his collar, he smiled against her mouth. It was a small, familiar gesture now, a way of making clear that she intended to stay rather than counting the hours until she had to leave. He responded by drawing her close again, and she could feel her own smile fading as the kiss became something more absorbing.

She was aware of his heartbeat beneath her hand. She was aware of the cool air at her back where her shirt had shifted. More than either, she was aware of how entirely natural it felt to want this, to be wanted in return, and to trust that either of them could change the pace without spoiling the evening.

Rhys drew back and rested his forehead against hers.

"Good enough day?" he asked softly.

"Improving."

He laughed.

She kissed him once more, then reached for the lamp. The wick made a small gold flare before settling to a dim light. She didn't bother turning it higher.

The rest of the evening belonged to them.

***

Much later, Thalia was lying beneath the quilt when she heard the first rain strike the shutters.

She opened her eyes. Rhys was beside her, one arm flung across the pillow, looking entirely satisfied with the decision to abandon the day's work when she arrived.

"Weather," she said.

He looked toward the closed curtains. "Yes."

"You're not going to make a prediction?"

"Not unless you ask."

"Excellent. A man who can be trained."

He laughed and moved closer. She rested her head against his shoulder. The quiet afterward felt different from the urgent anticipation of the hall, but no less intimate. She liked his warmth beside her and the fact that they could talk without the conversation having to become a review of what the evening meant.

After a while she said, "I had a good lesson."

"I heard."

"From whom?"

"Chiron."

"That man needs new hobbies."

"He seemed proud of you."

She looked at him. The compliment had been passed on by someone she respected. It mattered, though she wasn't sure how to accept it without making a joke.

"The students were good," she said.

"And their instructor?"

"Extremely demanding."

"I've heard that."

She smiled.

He touched her hair. "I'm glad the program's yours."

She raised her head. "What does that mean?"

"That you've made something you care about, something that doesn't need me to be important."

She considered. The answer was simple, but it carried an understanding of what she had spent months trying to explain. She could love him and still have work that belonged to her. He could leave to play music and still come back to someone whose life had continued in his absence. Neither had to apologize for being whole.

"I like that you have the music," she said.

He smiled. "Even when it takes me away?"

"Especially then, sometimes. I hate the leaving. I don't hate the reason."

She kissed his cheek, then settled beside him again.

Outside, the rain grew heavier. The room was warm, and neither of them needed to get up until morning. She considered reopening the curtains to watch the path, then decided against it. The world could continue without their supervision for a few more hours.

Rhys drew the blanket higher.

"Stay?" he murmured.

She looked up at him, smiling.

"I closed the curtains," she said.

He laughed, and she kissed him again.

---

## Sunday, March 3: The Afternoon She Chose Again

The first warm afternoon in March arrived without warning.

Thalia had been expecting another day of grey sky and wet grass. Instead the clouds lifted shortly after noon, leaving the north path bright and nearly dry. The lake reflected a strip of clear blue, and the air carried the faint smell of soil beginning to thaw.

She stood outside Cabin One with a book under one arm and considered what she wanted to do with the afternoon.

There were things she could work on. The advanced program needed another review, and the assistant instructor had left three questions in the margin of the latest plan. Annabeth had asked whether she could check the measurements near the east wall. Chiron would be pleased if Thalia spent an hour with the winter equipment list.

None was urgent.

She put the book away and went north.

The path was familiar. She knew every bend, every uneven stone and the lantern bracket that rattled when the wind came off the lake. It had been months since the first Monday evening when she'd walked this way in Percy's oversized coat, furious at her aching heel and at the fact that she wanted someone enough to risk looking foolish.

The heel no longer troubled her. The wanting remained.

She smiled as she climbed the last rise.

Rhys was at the workbench, his sleeves rolled, working on a piece of music. The door stood partly open to let in the warm air. Through the gap she could see him bent over the score, a pencil between his fingers and his hair falling across his forehead.

She stopped for a moment and watched.

He hadn't changed into a different person since the first Sunday afternoon. He still lost pencils, still left unfinished work on every available surface, still looked absurdly pleased when a piece of music finally behaved as he wanted. She had learned the shape of his concentration, the brief frown before he crossed out a note and the smile that came when he found a better answer.

She wanted to interrupt him.

She went in without knocking.

Rhys looked up.

"You're here."

She smiled. "I'm here."

"It's the middle of the day."

"Very good observation."

He looked toward the window, then at her face.

"Is something wrong?"

"Not at all."

She crossed the room, took the pencil from his fingers and put it safely on the table.

"Then what brings you here?"

"You."

He blinked, then smiled.

She kissed him.

The kiss was immediate and eager, and she could feel him moving toward her before she had quite finished closing the distance. There was no awkwardness in it now, no need to invent a professional reason for crossing the threshold. She wanted him, and he was delighted that she'd come.

His hands rested at her waist. She drew him closer, smiling when he tried to say something and found her mouth again instead.

The old room had not grown larger. The workbench was still too close to the table, and the cot still complained when someone sat on its edge. But she knew how to move through it now, where to put her coat and where the floor creaked. She liked the familiarity as much as the anticipation.

When they drew apart, Rhys was laughing softly.

"That was unexpected."

"Was it?"

"I was supposed to finish the score."

"You can finish it later."

"You're very demanding."

"I've been told."

She kissed him again, and the argument ended.

The afternoon moved slowly. Outside, the sun settled into the grass and made the last patches of frost shine along the fence. Inside, the cabin warmed enough that they opened the upper window. They talked when they wanted to, teased each other about the unfinished music and enjoyed the quiet when conversation became unnecessary.

Thalia liked how easy the intimacy had become without losing its intensity. She could still surprise him, still feel the sudden pleasure of seeing his expression change when she touched his face or caught his hand. She could also rest against him afterward, no performance required, and know that he was pleased she stayed.

At some point Rhys reached for the lamp, though it was still daylight.

"Leave it," she said.

He looked at her.

"Why?"

"Because I like seeing you."

His smile softened.

She caught his hand and drew him back toward her.

The rest of the afternoon belonged to them.

***

Later they sat on the floor with their backs against the cot, eating the bread Thalia had found in his cupboard. The sunlight had moved across the boards, leaving a bright square near the doorway.

Rhys looked at the empty plate.

"You know, you've been here for four hours."

"Your counting skills are improving."

"You haven't looked at the clock once."

"That's because I don't want to."

He smiled. She rested her head against his shoulder.

"I was thinking about the first Sunday," she said.

"November?"

"Yes."

"You arrived without knocking."

"I did today."

"Some things never change."

She looked at him. "Some things have."

He considered. "Yes."

They sat quietly for a while.

Thalia thought about the months between those two afternoons. The letters he'd sent from home. The first time she'd held his hand in public without wanting to look over her shoulder. The argument about the photograph, the concert, the difficult choices they had begun to make about spring. None of it had made the relationship easy. It had made it theirs.

"You know what I like?" she asked.

"Your own opinions?"

"Obviously."

He laughed.

"I like that I can be here all afternoon and it doesn't feel as if I've neglected the rest of my life."

Rhys looked at her. "You haven't."

"I know. That's new."

He took her hand.

She leaned over and kissed him, slowly enough that the small sound of the stove fading behind them became noticeable. The kiss was warm, comfortable and still capable of making her want to stay another hour.

When they drew apart, the sun was beginning to lower.

"Are you staying for supper?" he asked.

She considered.

"Yes."

He smiled. "Good."

"But I'm cooking."

He looked alarmed. "Should I be concerned?"

"Very."

She laughed and stood, pulling him up beside her.

They found enough food for a meal and began arguing about how to prepare it. The argument was unimportant. That was why she enjoyed it. No one was trying to prove who cared more or who would leave first. They were two people who wanted to spend an evening together, working out how to make supper in a cabin with a very small kitchen.

The warmth outside would not last. Spring would bring new work, travel and the possibility of more distance. Thalia knew that. She also knew she would choose another afternoon with him when she could, not because she owed him one, but because she wanted it.

For now, they had supper to make.

---

## March 7: A Place on the Calendar

Thalia had spent years believing that a schedule was a weapon against chaos.

She liked lists. She liked being able to point to a date and explain exactly what needed to happen before it arrived. She liked the sharp satisfaction of crossing out a completed task. When people made promises without thinking about the hours required to keep them, she had a habit of handing them a pencil and telling them to begin again.

The problem with Rhys's spring tour was that it didn't fit neatly onto the same page as her advanced program.

She had made three versions of the calendar. In one, she could visit him for a weekend halfway through the run. In another, the visit moved to a later performance but required her assistant to cover two lessons. In the third, she abandoned the idea entirely and told herself the six weeks would pass quickly if she stayed busy.

She hated the third version.

Annabeth found the pages spread across the table in Cabin One.

"Are you planning an invasion?"

"A trip."

"Same expression."

Thalia looked up. Her sister was standing beside the window with a small bundle of drawings under one arm.

"Would you mind looking at this?" Thalia asked.

Annabeth set down the drawings and came over. She studied the calendar for a moment, then pointed to the second option.

"You've marked two days away."

"Yes."

"But the assistant is already leading one of those lessons."

"Yes."

"Then what's the problem?"

"I don't know whether the rest of the schedule will be stable enough by then."

Annabeth considered.

"You're planning for a problem that hasn't happened."

"That's how planning works."

"Sometimes."

Thalia stared at her. Annabeth smiled.

"You don't have to decide the whole spring today," she said.

It was precisely the kind of advice that would have irritated Thalia from anyone else. From her sister it was irritating and useful.

She folded the calendars and took them to Rhys that evening.

He was at the workbench, packing a case of strings and spare parts he intended to take on tour. The sight of him preparing to leave still caused a small ache, but it no longer felt like a warning of catastrophe.

"I have a proposition," Thalia said.

He looked up. "How dangerous?"

"Moderately."

She unfolded the second calendar and put it on the table.

"There is a weekend in the middle of your tour when I could visit, if the program stays on schedule."

Rhys read the dates.

"You're serious."

"Unfortunately."

He looked at her. There was unmistakable pleasure in his face.

"I'd like that."

"So would I."

She hadn't expected the admission to come so easily. She had spent years turning desire into an argument because arguments were easier to manage than requests. Now she could place a date in front of him and say she wanted to be there without pretending the calendar was anything other than a way to make the wanting possible.

Rhys considered the rest of the page.

"What if your assistant needs you?"

"Then I stay."

"And if the performance moves?"

"Then we choose another day."

He nodded. "All right."

She looked at him. "You're suspiciously relaxed about this."

"I'm trying not to make the plan more complicated than it is."

"An unfamiliar habit."

He smiled. She reached for his hand, and he drew her closer before she could find another objection.

Their kiss was brief at first. When she smiled against his mouth, he kissed her again. She liked the simple physical answer, the way they could talk through a practical problem and then enjoy being close without requiring the problem to vanish completely.

He set the calendar aside and put both hands at her waist.

"Are you staying?"

"Yes."

His smile widened.

She laughed. "Don't look so pleased."

"I was hoping you would."

She kissed him again and let the evening become private.

***

Later they lay beneath the quilt with the calendar folded on the bedside shelf.

Thalia turned toward him.

"I used to think planning meant making sure nothing could surprise me."

"And now?"

"Now I think it means knowing which surprises are worth the trouble."

Rhys considered. "That's a very generous opinion of yourself."

She shoved his shoulder lightly.

He laughed, caught her hand and kissed it.

She rested beside him for a while, listening to the stove. The six-week tour was still ahead. She would miss him, and there would probably be days when she resented the distance. But she'd begun to understand that she could make room for something she wanted without allowing it to consume everything else.

The calendar had a possible weekend marked in pencil.

It was enough for tonight.

---

## March 12: The Empty Seat

The advanced program's first public demonstration went well.

Thalia had spent weeks designing the course, training the assistant instructors and persuading everyone involved that the students would learn more from explaining their decisions than from being told which target to hit. The morning finally arrived with clear skies and a range full of nervous young archers.

She was pleased with the result.

The students managed their own safety briefing, inspected the lanes and identified two deliberate errors before anyone handled a live bow. They then completed the shooting course without incident. The final group made a mistake with the wind marker, corrected it, and explained the correction clearly enough that Chiron nodded with approval.

Thalia stood near the fence watching them, feeling a private satisfaction she had not expected to be so strong. The program was hers. The work had succeeded because she'd made decisions, accepted criticism and kept changing the parts that did not work.

When the demonstration ended, she looked toward the benches.

Rhys wasn't there.

She had told him about the event. He'd said he wanted to come. She knew he had rehearsals, but the final schedule had seemed to leave enough time. She searched the crowd once, then stopped, annoyed with herself for doing it when she ought to be congratulating her students.

The rest of the morning passed in a blur of reports, equipment checks and well-earned praise. She answered the questions Chiron asked and gave her assistant a list of what had gone particularly well. She smiled when the students celebrated their success.

Then she went home and discovered she was angry.

Rhys came to Cabin One after supper.

He was carrying a small parcel and wearing the expression he used when he knew he had disappointed someone.

"Thalia."

She stood in the doorway.

"You missed it."

"I know."

"Did you forget?"

"No. The rehearsal ran long, and by the time I could leave, the demonstration was almost over."

She looked at him. He had offered a reason, not an excuse, but the distinction didn't make the empty seat less painful.

"You could have sent word."

"You're right."

She was silent.

He looked down at the parcel.

"I brought something."

"I don't want a gift."

"I know."

He put it on the porch rail and stepped back.

"I wanted to come," he said. "I thought I could finish the rehearsal and still make it. When that stopped being possible, I should have told you. I'm sorry."

Thalia looked at him.

She wanted to believe him, and she did. She was still angry. She could feel how much she'd wanted him in the audience, how much the sight of him smiling from the bench would have mattered even if she never acknowledged it aloud.

"It was important to me," she said.

"I know."

"You said you'd be there."

"I did."
