She waited for him to add another explanation. He didn't.

At last she stepped outside and closed the door behind her.

"The program went well," she said.

He smiled, relieved by the subject but not yet by the conversation.

"Tell me."

She considered telling him nothing, withholding the details because he'd missed the chance to see them himself. It would be a childish punishment. She had no interest in becoming that kind of partner.

"The students caught both deliberate safety errors," she said. "The assistant ran the first briefing without help. Chiron approved the next stage."

Rhys's smile widened.

"That's excellent."

"It is."

He reached toward her, then stopped when he saw her expression.

She looked at his hand. She wanted to take it. She wasn't ready to be kissed yet, and that was different from wanting him gone.

"Walk with me," she said.

They took the lower path around the meadow. The sky was darkening, and the first lanterns had been lit along the fence. Thalia told him more about the demonstration, including the mistakes the students had caught and the questions they'd asked afterward. Rhys listened. He offered no attempt to turn the conversation into an explanation of his absence.

At the far end of the path she stopped.

"I'm still disappointed," she said.

"I know."

"Don't promise to make it up to me with a dinner."

"I wasn't going to."

"Good."

He waited.

She looked at him. "Next time something changes, tell me."

"I will."

She believed him. It would take time for the disappointment to fade, and she was glad he hadn't tried to hurry it along.

She reached for his hand.

He took it, his fingers warm against hers.

"Come back with me?" he asked.

She looked toward the north path.

"Not tonight."

He nodded. "All right."

She kissed his cheek, then stepped away before the tenderness became a way to avoid the harder part. They could be close again tomorrow. Tonight she wanted to go home, put her notes away and enjoy the success she'd earned without waiting for anyone else to approve it.

Rhys watched her go.

***

The next morning he left a note at her door.

*I looked at your students' results. Chiron showed me the report. You built something good. I'm sorry I missed seeing it. I'm proud of you.*

There was no gift attached.

Thalia read the note twice, folded it and put it beside her program outline. She felt the last of the sharp anger ease, though she knew the disappointment would remain a memory they'd both have to carry.

That evening she went to his cabin.

He was at the workbench when she arrived. He looked up, uncertain for one moment, then smiled when she crossed the room and took his hand.

"Hello," she said.

"Hello."

She kissed him. He responded immediately, drawing her close without trying to turn the kiss into a declaration that everything had been forgotten. She liked that. They had told the truth, accepted a little distance and come back to each other when they were ready.

When they drew apart, she was smiling.

"Your note was good," she said.

He looked relieved.

"Thank you."

"Don't expect me to frame it."

"I wouldn't dare."

She kissed him again.

Outside the cabin, spring was beginning to loosen the last of the frost. Inside, the evening could finally be theirs.

---

## March 16: The Long Afternoon

Thalia arrived at the north cabin carrying nothing.

No reports, no borrowed books, no food that could be used to explain why she'd come. She had finished the week's lessons early, returned the equipment and spent an hour alone in Cabin One before deciding she wanted to see Rhys. There was no other reason.

He opened the door with his hair damp from washing and one sleeve rolled above his elbow.

"You're early."

"For what?"

"Whatever you're planning."

She smiled.

He stepped aside. The cabin was unusually neat, the workbench cleared and the guitar resting in its case. A soft rain had begun outside, making the window glass bright with reflected light.

"Where's your work?" she asked.

"Finished."

"All of it?"

"Enough."

She looked at him. He was watching her with an expression she knew well, pleased and a little surprised that she'd arrived without warning.

"Good," she said.

She crossed the room and kissed him.

The kiss began with the easy familiarity of their ordinary greetings. Then she caught his shirt and kept him close, and the mood shifted. He responded at once, his hand finding the back of her neck while the other rested at her waist. She liked the immediate recognition in his touch, the sense that neither had to explain what they wanted from the afternoon.

He smiled against her mouth.

"You're in a hurry."

"Not at all."

She kissed him again, slower this time.

The rain thickened against the window. The stove was still warm from breakfast, and the room held the faint scent of soap and wood. Thalia could feel the quick rhythm of his breath when she touched his cheek. She liked that she could still surprise him, even after months together, and she liked the way his composure vanished when she drew him closer.

They moved toward the cot without talking much. There was no pressure in the silence, only the familiar warmth of choosing one another. She kicked off her boots and laughed when one landed against the leg of the table. Rhys caught her hand, and she pulled him down beside her.

He kissed her with an eagerness that made her smile. She closed her eyes for a moment, feeling the warmth of his hand at her back and the soft fabric of his shirt beneath her fingers. The world outside had become a dull rhythm of rain and distant wind.

There was no audience. No one needed them for an hour. They allowed the afternoon to remain private.

***

Later, the rain had stopped.

Thalia lay against Rhys's shoulder, watching the light move across the wall. He was telling her about an argument he'd had with another musician over the opening of a piece they were preparing for tour. She listened, occasionally interrupting with a suggestion that was either useful or deliberately impossible.

"You're proposing we remove the entire melody," he said.

"It would solve the argument."

"It would destroy the piece."

"An unfortunate side effect."

He laughed and caught her hand.

She was still smiling when he turned toward her.

"You've gone quiet."

"I'm listening."

"Suspicious."

She considered. "I was thinking about April."

His expression became serious.

"What about it?"

"That we're going to have to do this differently."

He nodded. "Yes."

She looked down at their joined hands.

"I like what we have," she said. "I don't want to spend six weeks pretending I don't miss it."

"Then don't."

"But I also don't want every letter to be about how miserable we are."

He smiled slightly. "I think we can manage that."

She considered. The relationship had changed over the winter, not because the first excitement had vanished but because they had learned how to make room for ordinary life around it. She could want him passionately and still go home when she chose. He could be busy with music and still make her feel wanted. They could be disappointed with one another without treating the disappointment as a verdict on everything else.

"Tell me about your first concert," she said.

He blinked. "The one in April?"

"Yes. What are you playing?"

He began explaining the program, its more difficult passages and the musician he was worried about keeping up with. She listened with genuine interest, interrupting only when she had a question worth asking.

When he finished, she rested her head against his shoulder.

"I'm going to try to visit."

"I know."

"And if I can't, I'll send you an extremely hostile review from a distance."

He laughed.

"That would be comforting."

She lifted her face and kissed him, slowly enough that she felt the afternoon's quiet return. There was warmth in the touch and a familiar, cheerful desire that made her want to remain exactly where she was.

They stayed until supper.

When she finally rose to go home, Rhys came to the door with her. The path beyond the porch was wet, but the rain had passed and the air smelled of thawing earth. She turned and kissed him once more.

"See you tomorrow?"

"Yes."

"Good."

She walked downhill without hurrying. The afternoon had belonged to them because they had chosen it, and that was enough to make her look forward to the next one.

---

## March 19: The Last Rehearsal Before Spring

The guitar string broke in the middle of a perfectly good afternoon.

Rhys had been practicing in the hall for two hours, working through the program he expected to perform on tour. Thalia had been on the opposite side of the room reviewing advanced-course reports, occasionally looking up when he repeated a troublesome passage. Neither needed the other's attention. They liked sharing the space anyway.

Then the string snapped.

The sound was sharp enough to make Thalia look up. Rhys stared at the instrument for a moment with an expression of personal betrayal.

"That one was new," he said.

"Clearly it had ambitions beyond its abilities."

He looked toward her.

"I bought it in December."

"An ancient artifact."

He laughed, but she could see the frustration beneath it. The tour was getting close. Every small failure felt larger when measured against a date he couldn't change.

She put down the report.

"Do you have another?"

"Two."

"Then change it."

"You're very sympathetic."

"I've been told."

He set the guitar on the bench and went to the instrument cupboard. Thalia watched him return with the spare packet, then caught herself thinking about how ordinary his competence had become to her. The first time she'd seen him restring a bow she had found his patience irritating. Now she liked the way he worked, the steady hands, the habit of checking a repair twice before declaring it finished.

He replaced the string, tuned the instrument and played the passage again.

The sound was better.

"See?" she said.

"Your optimism is extraordinary."

"Only when it's supported by evidence."

He looked up and smiled.

She returned to the report, but she was no longer reading the numbers. She kept thinking about the fact that the room would be quiet when he went on tour. The program would continue. The students would arrive and leave. Someone else would play in the hall, perhaps better, perhaps worse. She would still have the work she liked.

She would miss this.

When the rehearsal ended, Rhys packed the guitar and came to sit beside her.

"How's your work?"

"Nearly finished."

"Anything difficult?"

"A student who thinks the point of an assessment is to convince me he's never wrong."

"Sounds familiar."

She gave him a pointed look.

"Don't."

He grinned. "I was thinking of myself."

"Good recovery."

They sat together with the report open between them. Rhys read the student's notes and suggested a question that might force him to explain his reasoning instead of defending the result. Thalia wrote it down, then looked at him.

"That's useful."

"I have moments."

"Rare ones."

He laughed.

The hall was empty now. Outside the windows, late afternoon light fell across the path. Thalia turned toward him, suddenly aware that they had spent two hours together without touching and that she wanted to remedy the situation.

She took his hand.

Rhys looked up.

"Come here," she said.

He moved closer. She kissed him, and he met the kiss with immediate warmth, setting the report safely out of the way before drawing her toward him. The familiarity of the gesture pleased her. He knew which pages mattered to her, which objects she would resent having damaged, and which teasing remarks were best left for another moment.

When they separated, she rested her forehead against his.

"You're going to take the guitar with you," she said.

"That was the plan."

"And leave me with the terrible piano."

"An unfortunate arrangement."

She smiled. "I'll survive."

He touched her cheek.

"I know."

The confidence in his answer made her feel unexpectedly steady. He was not treating her as someone who would collapse without him. He knew she would miss him, and he believed she could still have a good spring.

She liked that he believed it.

***

They walked back to his cabin for supper.

He cooked while she finished the report at the table. They had found a comfortable rhythm for evenings like this, two people occupying the same room without requiring each minute to be shared. At one point Rhys came behind her chair and kissed the top of her head; she caught his wrist and pulled him down for a better kiss, then sent him back to the stove before he burned anything.

"Very demanding," he said.

"Very observant."

The food was good. She told him so, and he looked theatrically astonished.

After supper they washed the dishes and settled beside the stove. Thalia had been thinking about the broken string, the small failure that had interrupted a long rehearsal, and how quickly he had repaired it without needing to believe the whole performance was ruined.

"Do you think that's what we're doing?" she asked.

"Cooking?"

"No. Making changes whenever something breaks."

He considered. "Sometimes."

She looked at him. "Not fixing each other."

"No."

"Just figuring out what works."

He smiled. "That sounds familiar."

She leaned closer and kissed him. The kiss lingered, warm and comfortable, and she could feel him smiling when she drew back.

The tour was less than two weeks away. Their lives were about to separate for a while again. But she was no longer treating the distance as evidence that either had failed to care enough. It was a problem with a timetable, not a flaw in the relationship.

She rested her head against his shoulder and listened to the stove.

Tomorrow she would run her program. He would rehearse. At supper they might have news worth sharing, or they might have nothing interesting to say at all. She liked that both possibilities felt good.

The guitar string held.

---

## March 22: The Visit She Didn't Schedule

Thalia had come to the cabin to return a book.

She had borrowed it a week earlier, a collection of old compositions that Rhys insisted would help her understand the way he arranged harmonies. She'd read most of it. The parts she understood were interesting; the parts she didn't understand had made her want to throw the book against a wall, which she considered a reasonable reaction to difficult theory.

She found him sitting at the workbench with three pages of notes spread before him. His guitar lay in its case. He was not playing, writing or even looking at the music. He was staring out the window.

"You're working very hard," she said.

Rhys turned. His expression brightened.

"You're here."

"I came to return your book."

"Did you finish it?"

"Mostly."

"Verdict?"

"Your author has a terrible sense of humor."

"That's not the usual criticism."

"Then everyone else is being polite."

She put the book on the table and looked at him. He seemed tired in a way she hadn't noticed earlier in the week, his shoulders a little too stiff and the usual teasing answer slower to arrive.

"Something wrong?"

He considered. "Nothing serious."

"Then something irritating."

"Several things. I've got a list of changes to the opening piece, and each one seems to make another section worse. I thought I'd finished it."

Thalia looked at the pages. The corrections were small but numerous, the kind that appeared when a musician was trying to improve a composition that was already nearly as good as he could make it.

"You've been staring too long," she said.

"Probably."

"Come outside."

He looked at the window. "Why?"

"Because the air is better than this room, and I'm tired of watching you punish a piece of paper."

He laughed reluctantly and stood.

They walked down the north path toward the lake, where the first signs of spring were appearing along the stones. The air was cool but not cold. A few birds had returned to the lower trees, and the water was beginning to move more freely where the winter ice had broken.

Rhys walked beside her without speaking much. She let the silence last. There was no need to turn a tired afternoon into an interrogation.

At the low wall she stopped.

"Sit."

He raised an eyebrow.

"Is that an order?"

"A strong suggestion."

He sat. She settled beside him, leaving the book and the music behind in the cabin.

For a while they watched the water. Thalia could feel the old instinct to offer advice, to ask for the score and solve whatever remained wrong with it. She had learned enough by now to know that he didn't need her to repair everything.

Instead she took his hand.

He looked down at their fingers.

"Thank you," he said.

"For what?"

"Getting me out of the cabin."

"You're welcome."

She leaned closer, resting her head briefly against his shoulder. It was the sort of touch she'd once been embarrassed to offer. Now it felt natural.

He kissed her hair.

"You're unusually quiet."

"I'm trying something new."

"How's it going?"

"Terribly."

He laughed. She lifted her face and kissed him, pleased by the familiar warmth of his response. He drew her close, his hand settling at her waist, and for a moment the music and the tour and the difficulties of spring ceased to be urgent.

When they drew apart, he was smiling.

"You have a very direct method of improving an afternoon."

"Don't tell everyone. They'll start expecting it."

He grinned.

They sat together until the sun moved behind the trees.

On the walk back, Rhys began talking about the piece again. This time he sounded less frustrated, able to describe the problem without treating each error as evidence that the entire composition had failed. Thalia listened, asking questions when she wanted and remaining quiet when she had nothing useful to add.

At the cabin door he stopped.

"Stay for supper?"

She considered the hour. There was a report waiting in Cabin One, but it could wait until morning. She had finished her duties for the day.

"Yes," she said.

He opened the door and let her in. The book she'd returned lay on the table beside the music, neither demanding immediate attention.

Thalia took off her coat and turned toward him.

"There's one thing I still don't understand," she said.

"Which part?"

She caught his collar and drew him closer.

"Why you keep letting me borrow terrible books."

He laughed, then kissed her before she could continue.

The room was warm, and supper could wait a little longer.

---

## Sunday, March 24: Before the Road

The Sunday before Rhys left was sunny.

Thalia thought that was an unnecessary act of kindness on the part of the weather. It would have been easier to dislike the day if it had arrived with rain, mud and a wind that made everyone regret leaving their cabin. Instead there was a pale blue sky, a clean stretch of light across the meadow and enough warmth to make the first buds visible on the lower branches.

She had spent the morning reviewing the advanced program's final winter report. It was finished, approved and ready for the spring instructors. She could have taken the afternoon off without neglecting anything. She had made certain of that.

At noon she left Cabin One carrying a small parcel, a book and the photograph case Rhys had made for her. Annabeth was sitting on the porch with a drawing across her knees.

"Where are you going?"

"North."

"I could have guessed."

Thalia stopped.

Her sister looked up, smiling. There was no malice in it. She had known about Rhys for months, had watched Thalia learn how to be happy without turning happiness into another duty, and had decided to be quietly pleased about the result.

"Are you going to miss him?" Annabeth asked.

"Yes."

"Are you going to be unbearable?"

"Probably."

Annabeth laughed. "Good. At least you've planned ahead."

Thalia leaned over and straightened the edge of her sister's drawing where the wind had caught it.

"Don't stay out here all afternoon," she said. "The light's changing."

"I know."

"Then move before the measurements become useless."

Annabeth looked at her with supreme patience.

"Go see Rhys."

Thalia smiled and went.

***

He was waiting on the porch.

She hadn't told him the exact time she'd arrive. She had merely said she wanted the afternoon, and he had apparently decided that meant looking down the path every few minutes until she appeared.

"You've been watching for me," she said.

He smiled. "Perhaps."

"Very undignified."

"I've learned from you."

She came up the steps. He took her parcel, put it safely on the bench and drew her close. She kissed him, smiling at the familiar pleasure of finding him eager to see her. They had spent months learning each other's habits, and she could still feel the small thrill of being welcomed without question.

When they separated, he looked at the objects she'd brought.

"What's in the parcel?"

"An item of great importance."

"Should I be afraid?"

"Yes."

She carried it inside and set it on the table. He followed, closing the door against the wind. The cabin looked different now that spring was approaching. The window was open a little at the top, and the stove had been allowed to burn low. The workbench held his packed guitar case, a stack of music and the travel papers for the tour. It was unmistakably a room preparing for departure.

Thalia didn't look away.

She unwrapped the parcel. Inside was a pair of dark wool socks, newly made, with a narrow blue stripe around the cuff.

Rhys stared at them.

"You made these?"

"No. I commissioned them from someone with more patience than I possess."

"Why?"

"Because your original striped socks were appalling."

He laughed.

"And these aren't?"

"These are respectable."

He took them and examined the neat knitting.

"They're lovely."

"Don't be dramatic."

He put them down and kissed her. She answered eagerly, caught his collar and kept him close long enough that he began laughing softly against her mouth.

"You knew I'd like them."

"I had a suspicion."

"Thank you."

She smiled. He had asked for nothing grand; she had brought something practical that would travel with him, a small reminder of the hours they had spent arguing about the old pair.

She took the photograph case from her bag and opened it. The two photographs lay inside, protected by the soft lining. One showed them laughing in the orchard. The other caught Rhys kissing her cheek. She had brought them because she wanted to look at them together once more before he left.

He sat beside her on the cot.

"That first one is terrible," he said.

"Accurate."

"You're shoving me."

"You deserved it."

He smiled and touched the edge of the second picture.

"I like this one."

"So do I."

She looked at the captured moment, the surprise on her face and the visible pleasure in his. It was not an image of people who had solved anything about the future. It was an image of two people who wanted each other and had been happy for the space of one afternoon.

She put the case away.

"There's one more thing," she said.

He looked at her, amused.

"Another gift?"

"No."

She took his hand.

"I don't want the next week to become a countdown. We both know you're leaving. We know what we can promise and what we can't. I want to enjoy the time we have."

He nodded. "So do I."

She looked at him. He wasn't waiting for a speech. He was looking at her with the familiar warmth that had drawn her to his cabin on the first Monday, months ago.

She kissed him.

It began softly, then deepened when he drew her closer. She felt the pleasant certainty of his answering eagerness, the warmth of his hand at her waist and the way he smiled when she caught his face between her fingers. They were alone, with no urgent work and no need to account for the afternoon to anyone else. She liked the quiet of that. She liked being able to reach for him without turning the wanting into something she had to justify.

His hand moved to the back of her neck. She leaned into the touch, letting herself enjoy the familiar rhythm of their kisses. The window was open enough to carry in the scent of thawing grass. Light fell across the floorboards, the same bright square that had surprised her on the first Sunday she came here in daylight.

She remembered the younger version of herself who had stood at this door determined to be bold, uncertain whether wanting a man made her less independent. She wished, briefly, that she could tell that younger self how unimportant the question would become. She had wanted him. She still did. She had also built her own program, learned to trust Annabeth's judgment and discovered ambitions that belonged to her alone.

She didn't need to choose between those things.

Rhys drew back, watching her face.

"What?"

"Nothing."

He smiled. "You're doing the thinking thing."

"An unfortunate habit."

She caught his shirt and kissed him again.

The afternoon moved around them, warm and unhurried. They let it remain private. The lamp was unnecessary in the daylight, and for once the stove was quiet. They stayed close until the brightness shifted toward the window, then let the rest of the day unfold without planning every minute.

***

At supper they ate the last of the bread Thalia had brought and a pot of soup Rhys had made that morning.

He told her the tour's first performance was still scheduled for the second week of April. The musicians would gather earlier for rehearsals, and he would be traveling with two people he'd worked with at the audition. He was nervous about the opening night. She could hear it beneath his description of the schedule.

"You're going to be good," she said.

"You haven't heard the new arrangement."

"I've heard enough."

"That's a very questionable standard."

She smiled. "Then send me the music."

He nodded. "I will."

She told him about the assistant instructor who had begun running parts of the advanced program without supervision, and the student who had finally learned to correct his own mistakes rather than arguing with the target. Rhys listened with real interest, asking questions that made her explain the parts she was proud of instead of merely listing the results.

They talked until the soup was cold.

Later he took out the guitar and played the familiar melody they'd worked on through the winter. Thalia listened from the cot, smiling when he reached the pause and let it remain a pause.

When he finished, she went to sit beside him.

"You finally learned how to play that," she said.

"I had an excellent teacher."

"Don't give me all the credit."

"Only some."

She leaned into him, resting her head against his shoulder. He put the guitar aside and drew the blanket around them.

For a long while neither spoke.

There would be letters again. There would be missed evenings and perhaps a visit in the middle of the tour if their schedules allowed it. There would be arguments about music, target distances, and who had failed to write when they'd promised to. It would not be easy all the time.

Thalia found that she could imagine wanting it anyway.

"Rhys."

"Hm?"

"I'll miss you."

He turned toward her. "I'll miss you too."

She kissed him, slow and warm, pleased by the familiar touch and by the fact that they could say something plain without demanding a grand answer.

He drew her closer.

Outside the window, the evening had begun. The north path would be dark soon, the lanterns lighting one by one along the hill. But she was not walking home yet. She had come for an afternoon, and she had chosen to stay for the evening as well.

The room was warm. The tour papers remained on the workbench. The socks she'd brought him were folded beside the photographs.

Nothing had to be settled tonight.

She smiled against his shoulder, content to let the darkness come when it would.

---

## Monday, March 25: The First Mile

They left the cabin before sunrise.

Thalia had meant to sleep. She had even taken the sensible precaution of making Rhys put the travel papers inside the wooden box she'd given him, beside the ticket and the small envelope of money he would need for the first week. Then she'd lain awake listening to him breathe until the sky beyond the shutter began to pale.

By the time he stirred, she had already counted every object in the room that belonged to him and would remain behind.

The spare guitar strings would stay. So would the unfinished bow grip, the extra blankets and the cracked cup he'd insisted could survive another winter. The dark scarf he'd lent her was back in his drawer, folded beside the clean shirt she still kept there. His best guitar was in the case by the door. The older instrument stayed on the workbench.

It was an irritatingly reasonable system.

"You haven't slept," he said.

"I have."

"How much?"

"Enough to be unpleasant."

He sat up, rubbing a hand over his face. "So no change."

She threw a pillow at him.

He caught it before it struck his shoulder, laughing, and for a moment the morning looked as though it might be ordinary after all.

They ate without much appetite. Thalia made toast that burned along one edge because she forgot to turn it, and Rhys cut the burnt part away without commentary. He was dressed for travel, not performance: a heavy coat, practical boots, the same worn trousers he wore when he spent a day moving targets. She was glad. A polished version of him might have made the departure feel like something ceremonial. This was simply the man she knew, trying to remember whether he'd packed a second pair of gloves.

"You did," she said.

He looked up. "Did what?"

"Packed the gloves."

"How do you know what I was thinking?"

"You were staring at the drawer."

He smiled. "Very unsettling."

"Good."

At the door he checked his bag. She caught him reaching for the latch, then stopping because there was nothing else to do. They had spent weeks preparing for this morning. Now the morning was here, and both seemed reluctant to admit that preparation did not change the necessity of leaving.

Thalia went to him.

She didn't make an announcement. She put her hands on the front of his coat and kissed him. He drew her close at once, leaving the bag where it stood. The kiss had an urgency neither tried to pretend away; they had been affectionate the night before, and the memory only sharpened the desire to remain together a little longer.

When she drew back, Rhys rested his forehead against hers.

"We have to go," he said.

"I know."

Neither moved.

Then Thalia caught the bag handle, opened the door and stepped into the cold.

***

The driver was early this time.

He had parked the supply van beside the service gate and was drinking from a battered metal flask while checking the tires. The road beyond the camp was wet from the night's rain, and a thin layer of fog had settled between the first line of trees.

Rhys loaded the guitar himself. Thalia watched him place it so the case wouldn't shift, then handed over the smaller bag with the music. The driver secured both without ceremony.

"You'll be at the station in time," he said.

"Good," Rhys replied.

Thalia nearly asked whether the man was certain, whether the road was clear, whether he had checked the schedule for delays. She stopped herself. Rhys was nineteen. He had traveled before. He was capable of arriving at a station without her turning the trip into an inspection.

She stepped aside with him instead.

The gate was quiet enough that they could hear the stream below the road. Spring water ran high beneath the culvert, carrying bits of dead grass and the last winter leaves downstream. She watched it for a moment, then looked at Rhys.

"You'll be nervous," she said.

"Probably."

"Your first rehearsal will be worse than the audition because you'll already know they chose you, and you'll spend half the time trying to prove they weren't mistaken."

He laughed softly. "That sounds uncomfortably plausible."

"Then listen to the first musician who tells you to slow down."

"I do listen."

"Eventually."

He smiled, and the smile made her want to kiss him again. She did, briefly, then stepped back before she could turn the goodbye into an argument with the clock.

There was one thing she had brought from Cabin One: the old photograph of them in the orchard, the one she'd kept because they were both laughing at the moment the shutter closed. She took it from her coat.

"I made a copy," she said.

He looked at the picture. "You did?"

"Don't sound surprised. There are other cameras in the world."

"Apparently."

She handed it to him. "For the road."

He studied the image. The two of them were out of focus, leaning into each other, obviously amused by some private disaster. It was an imperfect photograph, and he seemed genuinely pleased to have it.

"You've got the other one," she said.

"Yes."

"This one is worse."

"Better, I think."

She gave him a look. He slipped it into the inner pocket of his coat, close enough that it wouldn't bend beneath the guitar case.

"Thank you," he said.

The driver called that it was time.

Rhys caught her hand once more.

"I'll write when I know the first address."

"I know."

"And I'll tell you if something changes."

"Good."

She wanted to say a dozen things. None was new. She had already told him she would miss him; he already knew she wanted him back. She had warned him to eat properly, to rest his hand, to stop pretending that a difficult passage was a personal insult. He had heard every word and teased her about most of them.

There was nothing left to instruct.

"I love the work you do," she said, surprising herself. "Go do it well."

He looked at her for a moment, then kissed her again. This time she didn't pull away quickly. There were people at the gate, but they were busy with their own morning and had no interest in watching. The kiss belonged to them, and she felt no need to apologize for wanting it.

When he stepped back, his eyes were bright.

"I'll see you," he said.

"Yes."

He climbed into the van.

She watched it start, lurch once over the uneven road and move down through the trees until she could no longer see the tail lamps. She stayed a moment longer, staring at the place where it had disappeared.

Then she went to work.

***

Her first lesson began with a small disaster.

Two students had decided the newly installed safety screens could be moved closer together because doing so made the practice course more difficult. A third had agreed, then changed his mind when an arrow struck the wrong side of the dividing net. Nobody was injured. Thalia arrived to find three people arguing over a configuration that had already been declared unsafe.

She took one look at the range and said, "Out."

The students stared.

"Put the bows down. Out of the lane."

They obeyed.

She moved the screens back herself, not because they couldn't do it, but because the mistake required an immediate correction. Then she brought them to the bench and made them explain what had happened, each in turn, without interrupting the others.

The first blamed the equipment.

"The equipment was where I left it," Thalia said.

The second blamed the wind.

"The wind did not move a sixty-pound screen."

The third looked at the ground and admitted he'd known the placement was wrong but hadn't wanted to be the one who objected.

That was the part Thalia cared about.

"Next time," she told him, "say it before someone gets hurt. Being right afterward isn't a useful form of bravery."

She saw the words land. The student nodded, then looked at the other two with an expression that suggested there would be a second conversation when she was gone.

Good. That was part of the lesson.

At lunch she realized she had not thought about the van for almost an hour.

The realization made her feel guilty for exactly five seconds, until she recognized how foolish it was. Rhys had wanted her to keep working. She had wanted the same thing. The point of his departure wasn't to make the hours without him empty.

She finished eating and went to inspect the new target frames.

By evening the clouds had lifted. The lake was bright under a pale spring sky. Thalia walked the lower path before supper, not because she expected to see Rhys, but because she needed to think without another person asking whether the advanced program was going well.

She stopped at the low wall where they'd argued about the music offer and looked at the water. The old impulse to count how long he'd been gone arrived, and she let it pass. It was less than a day. She would not make the absence larger by measuring it every hour.

At Cabin One Annabeth was working on a scale drawing of the eastern wall. She looked up as Thalia entered.

"How was the range?"

"Disastrous. Educational."

"Sounds right."

Thalia sat beside her.

"How's Rhys?"

"On a train, probably."

"That wasn't what I asked."

Thalia gave her sister a sidelong look, then smiled.

"He's doing something he wants to do."

Annabeth nodded. "Good."

They spent the rest of the evening arguing about the wall's drainage. Thalia tried to suggest a reinforcement that Annabeth dismissed as excessive; Annabeth proposed a channel that Thalia thought would freeze too easily. Neither won the argument. They drew three more versions and agreed to inspect the ground again in the morning.

When Annabeth finally went to bed, Thalia took out her book and found the photograph she'd kept there. She looked at their faces a while, then put it back.

She could miss Rhys without making the feeling into the whole of her day. She could want to kiss him so badly that the memory made her smile and still be pleased with the work she'd done.

It wasn't the end of anything.

She turned down the lamp and slept.

---

## April 2: The Wrong Key

Rhys discovered on his second morning with the ensemble that he had been pronouncing the conductor's name incorrectly.

Nobody had told him.

He'd spent the previous day saying it at least six times, once while introducing himself to the older woman who ran the rehearsal hall, twice while asking where the scores had been put, and three times during a disagreement about tempo that had become unexpectedly serious. Now a violinist from the far end of the room corrected him while they waited for coffee.

"It's Edda, not Ida."

Rhys stared at her.

"She's been answering."

"She's polite. It doesn't mean you're right."

He wanted to disappear.

The violinist, whose name was Mara and who found embarrassment much more entertaining than he did, gave him a sympathetic pat on the shoulder.

"You should hear what she calls the second trumpeter."

"Does she get it wrong?"

"Deliberately."

Rhys laughed despite himself.

This was the part of the tour he'd known he couldn't learn at camp. Every rehearsal contained a hundred tiny habits that nobody thought worth explaining: where the players kept their coats, how long a break lasted, which passages the conductor treated as sacred and which she expected them to change, who could argue about an arrangement without starting a personal feud. He was new. He had been invited because he'd played well at the audition. That fact did not give him membership in the invisible system of the ensemble.

He'd arrived expecting hard work. He hadn't expected to feel like a novice who'd borrowed a better musician's clothes.

At ten the rehearsal began with a piece he'd never heard.

He read the opening line without difficulty. The ensemble played through the first movement, and he found his place quickly enough to be pleased. Then the conductor stopped everyone and told the strings to play the same passage again, half as fast.

Rhys adjusted.

They tried a third time.

The conductor shook her head and looked at him.

"Calder. You're still trying to lead the line."

He looked down at his score. "I'm following the written rhythm."

"Yes. The trouble is that the written rhythm isn't the music."

He stared at her.

She tapped the page with the end of her pencil. "Listen to the violin before you come in. There's a space there. You have to let them give it to you."

He had said almost the same thing to Thalia once, when she'd been trying to dance three steps ahead of him. She'd told him to stop treating a pause like a failure. Both memories arrived at the least convenient moment.

"Right," he said.

They began again.

He listened.

For the first time the line made sense. The violin phrase rose, hesitated and left a narrow opening. Rhys entered there, his own part settling beneath it rather than pushing the whole ensemble forward. The sound changed immediately. He heard it happen as clearly as a string going into tune.

The conductor nodded once.

"Better. Again."

They played the movement three more times. By noon Rhys's fingers ached, his shoulders were stiff and the coffee had gone cold. He had learned more than he could remember, mostly by being wrong in front of people who had no particular interest in pretending otherwise.

He was exhilarated.

***

At lunch Mara sat across from him with a bowl of soup that looked as though it had been constructed for the purpose of discouraging appetite.

"You survived."

"Apparently."

"She likes you."

"That's a strange way of showing it."

"She corrected you for half an hour. If she thought you were hopeless, she'd have let you keep playing wrong."

He considered. It sounded exactly like something Thalia might say after a lesson.

"You're smiling," Mara said.

"I know someone who makes the same argument."

"At camp?"

"Yes."

"Musician?"

"Archer."

Mara's expression brightened with the enthusiasm of a person who had found a better subject than lunch.

"That explains the way you move around a stage."

"How?"

"Like you're checking whether somebody's going to fire a projectile at you."

He laughed.

She asked about camp. He told her enough to explain the training program, the winter concert and the reason he'd brought a wooden box full of pencils that looked far too carefully repaired for anything he'd bought himself. He didn't give her a biography of Thalia. He told her that the box was a gift, that he knew a woman who would be offended if he used it carelessly, and that the offense would be entirely deserved.

"Sounds serious," Mara said.

Rhys considered.

"It is."

She nodded and returned to the soup. There was something pleasant about the lack of further questions. The relationship existed. It was not an invitation for everybody he met to ask what happened when they were alone.

Later, when he went back to rehearsals, he found himself imagining what Thalia would say about his morning. She would listen to the part about the conductor, offer exactly one helpful correction and then declare that she'd told him all of this months ago. She'd be infuriating. He missed it more than he expected.

That evening the ensemble went to a crowded restaurant near their lodging. Rhys stayed for the meal, laughed at a story about a broken cello case and listened to the older musicians argue about whether a small mistake onstage was better than a perfectly accurate performance nobody remembered. He joined the argument and discovered that he had opinions of his own, not merely things he'd been taught.

At nine he returned to his room.

The lodging was comfortable enough, but it was not his cabin. The mattress was softer, the lamp brighter, the floorboard beside the door completely silent. There was no slate with laundry instructions. Nobody had hidden a scarf in the drawer. The whole room smelled of furniture polish, and the curtains were too heavy to move in the evening breeze.

He put the wooden box on the desk.

Inside were his pencils, a small packet of spare strings and the photograph from the orchard. He took the picture out, studied Thalia's laughing face and set it beside the lamp.

He wasn't lonely enough to regret coming. He was lonely in a more specific way. He had spent a good day doing work he'd wanted to do for years. He wanted to tell Thalia about it while she sat beside him with her feet on the cot, making terrible comments about his hand position. He wanted the easy touch of her fingers against his collar. He wanted to see the brief shift in her face when she was pleased and trying to pretend she wasn't.

He could not have any of that tonight.

He could write.

He found a sheet of paper and began.

*Grace,*

*I've survived two rehearsals. Apparently this is an achievement. I also discovered that I've been calling the conductor the wrong name since I arrived. Nobody thought to correct me until this morning, which says unfortunate things about my coworkers.*

He paused. She would laugh. He could hear it.

*I was wrong about a passage today. Very wrong. The conductor made me listen instead of counting. You may take approximately one minute to celebrate the fact that your advice has finally reached me.*

He set down the pencil and considered what else mattered.

There was a difference between describing a rehearsal and describing the effect it had on him. He had been embarrassed, then delighted, then frightened by how much he wanted to keep working with people who challenged him. The fear was the part he had not yet told anyone.

He wrote it anyway.

*I think I'm going to be good here. Not good enough to stop making mistakes. Good enough that I want them to keep correcting me until the music sounds the way it should. That thought frightens me more than the mistakes.*

He looked at the sentence. Thalia had once told him to stop treating a choice as a verdict on everything else in his life. He wondered whether she'd recognize the same problem here.

He added another line.

*I wish you were in the room tonight. I wouldn't ask you to make the work easier. I'd like to be able to come home and stop thinking about it for an hour. I miss the way you can do that simply by looking at me as if I've said something ridiculous.*

He was about to sign the letter when a knock came at the door.

Mara stood in the hall carrying a torn page of music.

"Sorry. Can I borrow your pencil sharpener?"

He looked at the box on his desk.

"Of course."

She stepped inside, saw the photograph and smiled.

"Your archer?"

"Yes."

"She looks as though she's about to win an argument."

"She probably was."

Mara sharpened her pencil, thanked him and left. The interruption lasted two minutes. When he returned to the letter, the last paragraph seemed too solemn. He considered crossing it out. Then he remembered that Thalia had written about wearing his old shirt because it smelled like the cabin.

There was no advantage to being less truthful than she'd been.

He finished:

*I want to kiss you when I come back. No poetic explanation. Just that.*

*Rhys.*

He folded the page, put it in an envelope and placed it beside the door so he wouldn't forget it in the morning.

Then he practiced the troublesome entrance silently with his fingers on the edge of the desk, counting only the first measure before letting the imaginary violin give him the space he needed.

For once he got it right.

---

## April 5: An Apprentice Who Disagreed

Thalia knew the lesson would go badly when her assistant asked to change it.

They were standing beside the new advanced range, looking at a series of obstacles the students would have to use as cover during a timed exercise. The weather was clear. The ground was dry enough to be safe. The students had been looking forward to the session all week, and Thalia had written the sequence with enough care that she could explain every decision.

Her assistant, a daughter of Athena called Eleni, examined the obstacles and folded her arms.

"The second lane's too easy."

Thalia looked at her.

"It's a training lane."

"I know. But they've been practicing that turn for days. Half of them can do it without thinking."

"That's why they're doing it under a time limit."

Eleni shook her head. "The time limit will make them rush, not think. If they already know the movement, they're going to try to beat the clock instead of examining the angles."

Thalia was prepared to argue. The reply formed in her head with satisfying speed: the students needed to demonstrate that they could perform the movement consistently before a more difficult obstacle was introduced. They needed discipline. They needed to stop being distracted by novelty.

Then she heard herself.

She had made exactly this objection to Rhys months earlier when he'd suggested teaching a difficult student differently from the rest of the group. She had been partly right. She had also been so invested in proving that the method was correct that she had not listened to what the student needed.

Eleni was looking at her.

"All right," Thalia said. "Show me what you'd change."

The assistant blinked.

"You want me to move it?"

"I want you to explain your proposal."

Eleni came around the screen and pointed to the second obstacle. She wanted to rotate it slightly so the students would have to look for an opening instead of using the same movement every time. She also wanted to remove the visible marker they'd been trained to aim toward and replace it with three possible target positions.

Thalia followed the explanation. There were flaws. The new angle would leave one corner exposed, and the third target position was too close to the safety boundary. But the underlying idea was good.

She said so.

Eleni looked almost offended by the praise.

"You'd have to change the third marker," Thalia added. "Otherwise somebody could loose an arrow into the walkway. And you can't make the new lane harder without testing it first."

"I can test it."

"You can. I'll watch."

The assistant's expression changed. Thalia recognized the moment when a student realized she was being trusted with responsibility rather than merely invited to do extra work.

They spent twenty minutes moving the obstacles. Eleni tested the angles with an unstrung bow, then brought in a second instructor to check the safety line. The changes took longer than planned. The students grumbled while they waited. Thalia tolerated the grumbling because she had decided to see whether the new lesson worked.

It did.

The first group slowed down instead of rushing. They stopped to assess which target was live. They argued about how best to use the cover. One student chose a path Thalia hadn't anticipated and made it through the exercise without breaking a rule. Another took a risk she disliked but could explain it well enough that she allowed him to try again under supervision.

By the end of the hour Eleni was flushed and smiling.

"That was better," she said.

"It was different."

"Better."

Thalia looked toward the targets. The students were still discussing the exercise, which meant the questions would follow them beyond the range.

"Yes," she admitted. "It was better."

Eleni stared at her, surprised by the admission.

"Don't get used to it," Thalia said.

"I wasn't planning to."

The assistant laughed.

Thalia watched her go to collect the last of the arrows and felt a strange pride. She had not made the lesson better by doing everything herself. She had made it better by giving someone else room to challenge her and then having the sense to recognize a good idea.

That evening she went to the lake to think.

The path was muddy in places, and the first green shoots had begun pushing through the dead grass. The water lay dark and still beyond the reeds. She sat on the low wall and tried to decide whether to write Rhys about the lesson.

She wanted to tell him. That was easy.

What she didn't want to do was make it sound as if the whole afternoon had been about learning something he'd already told her. Eleni had been the one who suggested the change. She deserved the credit. Thalia had listened and made a judgment. That was her work, and she wanted it to remain hers.

She could write about it without turning her assistant into a prop in a story about herself.

The difficulty was that she had begun to recognize how much she enjoyed hearing Rhys describe his own work. She liked reading about the people he met, the mistakes that annoyed him, the moments when a passage finally came together. She wanted to give him the same kind of account rather than the clipped report she'd normally send Chiron.

At Cabin One she found an envelope from him waiting on the table.

She opened it standing up.

The letter was full of rehearsal details, including the story about calling the conductor by the wrong name. Thalia laughed so sharply that Annabeth looked up from her book.

"What?"

"He's an idiot."

"Is he all right?"

"Very."

She sat on the bed and read the rest. When she reached the sentence about being good enough to want correction, she stopped. It was a feeling she knew, though she wasn't sure she'd ever described it that way. The confidence to be wrong in front of someone else was rarer than accuracy itself.

Then she reached the last lines.

*I want to kiss you when I come back. No poetic explanation. Just that.*

She read them twice.

Annabeth was watching her now.

"What's in the letter?"

"Work."

"That's a very happy-looking work letter."

Thalia folded it carefully.

"He misses me."

"Of course he does."

She looked at her sister. There was something so unremarkable about the answer that she almost laughed. Annabeth seemed to consider it obvious that someone who knew Thalia would miss her. It was not the way Thalia thought about herself, though she'd begun to suspect Annabeth had been right about her more often than she liked to admit.

She fetched a clean sheet.

*Rhys,*

*You called the conductor the wrong name for two days. That's a remarkable achievement. I'm very proud of you.*

She paused, smiling.

*The advanced program is improving. One of my assistants made a suggestion I didn't like. I let her test it. It worked. I know you'll want to say something unbearably smug about that, so I am giving you permission to be smug for exactly thirty seconds.*

She looked at the page. Too much teasing. Not enough of the real day.

*I was proud of her. She didn't need me to give her the answer. She needed someone willing to trust her judgment and correct the parts that weren't safe. I want to be that sort of teacher, though I am beginning to understand how much work it will take.*

She sat still for a moment after writing the last sentence. It was the sort of statement she rarely allowed herself to make. She'd been comfortable claiming skill. She was less comfortable admitting she wanted to become better at something that mattered to her.

Rhys would understand. That was why she was writing it.

She continued.

*Your rehearsal sounds difficult, which is probably good. You should stay and be annoyed by people who know more than you do. I've had the pleasure myself.*

Then she read his final lines again and smiled.

*For the record, I also want to kiss you. I am not going to write any more about that in a letter somebody else might carry. Use your imagination, but don't make yourself insufferable.*

She signed the page, folded it and put it with the outgoing mail.

At supper she talked with Annabeth about the range exercise. Her sister wanted to know precisely how Eleni had altered the target placement and whether the third lane was safe under a crosswind. Thalia explained the changes, and they argued about the measurements until the food went cold.

She enjoyed the argument.

When she returned to Cabin One, she found herself looking toward the shelf where the orchard photograph stood. Rhys was away doing his work. She was home doing hers. The desire to see him remained immediate and sometimes distracting, but it did not make either part of their lives less real.

She went to bed thinking about the next lesson and the possibility that Eleni would disagree with her again.

She hoped she would.

---

## April 9: The Second Entrance

Rhys's first performance went wrong in a place he hadn't expected.

The rehearsal had been good. The conductor had given them twenty minutes to adjust to the small theater, whose ceiling swallowed the high notes and sent the lower instruments back toward the performers. The front row sat so close to the stage that Rhys could see the patterns in the audience's coats before anyone arrived. He had checked the strings, marked the difficult transitions and played the opening measure twice without trouble.

He was ready.

Then he heard the audience coming through the lobby.

They talked at a volume that made the backstage corridor sound smaller. Someone laughed too loudly. A child asked whether the musicians could hear people talking about them. Another voice answered that of course they could not, which was so plainly wrong that Rhys nearly laughed.

He stopped laughing when he realized how much he wanted the performance to go well.

It wasn't just the music. He wanted to deserve the invitation. He wanted the musicians who had worked with him all week to be glad they'd included him, and the conductor to stop looking at his entrance with the patient expectation that he'd eventually rush it. He wanted to write Thalia afterward and tell her something better than that he'd survived.

Mara found him beside the instrument cupboard.

"You're pale."

"Excellent opening remark."

"You look as though you're about to sit an examination."

"I feel as though I'm about to sit an examination."

She adjusted the strap on her violin case and looked him over.

"Listen to the room. It isn't asking you to prove anything. It's going to be there while you play."

Rhys considered.

"That sounds like advice somebody else has given me."

"Then perhaps you should listen to that person."

He smiled despite himself.

The stage manager called the first group.

The musicians filed out. Rhys took his place, set the guitar where it belonged and looked toward the conductor. Edda gave him a small nod. The audience settled. Somewhere in the front row somebody unwrapped a sweet with an enormous amount of paper.

Then the music began.

The opening movement went well. Rhys followed the strings, took his place beneath them and felt the sound move through the room in a way it never had during rehearsal. People were listening. That was the thing he'd forgotten while worrying about whether he'd make a mistake: the room was full of people who had chosen to come hear the music, not judges waiting to declare that the last two weeks had been a fraud.

He began to enjoy himself.

The mistake came in the middle of the second piece.

A violinist missed a cue. It wasn't serious. The melody would have recovered within two measures if everyone else had held their places. But Rhys heard the missing note and came in early, trying to fill the silence. He could hear the mistake the instant he made it.

He stopped.

The conductor's eyes flicked toward him. There was no time for apology. He waited for the next opening, counted the beat and entered correctly on the following measure. The ensemble moved on. The audience probably heard nothing beyond a brief roughness in the sound.

Rhys heard the error all the way to the end.

At the interval he went backstage and stood very still beside the water table. His face was hot. He had been doing the thing he'd sworn not to do, correcting somebody else's mistake before he'd understood what the music needed.

Mara came over.

"First night," she said.

"I know."

"You recovered."

"I caused the problem."

"You added to it. There's a difference."

"An important distinction."

"Here it is. You don't have to rescue every phrase. Sometimes you have to let somebody else recover."

He looked at her. She was not being especially sympathetic. He appreciated that.

"I know," he said.

"Then do it."

She went to check her violin. Rhys remained by the water table for a while, thinking about Thalia. She had an extraordinary talent for seeing a mistake and immediately making it her responsibility to correct. He had found the habit irritating when they first taught together. Now he'd managed to demonstrate the same mistake in front of an audience.

He wished she were there so he could complain about the unfairness of her having been right in absentia.

The second half went better.

He listened before he entered. He didn't rush to correct another player when a phrase slipped. The closing piece had a long passage where he could almost feel the music breathe around him, and for several minutes he stopped thinking about what the conductor would say afterward.

When the final note ended, the silence was complete.

Then the audience applauded.

Rhys stood with the ensemble and bowed. There was a sharp rush of pleasure, quickly followed by exhaustion. He had spent two weeks imagining the performance as a verdict on his future; it had turned out to be one evening, with mistakes and good moments and another rehearsal scheduled for the next morning.

He was glad he'd come.

***

Edda spoke to him afterward.

"You rushed the second entrance."

"I know."

"And then you fixed it."

"Eventually."

"Good. Do it sooner tomorrow."

Rhys nodded.

She moved on to the next musician before he could think of a clever answer. He watched her go, then caught Mara's eye across the room. She raised her cup in a small salute.

They went out for supper with the group. Rhys sat beside a cellist who had spent the first half of the performance pretending not to be terrified and had then played the final solo beautifully. The musicians talked about the evening in fragments. Somebody complained about the lighting; somebody else praised the audience's attention; the cellist admitted she'd forgotten which side of the stage the exit was on and nearly walked into a curtain.

Rhys laughed until his face hurt.

At the lodging he found a note from Thalia waiting among the mail.

She had written about the training range, an assistant called Eleni and a lesson that improved because Thalia had let somebody else alter the plan. The letter was funny, irritated and unmistakably hers. Near the end she'd admitted she wanted to kiss him and then warned him not to become insufferable about the admission.

He read that sentence three times.

He could picture her writing it, frowning at the page because the words made her feel exposed. He could also picture the smile she'd probably tried to hide when she sealed the envelope. The distance between them felt abruptly sharper, less like a gap on a calendar and more like the absence of a particular person who could have been sitting on the edge of his bed making fun of his performance clothes.

He put the letter beside the photograph.

Then he began his reply.

*Grace,*

*I rushed the entrance. You would have hated it. The violinist missed a note, I tried to correct it, and the result was worse than either mistake by itself. I recovered eventually, which apparently counts as progress.*

He paused.

*The rest of the concert went well. There were people in the audience who didn't know me or owe me anything, and they listened. I had forgotten how different that feels from playing for camp. I liked it.*

He thought of the applause. It had been good. It had not made every uncertainty disappear. He was still nervous, and he would be nervous tomorrow. There was relief in finally understanding that this was part of the work rather than evidence that he didn't belong.

He wrote that too.

*I think I can do this. Even on the nights when I don't play perfectly.*

Then he looked at her letter again.

*I know you don't want me to become insufferable about the last paragraph you wrote, but it was the best part of my evening. I want you here. I want to come back after a concert and find you waiting with your terrible opinions and that look you get when you're pretending not to be pleased.*

*I would like to kiss you until both of us forget the second entrance.*

He signed the letter and put it in an envelope. The image made him laugh to himself: Thalia in his room, reaching for his collar and telling him the music could wait. It wasn't a substitute for being together. It was something pleasant to imagine before the next morning's rehearsal.

He turned out the lamp.

In the dark he could still hear the place where he'd entered too early. He listened to the phrase in his head once more, let the missing note remain missing and entered on the beat where he belonged.

Tomorrow he would do it in front of an audience.

---

## April 14: The Letter She Didn't Send

The first mistake Thalia made was assuming the mail would arrive on Friday.

The second was pretending she hadn't made the assumption.

By Saturday afternoon she had checked the pigeonholes outside the pavilion three times, each time claiming she was looking for a notice from Chiron. On Sunday morning she stopped pretending and went directly to the courier's desk.

The woman from Hermes's cabin was sitting behind a stack of packages, sorting them into routes with the weary competence of someone who had learned that people could become very unreasonable about a delayed piece of paper.

"Nothing for you," she said before Thalia reached the table.

"I haven't asked."

"You asked yesterday. And Thursday."

Thalia narrowed her eyes.

The courier put down a bundle of envelopes. "The south road washed out. Everything coming through the station is delayed. We might get a delivery tonight. More likely tomorrow."

"How long has it been closed?"

"Since Wednesday."

Thalia thought about the letter she'd sent Rhys after Eleni's improved training lesson. It was possible he hadn't received it. It was possible he'd written back and the letter was sitting in a bag somewhere on the other side of a broken road. It was also possible that he had been too busy to write, had forgotten the schedule they had discussed or had decided his new life was too interesting to interrupt for a letter home.

The last possibility was unreasonable.

She thought about it anyway.

"Thank you," she said.

The courier looked startled enough to be offended.

Thalia went to the range before she could say something less polite.

***

The lesson was an advanced exercise in judgment. The students had to inspect a practice course and identify every unsafe angle before anyone was allowed to loose an arrow. Thalia had designed it so that an apparently easy route concealed a dangerous cross-line.

The first group missed it.

She stopped them before they took their positions and made them start the inspection again. The second group found the problem but suggested a fix that would have made another lane unsafe. The third found both issues and marked them correctly, then congratulated themselves so loudly that Thalia made them repeat the procedure in silence.

By the end of the lesson her assistant looked exhausted.

"You've been hard on them."

"They were careless."

"Some were. You made them all feel as if they'd nearly killed somebody."

Thalia looked at Eleni. There was a steadiness in the girl's expression she recognized from their earlier disagreement. Her assistant was not trying to undermine her. She was telling her something she thought mattered.

Thalia considered the lesson.

The students had been careless in places. The course was designed to reveal that. But she had also been harsher than usual, impatient with errors she normally would have used as opportunities to teach. She'd been carrying the missed letter around in her head and had let the frustration leak into her instructions.

"They deserved to be corrected," she said.

"Yes."

"They didn't deserve all of my mood."

Eleni's expression changed.

Thalia put down the clipboard. "I'll speak to them tomorrow. Before we begin."

"All right."

"And you can run the opening inspection."

Eleni looked surprised. "You want me to?"

"You're better at explaining it without making everyone feel stupid."

"You're not bad at it."

"Don't make the compliment worse."

Eleni smiled and went to put the equipment away.

Thalia remained by the empty range, irritated with herself. She had made the same mistake she feared making in the relationship: letting one feeling occupy the space meant for another. The students had nothing to do with Rhys. They did not need to carry the cost of her uncertainty.

She went back to Cabin One and wrote a letter.

It was a bad letter.

She began with a complaint about the missing post, then moved to a series of increasingly sharp observations about how busy people managed to write when they wanted to. By the second paragraph she was accusing a man who had told her repeatedly that he loved working and loved being with her of using the first as an excuse to neglect the second. She read the page and recognized the voice.

It was the voice she used when she was frightened of being left behind and didn't want anyone to notice.

She put the page down.

At the window, Annabeth was working on the east wall model. She looked over.

"What's wrong?"

"Nothing."

Annabeth gave her a look.

Thalia sighed. "The mail's late."

"Rhys?"

"Yes."

"Is he supposed to write?"

"He did write. The road is closed."

"Then what's the problem?"

Thalia stared at the unfinished letter. She knew the answer, but saying it aloud would make it sound as unreasonable as it was.

"I don't like not knowing," she said.

Annabeth considered. "Nobody does."

"You're not helping."

"I wasn't trying to solve it."

Thalia looked at her younger sister. Annabeth had survived years of uncertainty herself. She knew what it felt like to wait for an adult who might not come back, to make practical plans around someone's absence, to tell herself the uncertainty didn't matter.

Thalia didn't want to turn Rhys into another version of that fear. He hadn't earned it.

She tore up the letter.

Annabeth watched without comment.

Then Thalia took a clean sheet and began again.

*Rhys,*

*The mail is late because the south road is flooded. I know that. I'm writing anyway, partly because I want you to have something waiting when deliveries resume and partly because I am spectacularly bad at being patient.*

She paused. That was true. She could say more.

*The program is working. Eleni caught me being unfair with a group today. She was right. I was angry about something that had nothing to do with them, and they paid for it. I'm going to apologize tomorrow and try to do better.*

She looked at the sentence. It was less satisfying than the accusation she'd torn up. It was also something she could live with when Rhys read it.

She added a paragraph about the students' exercise, then stopped again.

*I missed your letter this week. I know it may still be in transit. I don't want you to write because you're afraid I'll be angry. I want to hear from you when you have something to tell me. I'm going to try to remember the difference, even when I am being unreasonable about a road I can't fix.*

She signed the letter and sealed it.

At supper she found Eleni and asked the assistant to gather the students a few minutes early the following morning. Eleni agreed without comment. Thalia was grateful for the lack of fuss.

The next day she apologized to the group before the lesson began.

"I was too hard on you yesterday," she said. "The safety errors were real. I was right to stop the exercise. But I made some of you feel foolish instead of helping you understand what needed to change. That's not how this program is supposed to work."

The students stared.

One of them raised a hand.

"Does that mean we can use the second target again?"

"No."

The group laughed.

"Nice try," Thalia said.

They began the lesson. Eleni led the inspection, and Thalia watched from the side, offering corrections only when the safety line required one. The students did better. The improvement belonged partly to the assistant and partly to their own willingness to learn.

Thalia felt better too, though not because she had found a way to stop missing Rhys.

The mail arrived late that afternoon.

His letter was there, written after the opening performance, full of complaints about stage acoustics and the conductor's merciless corrections. He had rushed a passage and recovered. He had enjoyed the applause. Near the end he had written that he wanted to kiss her until they forgot the mistake.

Thalia sat on the porch and read the line twice.

The accusation she'd nearly sent felt suddenly unbearable, but she was glad she'd torn it up before anybody else had seen it. She wasn't proud of the impulse. She was proud of not having treated it as the truth.

She put his letter inside her book.

There was no grand revelation in a road reopening. There was only a man who had written when he could and a woman who had remembered, just in time, that waiting didn't give her the right to invent his intentions.

She went inside to prepare the next lesson.

---

## Sunday, April 21: A Seat in the Third Row

Thalia bought the ticket with money she'd been carrying in her coat for three weeks.

She had not intended to save it. The coins had accumulated from small jobs and unspent allowances, the kind of money she normally forgot about until she needed a replacement bowstring. Then the ensemble announced a Sunday afternoon performance in a town within reach of the camp's supply route, and the possibility became too tempting to ignore.

She asked Eleni to cover the Monday opening lesson.

Her assistant looked at the schedule, checked the equipment roster and said she could manage it. She didn't ask why Thalia wanted the day off. Thalia told her anyway.

"I'm going to hear Rhys."

Eleni smiled. "Good."

"Don't say it like that."

"Like what?"

"As though you've been waiting for me to admit something."

"I've been waiting for you to take a day off."

Thalia stared at her. Eleni folded the schedule and put it in her bag.

"Have fun," she said, then escaped before Thalia could formulate a response.

The van left after breakfast. The driver was taking supplies toward the same town and agreed to drop her near the station as long as she didn't delay him. She carried one small bag: a clean shirt, her book, the photograph of the orchard and a dark scarf that still reminded her of the north cabin even though Rhys's mother had made the newer one.

Annabeth stood on the porch watching her leave.

"You're going to be late if you keep checking the bag."

"I have everything."

"Then go."

Thalia hugged her sister quickly. Annabeth hugged her back with enough surprise that she almost dropped her notebook.

"Tell him I said hello," Annabeth said.

"I will."

"And don't spend the entire concert judging his fingers."

"I make no promises."

The driver called from the road. Thalia climbed into the van and went.

***

The town was not especially impressive.

It had a market square, a narrow river crossed by two bridges, and a theater built behind a row of shops whose owners had evidently decided that architecture was someone else's concern. The theater entrance was modest, but the inside had a warm, careful beauty: old wooden seats, a low balcony and a ceiling painted dark blue. Someone had polished the brass railings until they caught the light.

Thalia arrived early.

She collected her ticket and found her seat in the third row, near the aisle. It had a clear view of the stage. She could see where the musicians would sit, where Rhys's guitar would rest when he wasn't playing, and the conductor's stand. There was no point trying to find him backstage. He was working. She was here to watch.

The idea pleased her.

She had watched Rhys play alone, in a cabin, and at the camp's winter concert. She had heard the music with the knowledge that he was thinking of her, that the pauses and little changes belonged partly to conversations they'd shared. Here the program contained his name among a dozen others, and no one in the audience knew that the woman in the third row had come because she loved him.

She liked that anonymity more than she'd expected.

People began filling the seats. A couple near the front argued over a coat, then resolved the matter by putting it under both their chairs. A little boy insisted he could see the musicians better from the aisle and was gently returned to his seat by a patient parent. An elderly woman sat beside Thalia and examined the program with intense concentration.

"Do you know the group?" the woman asked.

"A little."

"Are they any good?"

Thalia considered. "One of them is."

The woman laughed. "Then I hope he plays loudly enough for the rest."

Thalia smiled and turned toward the stage.

The musicians came out.

Rhys was among the last. He wore a dark shirt she recognized and had his hair neatly tied back, something he almost never bothered doing at camp. He carried the guitar with the practiced attention of someone entering a room where the instrument mattered more than his appearance. He glanced toward the audience, but she didn't know whether he saw her.

She hoped he wouldn't.

Not until afterward.

The concert began.

The first movement was unfamiliar, and Thalia had to work to follow it. The ensemble moved quickly between phrases, leaving spaces that required the players to listen as much as count. She caught herself thinking about archery: the distinction between completing a motion and judging what the motion had made possible, the way a student could perform every step correctly and still miss the actual target if he stopped paying attention.

Then she saw Rhys enter a phrase a fraction late.

Her whole body stiffened.

He recovered almost immediately, following the violin rather than trying to force his way through the gap. The rest of the piece moved on without trouble. Thalia let out the breath she'd been holding and made herself stop watching his hands as though she were grading a lesson.

She wasn't his instructor.

She was here because she wanted to hear him.

Once she remembered that, the music became easier to enjoy.

The second half contained a piece she'd heard in an earlier rehearsal, the one with the long quiet passage Rhys had written about in his letter. The conductor held the silence longer than Thalia expected. It felt precarious, as if the entire audience might begin coughing or moving or looking around for something to happen. Instead everyone waited.

The instruments returned together.

Thalia felt the sound move through her chest before she could identify which instrument carried the melody. She understood, in that moment, why Rhys had wanted to leave camp to do this work. The music didn't belong to one person. It existed between them, each player making room for the others and trusting them to enter when the moment came.

By the time the piece ended she was smiling.

The old woman beside her applauded vigorously.

"That was good," she said.

"Yes."

"Which one do you know?"

Thalia looked toward the stage. Rhys had raised his head at the applause. He was scanning the audience now, and she thought he might have found her.

"The guitarist," she said.

"Good choice."

Thalia laughed.

***

She waited until the audience had begun leaving before going toward the side entrance.

A stage manager was standing beside a narrow corridor, collecting programs and keeping people from wandering backstage. Thalia approached with her ticket in hand.

"I'm here to see Rhys Calder," she said.

The woman looked up.

"Family?"

Thalia hesitated for one heartbeat.

"His girlfriend."

The word felt both familiar and strange in the mouth of a person who had once refused to label anything that might make her vulnerable. She didn't regret it.

The stage manager smiled. "Wait here. I'll see whether he's free."

Thalia waited.

A few minutes later a door opened farther down the corridor and Rhys appeared carrying his guitar case.

He stopped so abruptly that the musician behind him nearly walked into his back.

"Thalia?"

She raised an eyebrow. "Good concert."

He set the case down.

"You're here."

"An excellent observation."
