He was smiling now, a bright, startled smile unlike anything she'd seen since December. He crossed the corridor and caught her against him, and she kissed him before he could begin another sentence. His arms came around her. For a moment the narrow corridor and the people passing through it ceased to matter.

When they separated he was laughing.

"You didn't tell me."

"I wanted to hear you without having to watch you worry that I was watching."

"That's a very complicated plan."

"It worked."

He looked at her for a long moment.

"You came all the way here?"

"By van."

"That's not what I meant."

She smiled. "I wanted to."

He kissed her again, less startled now and more deliberate. She felt his familiar warmth beneath the concert shirt, the quick beat of his heart, and the relief of finding the real person behind the letters she'd spent the month reading.

Someone cleared a throat behind them.

Mara stood beside the corridor door, violin case under one arm, looking thoroughly entertained.

"Calder. Are you going to introduce us?"

Rhys turned.

"Thalia, this is Mara. Mara, Thalia."

Mara looked at Thalia's face, then at Rhys.

"Ah. The archer."

Thalia narrowed her eyes. "What has he told you?"

"Mostly that you would correct his stance if you saw it."

"Accurate."

Mara laughed. "He has a performance review waiting, then."

"I enjoyed the concert."

Rhys looked faintly suspicious of how readily she admitted it.

Mara saw the expression and smiled.

"Good. He's been working hard."

"So I've heard."

The exchange lasted only a minute, but Thalia liked the way it felt. Mara wasn't a threat or an obstacle. She was one of the people who had been teaching Rhys things Thalia couldn't, part of a life he had begun building beyond camp. Thalia could meet her, like her and remain glad he was here.

Rhys took Thalia's bag after they left the corridor.

"Are you staying?"

"Tonight. Eleni has tomorrow's lesson."

He looked at her. "You arranged a day off?"

"Don't sound shocked."

"I'm not. I'm delighted."

"That's worse."

He laughed.

They walked toward the market square. The afternoon had turned warm, and the river carried sunlight beneath the bridges. Rhys had a room in the ensemble's lodging, but Thalia had already booked a small guest room nearby. She told him so as they crossed the square.

He looked at her.

"You planned everything."

"I planned enough."

"And you didn't tell me."

"I wanted it to be a surprise."

He stopped beneath the awning of a shop.

"Was this about making sure I was doing all right?"

Thalia looked at him. The question was gentle, but it touched the fear she'd been trying to avoid since she bought the ticket. He'd spent weeks proving he could work away from camp. She didn't want her visit to make him feel inspected.

"No," she said. "I knew you were all right. I wanted to hear you play. And I wanted to see you afterward."

He studied her face.

She added, "I missed you."

He smiled. The doubt vanished.

"Then I'm glad you came."

She caught his hand and began walking again.

***

The guest room was smaller than Rhys's cabin and considerably more expensive.

It had a narrow bed, a polished dresser and curtains that would not close properly no matter how carefully Thalia adjusted them. The wallpaper was a faded pattern of flowers that seemed to have survived three decades of people disliking it. The room smelled of soap and the faint scent of the bakery below.

Rhys looked around.

"You booked this?"

"The other place was worse."

"How?"

"The curtains were orange."

He laughed.

She put down the bag and went to the window. From there she could see the market square and the river beyond it. She had never pictured this room while missing him. She had imagined the north cabin, the guitar by the bench, the stove and the familiar uneven floor. Now he was here in a strange place, and she was surprised by how quickly the desire to be close returned.

He came up behind her.

"Was the concert really good?" he asked.

She turned.

"Yes."

"You're not just saying that because you traveled?"

"Your opening was slightly late. The second movement was excellent. The last chord worked better than it did at camp."

He looked delighted.

"That's more like you."

"You asked for a review."

"I did."

She rested her hands against his shirtfront.

"And I liked watching you."

His smile changed. He touched her cheek, then kissed her. The first kiss was gentle and almost uncertain in the unfamiliar room; the second was not. She caught him closer, feeling the long weeks apart in the immediacy of his response. He moved with her away from the window, both laughing when the edge of the dresser blocked their path.

"Terrible room," she murmured.

"Very."

"Bad layout."

"Unacceptable."

She kissed him again.

There was something different about the desire here. At camp the cabin had held their habits and small agreements; the room knew nothing about them. Nobody had left gloves in a drawer or drawn notes on a slate. They had to bring their own familiar gestures into it, and the discovery that those gestures still worked made Thalia smile.

Rhys caught her face between his hands. She leaned into him, then drew him toward the bed, careful of the bag she had left beside it. He followed with an eagerness that made her laugh against his mouth. They had both spent weeks writing about wanting this. They didn't need to discuss it now.

The curtains remained crooked.

They stayed together until the square outside went quiet and the room grew dark.

***

Later, Rhys was sitting on the floor with a plate of bread and cheese he'd collected from the bakery before it closed.

Thalia lay against the pillows, wearing his shirt because she'd decided hers was less comfortable. She watched him inspect the cheese as though he expected an accusation of poor workmanship.

"Good?"

"Acceptable."

"A rare compliment."

"Don't get used to it."

He handed her half the bread. She accepted it, then shifted so he could sit beside her.

They ate in comfortable silence for a while. The sounds from the market square had faded. Somewhere below, the baker was closing shutters. The room smelled of bread and soap, and the window had become a dark reflection.

"Are you happy?" Thalia asked.

Rhys looked at her.

"Here?"

"On the tour."

He thought for a moment. "Yes. More often than not."

"What's the worst part?"

"Being wrong in front of people who have been doing this for years. Sometimes I feel as if I'm back on the novice line."

"Good."

He laughed. "You'd say that."

"What's the best part?"

"When a piece finally works. When I can hear something the others are making that I couldn't have made alone."

She nodded. That was the part she'd heard during the concert. She was glad he had found it.

He turned toward her.

"What about you?"

She told him about Eleni and the advanced program, the mistake she'd made when the mail was late, and the apology she'd offered her students. He listened without attempting to rescue her from the embarrassment of admitting she'd been unfair.

When she finished, he squeezed her hand.

"You're becoming a good instructor."

"I was already a good instructor."

"You're becoming a better one."

She considered. The correction was accurate.

"Fine."

He smiled.

They lay together for a while, neither sleepy enough to turn out the lamp.

Then Rhys asked, "Will it feel worse when you leave tomorrow?"

Thalia considered. She had been wondering the same thing.

"Probably."

"Then are you glad you came?"

She looked at him. She could have spent the weekend at camp and spared herself a second goodbye. The letters would have continued, the weeks would have passed, and the tour would eventually have ended. But she would not have heard him play, would not have met Mara, would not have learned that the man she loved was capable of feeling at home in a place she'd never seen.

She touched his face.

"Yes," she said.

He kissed her gently, and she drew him closer, wanting one more quiet hour before the morning arrived.

The second goodbye was difficult.

It was also worth having.

---

## April 27: A Program with Two Names

The trouble began with a printed schedule.

Thalia had written the original by hand. Chiron preferred that important changes be typed and pinned in several places, because he had learned that even a very good plan was useless if the people following it couldn't tell which page was current. Someone had finally copied Thalia's spring archery program into a clean format.

The title looked good. So did the dates.

Below it, under Instructor, were two names.

Thalia Grace and Eleni Markos.

Thalia stared at the page.

She had known Eleni would be taking more responsibility. She'd trained the younger woman, watched her handle the students with improving confidence and deliberately let her change lessons that needed changing. The plan had become better because she had stopped treating every decision as proof of her own competence.

It had not occurred to her that her name would have to share the line.

She was still looking at the schedule when Chiron came into the room.

"Is something wrong?"

"No."

He looked at the paper, then at her face.

"That's rarely an encouraging answer."

"Eleni's name is on my program."

"She's teaching half the sessions."

"I know."

"Then her name belongs on the schedule."

Thalia opened her mouth and found she couldn't argue with that. She'd made the decision herself. She'd wanted Eleni to have real responsibility rather than perform a few delegated tasks under constant supervision. She had meant it.

She had not anticipated the strange, sharp feeling of seeing that choice made official.

Chiron went on gently. "If you want a program that can last, it will need more than one person capable of running it."

"I know."

"Knowing and enjoying are different."

Thalia folded the schedule.

"I'll talk to her."

"About what?"

She looked at him. It was an excellent question.

"About the next lesson," she said.

Chiron nodded and left the room.

Thalia remained by the table, feeling more exposed than she'd expected. She had begun the program because she believed the advanced students were not being taught to make enough decisions for themselves. She'd fought for equipment, revised training rules and defended the difficulty of the work when other instructors wanted a simpler competition. She was proud of it. She wanted that pride to remain hers, even while the program became something the camp could maintain without her.

That was the problem.

If the program succeeded, it would stop belonging exclusively to her.

***

Eleni arrived carrying two quivers and a notebook with three pages of observations from the previous session.

"Have you seen the schedule?" she asked.

"Yes."

"They put my name on it."

Thalia looked at her.

Eleni seemed almost embarrassed. "I thought Chiron was going to keep mine under the assistant column. I didn't ask him to change it."

The words were careful. She was trying to reassure Thalia that she hadn't reached for something that belonged to someone else.

Thalia felt a small surge of shame.

"I know you didn't."

"I can ask him to change it."

"Don't."

Eleni stopped.

Thalia picked up the schedule and laid it flat on the table. The two names were printed in the same size. No one had been made smaller. It was ridiculous that she'd needed to look at the page twice to see it.

"You've earned your place there," she said.

Eleni's expression changed.

"You mean that?"

"Yes."

"Then why did you look as though you wanted to eat the schedule?"

"An unfortunate facial habit."

Eleni laughed.

They spent the next hour preparing the lesson. Eleni had developed a variation on the moving-target exercise that required students to work in pairs and agree on a safe sequence before either could take a shot. Thalia thought the exercise was too elaborate. Eleni argued that the whole purpose was to make the students speak honestly when they disagreed, not to make them faster.

Thalia listened.

"Fine," she said at last. "We'll try it."

Eleni grinned. "You said that quickly."

"Don't celebrate. We haven't seen the results."

The results were mixed.

Some pairs worked beautifully. Others spent more time arguing over who would take the first shot than examining the course. One student refused to change his plan after his partner identified a safety problem. Eleni stopped the exercise and made him explain why he believed his choice was safer. He couldn't. The discussion that followed was uncomfortable, but the student eventually admitted he'd treated disagreement as a challenge rather than useful information.

Thalia watched from the side.

She did not need to intervene.

That was almost the entire point.

Afterward Eleni came over, looking tired and pleased.

"Well?"

"The third pair needs another session on communication," Thalia said.

"I know."

"The first pair could use a harder lane."

"I thought so too."

"The second target is too close to the fence."

Eleni nodded. "I'll move it."

Thalia considered.

"Good lesson," she said.

Eleni smiled.

They carried the equipment back together, and Thalia realized she'd stopped thinking about the printed schedule. It was easier to share credit when she could see what the other person had done to earn it.

At lunch she told Annabeth.

Her sister considered the arrangement.

"You've been telling me for months that you want people to make their own decisions."

"I know."

"Then why is it surprising that one of them is good at it?"

Thalia gave her a weary look.

"I preferred you when you were small and impressionable."

"I was never impressionable."

"An unfortunate fact."

Annabeth laughed and returned to her drawing.

***

That evening Thalia received a letter from Rhys.

He described the concert she'd attended, including the moment when he looked up and discovered her in the third row. He admitted that he'd nearly missed an entrance because he was so surprised to see her. She smiled at that. He also wrote about the morning after she left, how the room had seemed strangely ordinary again once the extra coat was gone and the little guest-house bag had disappeared from beside the bed.

*I don't want you to feel you have to travel every time I miss you,* he wrote. *I was happier than I knew what to do with when I saw you. I also liked that you had your own reasons for coming. You heard the concert. You met Mara. You came because you wanted the day, not because I needed rescuing.*

Thalia read the paragraph twice.

She had spent the afternoon discovering that she could share a program without losing it. Now Rhys was writing about how much he had liked her showing up at a concert without treating his life as something she'd come to supervise.

She took out a fresh page.

*Rhys,*

*Chiron put Eleni's name beside mine on the spring schedule. I was furious for approximately four minutes, then realized I had trained her well enough to deserve it. She's running half the lessons now. The students are improving. I am trying to be pleased about the part where none of them needs to wait for me to approve every good idea.*

She added a line about the moving-target exercise and the boy who'd refused to listen to his partner, then paused.

*I liked seeing you in the theater. Not because you were playing for me. You weren't. I liked watching you do something that mattered to you and then having you choose to spend the evening with me afterward. Those two parts belonged together.*

She sat still a moment after writing it. The thought felt important, though she couldn't have explained precisely why. Perhaps because she had spent so much of her life believing that being needed was the surest form of belonging. Rhys had made her feel wanted without depending on her to keep his world in order.

She finished with a joke about the curtains in the guest room and a warning that if he ever booked the place for her again, she would bring her own tools to repair them.

At the bottom she wrote:

*I still miss kissing you. But you already know that.*

Then she signed it.

The next morning she went to the range and found Eleni waiting beside the printed schedule.

"Ready?" her assistant asked.

Thalia looked at the two names.

"Yes," she said.

They began.

---

## May 3: The Part Nobody Claimed

The problem with the final town was that nobody had written down who owned the last arrangement.

Rhys discovered this at rehearsal when Edda placed a new score on the music stand and announced that they would play it at the closing concert. The piece was short, bright and full of sudden changes in tempo. It contained several passages that looked as though they had been written by different people, which turned out to be true.

Mara had contributed the opening violin line. The cellist had changed the middle section during the previous week's rehearsals. Edda had rewritten the ending. Rhys had been asked to add a guitar part after the others were finished, though the other instruments had already begun treating his notes as if they were a fixed part of the arrangement.

At the bottom of the page, under Composer, there was a blank space.

"Who's credited?" Rhys asked.

Edda looked up.

"The ensemble."

"That's not a person."

"It's a collaboration."

"Then there should be names."

A few musicians glanced over. Rhys felt the sudden discomfort of speaking into a room where everyone else had already decided the matter was too small to discuss. He nearly let it go. He had been the newest member for six weeks, and he was still trying to earn the right to object without sounding defensive.

Then he thought of Thalia looking at the printed schedule and finding Eleni's name beside her own.

He had liked her for accepting it, though he knew the acceptance hadn't been easy. Here was the same question wearing different clothes: if several people had made something good, could they let the result belong to more than one person?

Edda put down her pencil.

"What do you propose?"

"List everyone who wrote a part."

"That's a long list."

"It's a short score."

Mara laughed. Edda's expression did not change.

"The program is already printed," she said.

"Then announce it."

There was a silence. Rhys felt heat rising into his face. He could hear himself sounding like an advocate for his own credit, though he'd been given the smallest role in the arrangement. He wanted to explain that the problem was not his name. Then he realized that explaining might only make it sound more self-serving.

Edda studied him.

"All right," she said at last. "We'll announce the contributors."

Rhys blinked.

She turned back to the music.

"Now please stop arguing and play the second entrance on time."

The room laughed. Rhys smiled despite himself and took his place.

The rehearsal was difficult, partly because the piece was unfamiliar and partly because the players were now paying closer attention to who had changed what. The opening was too fast. The cello part needed an extra bar. His guitar line sounded beautiful during the rehearsal break and disappeared beneath the strings when the whole ensemble played together.

He had to rewrite it.

That was harder than it should have been. He had spent the previous night working out the phrase, and the notes looked elegant on the page. He liked the progression. He liked the particular shape of the final chord. He disliked hearing the conductor say that none of it could be heard from the back of the room.

Mara came to stand beside him.

"You're staring at it."

"I'm thinking."

"You're sulking."

"An uncharitable interpretation."

She pointed at the score. "What if you come in after the violin instead of with it?"

"Then there's a gap."

"Yes."

"The gap will be audible."

"Yes."

He considered. The idea was irritatingly good.

"You sound like somebody I know."

"The archer again?"

"Yes."

"Then perhaps you should stop talking about her and try the suggestion."

He laughed, struck through two measures and began again.

The revised line worked. It was simpler, less clever and better suited to the room. When Edda heard it, she nodded once and told the ensemble to move on.

Rhys felt an unexpected pleasure at letting go of the version he'd wanted to keep.

***

After rehearsal, Mara joined him on the back steps with two cups of tea.

"Last week," she said.

"Apparently."

"You going back to camp?"

"Yes."

"Not staying on the road?"

"Not after this engagement."

She looked at him, considering. "You'll be missed."

He smiled. "Thank you."

"That's not an invitation to become sentimental."

"I wouldn't dare."

She handed him a cup. The tea was terrible. He drank it anyway.

"Do you think you'll come back?" she asked.

"To the group?"

"To this kind of work."

Rhys looked toward the rehearsal hall, where someone was playing the difficult transition again and making a different mistake each time. He had been exhausted for weeks. His fingers hurt by the end of most days, and he had learned more about bad acoustics than he ever wanted to know. He had also begun to understand why some musicians kept returning to the road despite the inconvenience.

"I think so," he said.

"When?"

"I don't know."

Mara nodded. She didn't ask for a promise.

They sat for a while watching people pass along the street. Rhys thought about his cabin, the familiar workbench and the narrow path through the pines. He missed those things. He also thought about Thalia and the way she'd looked in the third row of the theater, smiling before he even knew she was there. The memory remained sharper than the applause.

"I want to write," he said.

Mara looked at him.

"More music. Not just parts for this group. Things of my own."

"Then write."

"I don't know whether I can make enough of it."

"Nobody does."

He laughed. That was a more honest answer than a reassurance.

She stood to go back inside.

"Bring us something next time," she said.

"I might."

"Make it difficult."

He smiled.

When she left, Rhys took out the notebook Thalia had given him and began writing a melody that didn't belong to the closing concert. He had been hearing it for several days, a quiet phrase that began almost too low to notice and then rose without becoming loud. He wrote the first bars slowly, leaving room beneath them for another line.

He didn't know what the second line would be.

That was all right.

***

That night he wrote to Thalia.

He told her about the argument over the arrangement's credit, and how Edda had accepted the point before telling him to stop behaving like a lawyer. He told her about Mara's suggestion and admitted it had improved the music. He described the new melody, though he refused to include the notes until he could send something worth playing.

Then he came to the part he had been avoiding.

*The tour ends next week. I'm ready to come home. I also know I'll miss this work. I don't want you to think I'm choosing one life instead of the other, because I don't know yet how they fit together.*

He read it over.

*I want to find out. I want you in the answer, but I don't expect you to do the arranging for me.*

He thought of their arguments over the winter: the photograph he'd displayed without asking, the evenings he had been too tired for company, the times she'd arrived wanting him to be available simply because she'd finally found time to visit. They had been learning how to keep affection from turning into obligation. He hoped that skill would be useful now that the future contained more than one possibility.

He added a short postscript.

*I have become very good at hearing your voice when somebody tells me I'm wrong. It's appalling. Sometimes you're right even when you aren't here.*

He signed the letter and put it with the outgoing post.

At the window the town was quiet. The ensemble would have another rehearsal in the morning, then the closing concert, then a final round of packing and goodbyes. He wanted to do all of it well. He also wanted to go home and kiss Thalia without having to save the desire for a page.

He put the notebook away.

There would be time for both. Not always on the same day. Not always easily. But he had stopped believing that wanting more than one thing was evidence that he'd chosen badly.

He went to bed thinking about the unfinished melody, and in the morning the second line finally began to appear.

---

## May 10: The Last Empty Chair

The final rehearsal ended without a speech.

Edda simply lowered her pencil, told the ensemble the last movement was ready and dismissed everyone to prepare for the evening. The musicians looked around at one another with the disbelief of people who had been corrected so many times that a finished piece seemed faintly suspicious.

Rhys remained in his chair after the others stood.

The room was familiar now. He knew which floorboard creaked beside the door, where the light caught the back of Mara's music stand, how long the conductor could remain silent before everyone understood they were supposed to begin again. He had spent six weeks learning those details, and the knowledge had become a kind of belonging.

Tomorrow they would perform one last time.

After that he would pack.

He had expected the prospect to feel like relief. It did, partly. His fingers were tired, his clothes needed washing and he wanted a bed that did not move every time another guest walked along the corridor. He also felt an unexpected reluctance to leave the rehearsal room, as though the chairs and stands might forget the work if nobody remained to remember it.

Mara came back for her violin.

"You're still here."

"Very observant."

"Are you composing a farewell speech?"

"Certainly not."

"Good. The cellist has already written one. It's dreadful."

Rhys laughed and began packing the guitar.

Mara watched him for a moment.

"What are you going to do when you get home?"

"Sleep."

"After that."

"Teach. Finish a piece I've been working on."

"Send it to Edda."

He looked up. "It's not ready."

"Then finish it."

"You're giving me homework."

"Someone has to."

He smiled. There was something reassuring about how little ceremony the last day required. He did not need to make a speech about belonging. He could leave knowing he'd been useful, that he'd made mistakes, improved and earned the respect of people whose work he admired.

Before she went, Mara handed him a folded page.

"What's this?"

"Addresses. Mine and the others. If you want to work with us again, don't vanish into the woods for six months without telling anybody what you're writing."

He looked at the page. It contained several names, each with a place where letters could be delivered. There was room at the bottom for his.

He wrote it down.

"Thank you," he said.

"Don't become sentimental."

He laughed. "Too late."

***

The closing concert was in the largest hall they'd used.

Rows of seats rose toward a balcony, and the stage was deep enough that the musicians could move without bumping into one another. The acoustics were better than those of the first theater, but the larger room carried sound farther, making small mistakes more audible.

Rhys was nervous again.

This time the nerves did not surprise him.

He knew he could play the program. He'd rehearsed it until the entries lived in his hands, and he knew exactly where to listen for the other instruments. He still felt the quick, uneasy anticipation as the audience took their seats.

That was part of performing. He didn't need to be cured of it.

When the musicians entered, he looked briefly toward the third row. He knew Thalia wasn't there. She had written that the advanced program needed her for its closing spring evaluation, and she'd asked him to send an account of the concert rather than trying to travel again. He was glad she had chosen the work she cared about.

He still imagined her in the audience for one absurd second, eyebrow raised, waiting to catch a mistake.

He smiled and took his place.

The first half went well.

During the interval, Edda gathered the ensemble and said that the closing piece had been altered slightly. She wanted the final refrain played without the extra doubling they'd been using, leaving the main melody exposed. Several musicians looked alarmed. The change was small on paper but risky in the room.

Rhys considered the score.

The opening would be quieter. The ending would feel less triumphant. The audience might not even realize something had changed.

He liked it.

They returned to the stage.

The closing piece began with the violin carrying a line Mara had written months earlier. The cello entered beneath it, steady and dark. Rhys waited for his place, listening to the room rather than counting every second as though the music were an examination. The gap opened. He came in.

This time he did not rush.

The melody grew, folded back and rose again. The new ending left the final note almost alone. There was a heartbeat when Rhys wondered whether the sound would be too thin to fill the hall.

Then it held.

The silence afterward was longer than he'd expected. He could feel the audience listening as the note faded. Nobody moved. Nobody coughed. The room seemed to remain suspended for one impossible second.

Then the applause began.

Rhys looked across at the other musicians. Mara was grinning openly. The cellist looked as though she might cry. Edda gave one small nod before they stood together to bow.

He had never felt so proud of a room full of people.

***

At supper they made speeches after all.

The cellist read hers. It was terrible. Everybody applauded. Edda said something brief about learning to listen and work together, which made Mara roll her eyes because the conductor had spent much of the tour shouting exactly that. The older musicians told stories about the first performances, including the night a violin stand collapsed in the middle of a quiet passage and nobody admitted who had forgotten to tighten it.

Rhys found himself laughing hard enough to forget he was leaving.

Later he went back to the lodging, took out Thalia's photograph and sat at the desk.

He had been practicing what he would say to her about the tour. A tidy account seemed impossible now. There had been too many small changes, too many moments of doubt followed by improvement, too many people he'd come to care about without expecting to.

He began the letter anyway.

*Grace,*

*We finished. The last concert was good. No disaster, no heroic recovery, only music that sounded the way we'd been trying to make it sound. I was proud of everyone.*

He stopped. The words looked plain. They were the right words.

*I missed you tonight. I was glad you were doing your program instead of sitting here to reassure me. Both feelings fit.*

He considered the next sentence.

*I want to come home. I also know I want to do this again someday. I don't have a plan beyond finishing some music and talking to you about what happens next. That ought to be enough planning for one evening.*

He signed the letter, put it in an envelope and tucked the copy of the new score beside it. Thalia would read the notes and find three mistakes he hadn't noticed. He hoped she'd tell him about all of them.

Then he lay down and let the exhaustion reach him.

He was going home. Not because the tour had failed to give him something, but because it had given him enough to bring back.

---
